Power Lane County Electric Grid Handbook
A comprehensive assessment of Lane County’s electric system

July 2026

Edited and assembled by: Ali Trueworthy, Ian Hermanson, Brooke Marten, and Michael
Barthelmes, Pacific Northwest National Laboratory

Authorship listed by Chapter

Suggested Citation: A. Trueworthy, I. Hermanson, B. Marten, H. Ross, A. Krelling, C.
Briggs, T. Naoshin, A. Rackley, E. Estreich, K. Sun, M. Barthelmes. Power Lane County
Electric Grid Handbook. June 2026

Prepared for
The staff of Lane County, Oregon and the members of the public whom they serve. The
information provided herein is accurate to the best information available at the time of
writing. However, much of the data and practices that we discuss will change with time.

Pacific Northwest National Laboratory
Richland, Washington 99354

 ii

In 2024, Lane County, Oregon, was selected to participate in the U.S. Department of Energy’s
(DOE’s) Energy to Community (E2C) program. Through the program, national laboratory
researchers from Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL), Pacific Northwest National
Laboratory (PNNL), and the National Laboratory of the Rockies (NLR) provide three years of
technical assistance to Lane County and their project partners: the Center for Rural Livelihoods,
Springfield Utility Board, and Emerald People’s Utility District. The project, named Power Lane
County, aims to improve the local electric system’s affordability, reliability, and resilience in the
face of natural hazards and potentially rising loads.

The Power Lane County Electric Grid Handbook establishes a foundational understanding of
the electric system in Lane County to inform community engagement, education, and analysis in
future project phases. This Handbook synthesizes public data and utility and stakeholder
engagement, including utility interviews. It consists of three parts subdivided into nine total
chapters and concludes with a review of identified Power Lane County project opportunities.

Part I: Foundations introduces the fundamental technologies, organizations, policies, history,
and conditions that shape the modern U.S. electricity system. This information supports
informed participation in energy decision-making. Part I has two chapters. Chapter 1: How
Does the Lane County Electrical System Work? differentiates between energy and electricity
before providing a foundation of how Lane County’s electric system works, how it evolved, and
who manages it today. Lane County’s utilities participate in a complicated and dynamic system
of electricity generation, transmission, and exchange involving entities like federal regulators,
independent power producers, the Bonneville Power Administration (BPA), regional power
markets, and end-users. Nine of the 10 electric service providers in Lane County are consumer-
owned utilities that buy most of their electricity from BPA, the federal entity that markets and
transmits power from the Pacific Northwest’s system of federally owned hydropower dams.
Chapter 2: What Policies Impact Lane County’s Grid? reviews state and federal policies that
impact grid management, development, energy efficiency and conservation efforts, and
environment and resilience efforts related to energy.

Part II: Lane County Utilities reviews the external factors that impact Lane County’s local
electric utilities and the actions utilities can take to manage those impacts. Changes in the
regional electricity system present challenges to Lane County utilities and can impact end-
users. Utilities must navigate these challenges while fulfilling two key responsibilities: balancing
electric load and generation, and balancing costs and revenues. Part II has four chapters.
Chapter 3: Regional Dynamics Impacting Lane County Utilities examines the impacts of
Western power market restructuring, resource adequacy, operational reliability, and rising costs
on Lane County utilities. Despite these changes, utilities are expected to meet evolving
customer and stakeholder expectations, including providing affordable service, serving changing
electric loads, promoting efficient energy consumption, and providing reliable and resilient
service. Chapter 4: Expectations of Lane County Utilities describes best practices for
meeting those expectations, examining which of those practices are being implemented by
utilities in Lane County, and where there are opportunities for improvement. Opportunities exist
in the areas of rate design, demand management, and the valuation of resilience benefits. Lane
County utilities are exposed to significant natural hazards that are increasing in both severity
and frequency in the Pacific Northwest. Chapter 5: Utility Natural Hazard Mitigation and
Preparedness summarizes Lane County’s exposure to wildfire, earthquake, storm, and security
hazards. This section includes best practices for utilities to mitigate risk from each of these

 iii

hazards. Opportunities for improvement emerge in the areas of risk modeling and management,
wildfire mitigation, and wildfire hazard characterization. Chapter 6: Available Technologies for
Grid Modernization presents a descriptive library of technologies available to utilities to
improve distribution grid reliability and resilience. Technologies are divided into four groups:
operations and hardening, visibility and monitoring, remote control and automation, and
integrated management platforms.

Part III: Community Energy Baseline examines Lane County’s electricity- and resilience-
related community resources, vulnerabilities, and goals, and identifies local factors that impact
energy decision-making for utilities, investors, and local governments. It has three chapters.
Chapter 7: Local Electricity Generation Resource Availability maps the availability of solar,
wind, and biomass electricity generation across Lane County. NLR’s Annual Technology
Baseline is used to estimate cost and performance for each energy technology in Lane County.
As wind and solar technologies mature, they will become more economically competitive with
current, low-cost Pacific Northwest power sources. Choosing where to invest in energy reliability
and resilience projects and strategies is a challenge for Lane County planners, utilities, and
partners. Chapter 8: Geospatial Data to Inform Project Prioritization maps socio-economic
metrics and critical facility locations across Lane County to help decision makers identify where
investment is most needed. Lane County cities and communities undertake energy and
resilience planning to strategize community projects, policies, and coordinated actions. Chapter
9: Community Energy and Resilience Planning reviews available resilience and energy plans
from Lane County municipalities to inform planning future coordination. By evaluating current
practices against best practices, we identified key gaps and opportunities that could inform
future projects that advance the Power Lane County goals.

The final chapter, Opportunities for Improving Lane County Electric System Affordability
Reliability, and Resilience consolidates the various opportunities for improvement discussed
throughout the Handbook. Opportunities are organized across five categories: utility studies and
programs; collaborative evaluation, planning, and framework development; city efforts; public
accessibility, education, and outreach; and countywide modeling.

The following Key Findings Summary synthesizes important electricity system takeaways from
this document into a concise list.

 iv

 Key Findings Summary
• Lane County is served by a high density of mostly small publicly owned
 utilities, which creates a patchwork of service areas, utility policies,
 local oversight and programs. This creates challenges for coordination and
 planning.

• For Oregon publicly owned utilities that rely on low-cost BPA hydropower, rising
 customer electricity rates have largely tracked inflation and are among the lowest in
 the nation. Customers of Oregon investor-owned utilities have seen larger rate
 increases. Pressure on rates is expected to rise.

• Policy makers are increasingly concerned about the electricity cost and reliability
 impacts of projected demand growth from data centers and electrification of homes
 and vehicles. Small public utilities tend to rely on BPA to predict future demand,
 however BPA’s models are based on historic demand data, and do not always
 capture significant technology-driven changes. This leaves utilities and policy makers
 uncertain about future costs and risks. There is a need for more robust and data-
 driven methods of projecting future loads.

• Extreme weather, increased wildfire risk, and other hazards stress the Pacific
 Northwest’s aging electric infrastructure and require utility coordination and
 investment. These investments could increase customer rates but would promote
 resilience.

• Some areas of Lane County’s electric grid are more reliable than others.
 Metropolitan Eugene/Springfield customers are served by multiple transmission lines
 and a redundant distribution network. Some rural areas, however, are served by a
 single transmission line and a more vulnerable distribution network.

• High temperatures and low regional snowpack can decrease the water available for
 power generation in the PNW’s hydropower- based electricity system.

• Lane County could look to local solar or wind energy generation to improve future
 resilience, however the limited renewable energy potential of those natural resources
 within the county means that the cost of electricity from them will be high compared
 to the current low cost of BPA hydropower. This makes it difficult for utilities to invest
 in local generation for resilience while keeping rates low.

• When new, large commercial or industrial users want to connect to the grid,
 sometimes both distribution utilities (Lane County’s retail utilities that sell electricity to
 consumers like homes and businesses) and transmission utilities (BPA and Pacific
 Power, which both own transmission infrastructure in Lane County) need to conduct
 studies to make sure that the grid can serve those loads. If upgrades or new
 infrastructure are necessary, the utility determines whether the new large customer is
 financially responsible for the needed infrastructure. This can add a challenge to
 economic development, but it can also serve to ensure that existing customers do
 not bear the burdens of increased costs to serve new loads.

 v

 Key Findings Summary (continued)
• Powering some, but not all, loads in emergency or shortage situations can be difficult
 because utilities cannot control demand. The ability to isolate portions of the grid can
 improve resilience, for example, by providing continued service to critical facilities
 during shortages or by de-energizing a portion of the grid in a high wildfire risk area.
 Currently, there is room for improvement in knowledge of the electricity needs of
 critical facilities and the risks to the electric system, and the capacity of utilities to use
 this knowledge to isolate portions of the grid.

• Currently, none of the generation resources in Lane County have blackstart
 capabilities. This means that, in the event of a regional grid outage, none of the
 generation resources in Lane County would be able to generate usable electricity
 and deliver that electricity to end users. Because so much of Lane County’s
 electricity comes from outside the county, in the event of a regional outage, the
 generation sources within Lane  KeyCounty
 Findings  Summary
 could not come close to meeting the demand
 within the county.

• Demand response programs and distributed energy resources are ways that utilities
 can reduce peak loads. Reducing peak loads is important in circumstances when the
 costs of meeting peak loads are high or when individual grid components adjacent to
 peak loads are nearing their operational capacity limits. For Lane County utilities, the
 marginal cost of meeting peak demand is often manageable due to the terms of their
 contracts with BPA. Thus far the benefits of most types of demand response
 programs and distributed energy resources have not been seen by most of Lane
 County's utilities as outweighing the costs, however, interest may be emerging.

• Enhanced grid resilience can be supported by a portfolio of technology solutions that
 strengthen physical assets, improve operational flexibility, and build the capacity
 to anticipate and respond to disruptions. Utilities across Lane County are at varying
 stages of modernization, depending on their size, resources, and regional risks.

 vi

 Acknowledgments

 vii

Lane County, Oregon spans from Oregon's rugged coast to the foothills of the Cascade
Mountains, with boundaries crossing the Oregon Coast Range and the Willamette Valley.
Centered in Lane County is one of Oregon's most populous metropolitan areas, the Cities of
Eugene and Springfield. The city is home to University of Oregon and sits at the confluence of
the McKenzie and Willamette Rivers, which supply drinking water, recreation opportunities,
and—as we will discuss—electricity from hydropower dams.

 Figure 1: Map of Lane County, Oregon 1

In 2024 Lane County was selected to participate in the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE)
Energy to Communities (E2C) program. The program supports Lane County and their project
partners (the Center for Rural Livelihoods, Springfield Utility Board, and Emerald People’s Utility
District) with three years of technical assistance from DOE national laboratory researchers,
namely, Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL), Pacific Northwest National Laboratory
(PNNL), and the National Laboratory of the Rockies (NLR). Lane County is engaging all electric
utilities that serve in Lane County (not just project partners), as well as other stakeholders. Lane
County and their partners are focused on improving affordability, grid reliability in the face of
potentially increasing electric loads, and grid resilience to support community resilience,
especially during extreme weather events. These goals are further articulated in Table 1.

1 Data from GEBCO. “General Bathymetric Chart of the Oceans,” 2022. https://www.gebco.net/data-

products/gridded-bathymetry-data/gebco-2022

 viii

 Table 1: Lane County’s Electricity System Goals
 Power Lane County Goals
 People in Lane County should be able to afford their electricity bills while Lane
  Affordability County utilities should be able to conduct business in such a way that ensures the
 financial stability and longevity of their operations.
 Lane County electric services should be consistently available, and customers
 Reliability
 should face minimal interruptions.
 Lane County electricity services should be able to withstand and quickly recover
 Resilience
 from disruptions such as those caused by storms, failures, or other hazards.

According to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC), grid reliability is “the
provision of adequate, secure, and stable flow of electricity as consumers may need it.” 1 A
reliable grid is one that always—or almost always—works. When an end user flips a switch, the
lights come on.

Grid resilience is the ability of the electric grid to respond to and recover from a wide variety of
hazards. Hazards might include natural hazards (see Sections 5.2–5.5), security hazards (see
Section 5.6), or routine hazards such as workforce insufficiencies, changes in customer
expectations or behavior, supply chain disruptions, and similar events.

Electric grid resilience can be thought of as a subset of energy resilience, as energy resilience
covers the fuels systems as well. This Handbook discusses grid resilience and the ways that the
electric system supports other types of community resilience. For instance, the cost of electricity
can impact a community’s economic resilience, or the efforts that a utility takes to prevent utility-
caused wildfires (see Section 5.2) can improve the natural hazard resilience of the community.

Over the course of the three-year E2C project, leaders in Lane County will work with
researchers at national laboratories to explore opportunities for distributed energy resources,
grid upgrades, grid management strategies, and natural hazard mitigation. The project aims to
improve the reliability and affordability of energy, the resiliency of existing infrastructure,
community resilience during electric outages, and the overall levels of community engagement
and collaboration on energy issues across the county (Figure 2).

1 Federal Energy Regulatory Commission. “Reliability Explainer,” Aug. 2023.

https://www.ferc.gov/reliability-explainer

 ix

Figure 2: Aims of Power Lane County Project

 x

The Power Lane County Project has three planned phases: a baselining phase to help leaders
and researchers understand the needs and priorities of Lane County’s electric system; an
analysis phase to help inform current and future decision making; and an implementation
planning phase to help Lane County move forward when the project concludes. This Handbook
is the product of the first Phase of Power Lane County.

This Handbook establishes a foundational understanding of the energy landscape and electric
grid in Lane County to inform community engagement, education, and analysis in future phases
of Power Lane County. The information provided herein was gathered through publicly available
data sources and utility and stakeholder engagement, including interviews with utility
representatives detailed in Appendix A.

Following this brief Introduction and Purpose, this document contains nine chapters, divided
into three parts.
 Part I: Foundations
  – Chapter 1: How Does the Lane County Electrical System Work? introduces Lane
 County’s electric energy system, how it evolved, and who keeps it working today.
  – Chapter 2: What Policies Impact Lane County’s Grid? reviews the policies that govern
 the grid, and the opportunities that Lane County and the local utilities have within the
 structures of state energy policy and strategy.
 Part II: Lane County Utilities
  – Chapter 3: Regional Dynamics Impacting Lane County Utilities discusses the ways
 that events on the regional grid impact Lane County’s utilities.
  – Chapter 4: Expectations of Lane County Utilities describes the main operational
 challenges facing Lane County electric utilities, the best practices for utilities dealing with
 those challenges, and Lane County utilities’ adoption of best practices.
  – Chapter 5: Utility Hazard Mitigation and Preparedness describes the main hazard-
 related challenges facing Lane County electric utilities, the best practices for utilities
 dealing with those challenges, and Lane County utilities’ adoption of best practices.
  – Chapter 6: Available Technologies for Lane County Utilities presents a suite of
 technologies available to utilities for improving reliability and resilience.
 Part III: Community Energy Baseline
  – Chapter 7: Local Energy Generation Resource Availability outlines available electricity
 generation within Lane County.
  – Chapter 8: Geospatial Data to Inform Project Prioritization presents data on local
 vulnerabilities that could help decision makers prioritize reliability or resilience projects or
 programs.
  – Chapter 9: Community Energy and Resilience Planning reviews available resilience
 and energy planning from cities and communities in Lane County.
 The final section, Opportunities for Improving Lane County Electric System Affordability,
 Reliability, and Resilience summarizes feasible directions for action based on the
 information available in this assessment.

Part I: Foundations Preface
The energy system, illustrated in Figure 3, supplies energy for human activities and
encompasses all components related to production, conversion, delivery, and end-use of
energy. Energy and electricity are not the same, but the electricity system is a subset of the
larger energy system that includes things like natural gas infrastructure, transportation, and
more.

 Figure 3: Elements of the energy system

Getting to know the technologies, organizations, policies, history, and existing conditions of the
electricity system provides a foundation for future decision-making. The sections in this part of
the assessment aim to help the reader build that foundation.

 Part I: Foundations Preface

 12

 Chapter 1
 How Does the Lane County Electrical
 System Work?
 Ian Hermanson, Ali Trueworthy, and Candace Briggs

 Many technologies, organizations, and policies help ensure that people in
 Lane County have access to electricity in their homes, businesses, and
 community spaces. This section provides a foundation for understanding
 the modern electric grid.

 Energy is the ability to do work, and it can take many forms. Humans use technologies to
 convert naturally occurring energy sources into useful forms like light, heat, or motion. When a
 car engine combusts gasoline, hydrocarbons break in a burst of heat and pressure that is
 harnessed into mechanical motion. Water stored behind a dam builds immense pressure that
 drives turbines and generates electricity. Sunlight striking a solar panel excites electrons in
 semiconductor materials, producing a steady flow of electricity. Uranium atoms split inside a
 nuclear reactor, releasing heat that vaporizes water to spin massive turbines. When we charge
 our phones, have a package delivered by truck, or scrub dishes with hot water, we are
 consuming energy.

 Electricity is a valuable form of energy that can be precisely controlled, efficiently carried over
 long distances, and easily converted to other energy forms. Power generation is the process of
 converting any primary energy source into electricity.

 1.1 Energy and Electricity in Oregon
 In the 2024 Biennial Report, the Oregon Department of Energy (ODOE) breaks Oregon’s total
 2022 energy consumption (energy consumed to meet demand in Oregon, sourced from in-state
 or out-of-state sources) into three categories: electricity, direct use fuels, and transportation
 fuels. Figure 4 illustrates each category’s share of total 2022 consumption, and Figure 5
 illustrates Oregon’s 2023 state-level electricity resource mix.

Oregon’s population and economy have grown over the past twenty years while annual energy

energy efficiency initiatives and has resulted in Oregon’s relatively low per capita energy
consumption. In 2022, Oregon ranked 10th among U.S. states for lowest per capita energy use
overall 1 and 3rd for lowest per capita energy use in the residential sector. 2

1.2 Northwest Energy History
Before the advent of the electric grid and large-scale energy conversion, people living in the
Pacific Northwest relied on local resources for energy. Waterways and wind propelled canoes
that enabled regional transport. Woodfires provided indigenous peoples and early settlers with
heat, light, and energy for cooking. In the pre-industrial Pacific Northwest, most usable energy
came from food and burning wood and plant material. In many areas, seasonal salmon harvests
and camas, an energy dense native-growing staple, were foundational caloric sources. Land-
tending practices, including controlled burns, nourished the soil and supported camas and other
harvests. As settlers and the U.S. government forcibly relocated Indigenous people to
reservations, settler agriculture practices became the dominant mode of food production in the
Pacific Northwest.

category includes real-time market purchases utilities make to meet demand and is used by ODOE due to
the increasing complexity of accurately calculating a resource mix for market purchases.
1 Janine Benner et al., “2024 Biennial Energy Report,” Oregon Department of Energy, Nov. 2024.

https://www.oregon.gov/energy/Data-and-Reports/Documents/2024-Biennial-Energy-Report.pdf
2 Data from Energy Information Administration “Analysis,” June 2026.

https://www.eia.gov/states/OR/analysis

 Figure 7: Fishing with a Dip-net at Celilo Falls, 1942 1

Throughout the 19th century, technological, social, and political forces dramatically changed
human energy use. Irrigation technologies and fertilizers enabled a boom in farming throughout
the Columbia River Basin, coal-powered trains brought more people and products to the west,
and industries using mills and waterwheels were established along rivers. The first electric lights
in Oregon came to Portland in 1880 on the SS Columbia. An onboard generator powered arc
lights strung from the dock on the Willamette River to the Clarendon Hotel as thousands of
people witnessed the spectacle. In 1889, a fourteen-mile high-voltage transmission line
connected the Willamette Falls hydroelectric plant to Portland, 2 shown in Figure 8. This was the
first long-distance electricity transmission line in the United States.

1 Image from Old Oregon Photos https://www.oldoregonphotos.com
2 J. Hammarlund, “Oregon’s Role as an Energy Innovator,” FORUM.

https://digitalcollections.willamette.edu/bitstreams/de2efbd2-14e5-49a6-8bb3-b3b409830210/download

  Figure 8: Willamette Falls Powerhouse and Oregon’s first High-Voltage Transmission Line 1

Pacific Northwest electrical systems expanded dramatically in the early 20th century. At first,
many private companies provided small-scale electricity services, and several industries owned
their own generation. Private companies then began to consolidate, and industry began
purchasing electricity from utilities. Nationally, electrification of rural areas lagged behind
cities—by 1930, only about 10% of farms had electricity compared to nearly 90% of urban
homes. 2 The 1936 Rural Electrification Act (REA) rapidly extended electric service to half of
rural farms by 1942, as shown by Figure 9.

 Figure 9: The Rural Electrification Administration’s effect on Farm Electrification2

1 Image from Old Oregon Photos https://www.oldoregonphotos.com/
2 Information and Image from Federal Reserve Bank of Richmond, “Electrifying Rural America,” 2020.

https://www.richmondfed.org/publications/research/econ_focus/2020/q1/economic_history

 How did Lane County end up with so many
 electric service providers?
 In the early 20th century, the growth of electric systems was rapid, chaotic, and uneven.
 Cities often had multiple service providers, while many rural areas did not have electricity.
 Many providers were private companies. Because providing electric services was a
 capital-intensive business with limited end user markets, the 1920s and ‘30s brought an
 era of consolidation as many companies that owned limited assets (e.g., a generation
 plant and power lines connecting a handful of customers) were bought out.

 Lane County’s consumer-owned utilities grew out of citizen discontent with private service
 providers and the lack of service in rural areas. Local community debates reflected a
 broader national discussion about whether electric service should be private or public. In
 1908, citizens of Eugene voted to replace their private water company with the area’s first
 municipal water utility following a typhoid epidemic. They created the Eugene Water
 Board, which would become the Eugene Water & Electric Board (EWEB). EWEB entered
 the electric service sector by constructing the Walterville Hydroelectric Plant in 1911 and
 purchasing assets from a competing private power company in 1916. In the early 1930s,
 Washington and Oregon passed state laws allowing rural areas to form public utility
 districts. Other consumer-owned utilities (COUs) in Lane County followed: Blachly-Lane
 in 1937, Lane Electric in 1939, Central Lincoln Public Utility District in 1943, Springfield
 Utility Board (SUB) in 1949, and Emerald People’s Utility District (EPUD) in 1978.

 Lane County citizens created COUs to serve their communities despite opposition from
 existing private service providers. Because COUs exist to serve community interests
 rather than to profit, these utilities had little incentive to expand. As a result, Lane County
 has many individual utilities that serve individual cities or specific rural areas. 1,2,3,4

In 1929 the U.S. Army Core of Engineers (USACE) published what was known as the 308
Report. 5 The 308 Report recommended the construction of 10 hydroelectric dams on the
Columbia River. Because such construction projects were too large for private utility companies,
the federal government under President Franklin Roosevelt began construction of the
Bonneville and Grand Coulee Dams in 1934. In 1937, Roosevelt established the Bonneville
Power Administration (BPA) to market power and construct transmission lines for the new
Bonneville and Grand Coulee Dams. There are now 274 hydroelectric dams in the Columbia
River Basin, including 19 in British Columbia, Canada. Nearly 70% of the total generating
capacity in the Columbia River basin is owned by the federal government. While BPA markets

their-own/

power from these dams, the dams themselves are owned and operated by USACE and the U.S.
Bureau of Reclamation. 1 19% of the basin’s capacity is operated by municipally owned utilities,
like Seattle City Light. The remaining 12% of the basin’s capacity is owned by private
companies like investor-owned utilities. Figure 10 provides a map of major Columbia River
Basin dams and operators.

The government’s expanding hydroelectric system provided abundant, reliable power that
fueled post-war industrial growth. Between 1950 and 1970, the region’s electric system
expanded rapidly as a population boom, residential appliances, new industries, and rural
electrification increased demand. 3 By the 1970s, hydroelectric capacity in the Columbia River
Basin had reached its limit. Forecasts projected continued demand growth, which raised
concerns about energy shortages. In 1968, BPA and Washington Public Power Supply System

1 Northwest Power and Conservation Council, “Dams: history and purpose,”

https://www.nwcouncil.org/reports/columbia-river-history/damshistory/
2 Image from Bonneville Power Administration “Columbia River Basin,” https://www.bpa.gov/-

launched the Hydro-Thermal Power Program which aimed to build twenty nuclear plants and
two coal power plants in Oregon and Washington to meet projected demand. However, project
costs skyrocketed and the load growth forecasts that justified the massive project proved
inaccurate. Only two nuclear power plants were built, and public perception of the electric
industry soured as costs were passed on to customers. 1 With other options available, many
customers left BPA. BPA is still paying off debt from this program.

Public interest in energy efficiency, distrust in BPA, concerns over declining salmon populations,
and the need for a new long-term planning approach underpinned the 1980 Northwest Power
Act, which created the Northwest Power and Conservation Council. With the passage of the Act,
the Council was tasked to prioritize energy conservation and balance fish and wildlife protection
with the region’s need for affordable, reliable electricity. BPA funds were directed to mitigate
hydropower impacts on fish and wildlife. 2

Between the late 1990s and early 2000s, changing energy policies and a volatile regional
electricity market caused BPA to swing from being overpriced and undersubscribed to
underpriced and oversubscribed. At the time, BPA was obligated to meet the entire power
demand of its customer base. If BPA could not fully meet customer demand, it was forced to
buy relatively expensive power off the market to resell at a low rate. This was an untenable
sales structure. In 2002, BPA customers approved a revised structure in which BPA would sell
shares of the federal system output, known as “slices of the system.” This shift would reduce the
agency’s market exposure by shifting financial risks and benefits to customers. In 2011, BPA
introduced a tiered rate system that let customers decide whether the agency should purchase
additional electricity from the market on their behalf when their loads exceeded the allotted
amount 3 (see Sub-Section 1.5.1 for a description of the tiered system).

Throughout the 2000s and 2010s Oregon passed legislation to reduce emissions from electricity
and transportation. In 2007, Senate Bill 838 set a target for 25% renewable electricity by 2025,
and in 2016 the Oregon Renewable Portfolio Standard increased that goal to 50% by 2030. The
Oregon Department of Environmental Quality introduced incentives and targets for alternative
transportation fuels, while additional programs promoted residential solar, energy efficiency, and
electric vehicles. New wind and solar farms were gradually interconnected to the grid by BPA. In
2020, several large-scale wind and solar projects began development. 4

A timeline of Oregon’s Energy History is published by the Oregon Department of Energy.4

1.3 What is the Grid?
The electrical grid, depicted in Figure 11, is a vast, interconnected network that delivers
electricity to homes, businesses and other end users. The grid performs three main functions:
generation, transmission, and distribution. Energy is converted from its primary source to
electricity at a power generation facility. Generated electricity passes through generation

enters homes and businesses. 1,2

Electric grids span massive geographic areas called interconnections. The U.S. grid is divided
into three geographic areas: west of the Rocky Mountain region, east of the Rocky Mountain
region, and Texas, as shown in Figure 12. 3 Interconnections evolved in the early 20th century
as local utilities began connecting to neighboring transmission systems to meet growing
electricity demand. Synchronizing power generation across an entire continent is a technical
challenge, so the grid evolved into the separate Eastern Interconnection and Western
Interconnections linked only by a few high-voltage DC ties. Within each interconnection, there
are usually multiple routes for power to flow from generators to consumers. This redundancy
promotes reliability by preventing individual component failures from causing outages or
cascading failures. 4 Lane County is part of the Western Interconnection, which covers British
Columbia and Alberta Canada, the United States west of the Rocky Mountain region to the
Pacific, and Baja California, Mexico.

 What is the Grid?

 21

 Figure 12: U.S. Interconnections

On the grid, electricity supply and demand must remain in balance. Excess supply or shortfalls
risk triggering grid overloads, infrastructure and equipment failure (due to melting or breaking),
and rolling blackouts. 1 A reliable grid can withstand instantaneous changes in supply and
demand to provide a stable and adequate flow of electricity to end users. 2 Grid reliability
depends on long-term planning to balance generation and load (for more on this planning see
Section 2.2). Grid operators keep the grid balanced as electricity demands fluctuate by the
minute, hour, day, season, and year. Operators can control the electric output of generation
sources to adjust to demand changes.

Energy storage technologies support grid reliability by storing electricity when generation
exceeds demand and releasing electricity back to the grid during higher-demand periods.
Storage technologies include residential, commercial, and grid-scale batteries, pumped hydro
storage, compressed air, and thermal systems. 3 Water stored in reservoirs behind hydroelectric
dams is another form of energy storage. Utility-scale energy storage is growing in the United
States. 4

1.4 Who Manages the Grid?
The power grid, which is often described as the “world’s largest machine,” operates on scales
too large for a single entity to manage. Electricity generation, transmission, distribution, sales,
and regulation are managed by a network of interconnected organizations and structures.
Those organizations vary in different regions of the United States. Generally, they include
utilities, balancing authorities, power marketing administrators, and regulators.

 Who Manages the Grid?

1.4.1 Utilities
Electric utilities manage the electric distribution system, a network of low voltage power lines
that delivers electricity to end-users such as homes and businesses. 1 Utilities can be investor-
owned utilities (IOUs) or consumer-owned (COUs). IOUs are for-profit companies regulated as
monopolies by state public utility commissions. COUs are public entities that fall into one of
three categories: (1) Municipal COUs are governed by board members who are either elected
by city residents or appointed by city officials, (2) Public Utility Districts (PUDs) are governed by
commissioners elected by residents of the (usually rural) district, and (3) Cooperative COUs are
governed by a board elected by co-op members. 2

Figure 13 is a service area map of Lane County’s ten electric utilities. Of the ten utilities, one is
an IOU that serves a large area outside Lane County (Pacific Power) and the rest are COUs.
Three COUs are public utilities that primarily serve areas outside Lane County, but have small
service areas within the county (Consumers Power, Douglas Electric and Midstate Electric), two
are municipal utilities (Springfield Utility Board and EWEB), two are people’s utility districts
(Emerald PUD and Central Lincoln PUD), and two are electric cooperatives (Lane Electric and
Blachly-Lane). By number of customers, EWEB is the largest COU in Oregon Lane County
utility types are listed in Table 2. Lane County’s utilities vary in both the size of the service area
and the number of customers.

Table 2 shows these statistics for each utility. Throughout this report, we often provide
information by utility, but do not always include information about Consumers Power, Douglas
Electric, or Midstate Electric because their service territories in Lane County are so small. Of the
remaining utilities, Pacific Power, a business unit of PacifiCorp, is the only one with most of their
service area outside of Lane County.

1.4.2 Balancing Authorities
Balancing authorities stabilize and synchronize a defined region of the power grid, called the
balancing authority area, with the rest of the interconnection. Each balancing authority ensures
that energy supply and demand are finely balanced in their territory. To meet real-time and
forecasted supply and demand change, balancing authorities exchange energy with neighboring
balancing authorities and adjust the electric output from generation sources. 1 A variety of
entities serve as balancing authorities. In the Western Interconnection, 38 different balancing
authorities coordinate to maintain grid stability (Figure 14). Major balancing authorities include
the California Independent System Operator (CAISO, noted as CISO on map), which manages
most of California, the Bonneville Power Administration (or BPAT on the map, the “T” denotes
transmission) which manages broad areas of the Pacific Northwest, and large IOUs such as
Portland General Electric (PGE) and Pacific Power. Coordination across this complex balancing
authority patchwork introduces planning and market coordination challenges but also offers
opportunities to enhance reliability and reduce costs through regional resource sharing.

1 U.S. Department of Energy, “How it Works: The Role of a Balancing Authority,” 2023.

https://www.energy.gov/sites/default/files/2023-08/Balancing%20Authority%20Backgrounder_2022-
Formatted_041723_508.pdf

1.4.3 Power Marketing Administrators and the Bonneville Power
 Administration
A Power Marketing Administration (PMA) is a DOE agency that operates regional electric
systems and sells the output of federally owned and operated hydroelectric dams. 1 PMAs
generally operate in self-contained territories and commonly serve as the balancing authority for
their respective regions. The United States has four PMAs: the BPA, Western Area Power
Administration (WAPA), Southwestern Power Administration (SWPA), and Southeastern Power
Administration (SEPA), shown in Figure 15. The majority of PMA power is hydroelectric. By
federal statute, PMAs prioritize selling power to publicly owned and cooperative utilities and set
the lowest feasible electric rates. PMA power is relatively inexpensive because it originates from
decades-old dams. Typically, PMAs do not own the generating plants, which are owned by
USACE, U.S. Bureau of Reclamation, or International Boundary and Water Commission (an
agency that operates hydroelectric dams along the U.S.-Mexico border. 2,3

 Figure 15: U.S. Power Marketing Administrators Map 4

In the Northwest, BPA sells power from one nuclear power plant and 31 dams throughout the
Columbia River Basin, serves as the balancing authority for most of the Pacific Northwest,
including Lane County, and coordinates most of the Pacific Northwest's power supply. BPA
manages around 15,000 miles of high-voltage transmission lines, representing 75% of the
region’s high-voltage transmission. WAPA manages 17,000 miles of high-voltage transmission
across a much larger territory. While BPA operates slightly fewer miles of transmission than
WAPA, its infrastructure is concentrated within a smaller geographic area. As a result, BPA has

a distinctly outsized influence over the Pacific Northwest electrical system and broader economy
relative to other PMAs. 1

BPA is a self-financed agency that receives no federal appropriations or tax dollars, instead
covering operating costs through power sales. To finance large projects, BPA borrows from the
U.S. Treasury through bonds. As of 2018, BPA was $5.53 billion in debt to the U.S. treasury,
nearing BPA’s $7.70 billion borrowing cap.1 Limited borrowing capacity constrains BPA’s ability
to tackle large infrastructure projects. Nevertheless, BPA currently has a portfolio of projects
planned in their Grid Expansion and Reinforcement Portfolio. 2

1.4.4 Regulation and Oversight
The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) was created as an independent
commission within DOE in 1977 when Congress passed the Department of Energy Organization
Act (P.L 95-91). FERC was authorized to take over regulatory duties that were previously the
domain of other agencies. FERC oversees the bulk power system, which includes interstate
transmission of electricity, natural gas and oil, the wholesale electric market, and operation of
federal hydroelectric projects. The North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) is
a non-profit, non-governmental organization certified by FERC in 2006 to maintain and improve
the reliability of the bulk power system by issuing mandatory reliability standards and
guidelines. 3 FERC and NERC delegated authority to six regional entities to coordinate and
promote bulk electric system reliability across the United States. The Western Electricity
Coordinating Council (WECC) is the regional entity responsible for system reliability across
the Western Interconnection. 4

The Oregon Public Utility Commission (PUC) is a state regulatory agency that regulates the
rates, reliability and service quality of IOUs, natural gas utilities, and telephone service
providers. PUC Commissioners are appointed by the Oregon governor for four-year terms. 5
Oregon PUC does not regulate COUs, except regarding safety. COU policy mandates are often
less rigorous. 6 An important Oregon PUC role is enforcing the National Electrical Safety Code
(NESC), which sets standards to protect utility workers and the public during the installation,
operation and maintenance of transmission and distribution electrical systems and
communication lines. The NESC is published by IEEE and updated every five years. 7

The Oregon Consumer Utility Board was established in 1984 to advocate for consumer utility
needs by conducting economic and legal analysis, engaging with community organizations and
policy makers, and promoting equitable policies that prioritize communities. 1

1.4.5 Regional Grid Operators and Power Markets
Regional Transmission Operators (RTOs) and Independent System Operators are
organizations established by FERC to manage how power generators compete to generate and
sell electricity. Figure 16 provides a map of U.S. RTOs and ISOs. Many RTOs and ISOs also
serve as balancing authorities, managing the transmission system and supporting grid reliability
planning.

 Figure 16: U.S. RTOs and ISOs Map 2

While Oregon is not part of an ISO or RTO, some ISOs and RTOs that serve within the Western
Interconnection offer power markets that Oregon utilities, balancing authorities, and
independent power generators may choose to join (Section 2.1). Organized power markets
provide a platform to exchange electricity across a broader regional network, which can lower
electricity costs and provide access to diverse generation resources. 3 In Lane County, BPA and

1 Oregon Citizens’ Utility Board, “About Us,” https://oregoncub.org/who-we-are/

commission

EWEB are examples of entities that participate in power markets. BPA’s and Pacific Power’s
current power market transition is detailed in Section 3.1.

Power market participants—which include independent power producers, utilities, balancing
authorities, and others—buy and sell electricity in day-ahead or real-time transactions. In day-
ahead transactions, participants exchange electricity the day before service is needed based on
demand and supply forecasts. Real-time markets allow participants to exchange electricity on
the same day as service. 1 Day-ahead markets often include real-time components to respond to
unexpected demand changes. Real-time and day-ahead transactions help utilities and
balancing authorities plan and maintain system reliability.

1.5 How is Electricity Exchanged in Lane County
In the United States, electricity is exchanged through a dynamic system of interconnected
transactions between independent power producers, power market administrators, power
markets, utilities, and end-users. Because the structure and organization of these systems vary
regionally, this section will focus specifically on Oregon and Lane County. Figure 17 maps the
main participants, interactions, and associated physical grid assets that comprise electricity
exchange in the Pacific Northwest.

 • Boxes represent entities participating in regional electricity exchange.

 • Circles represent power generation facilities or other electricity sources. Although we
 use icons that represent different types of electricity generation (e.g., nuclear,
 wind), those are only examples. For instance, independent power producers sell
 electricity from many different resources, not just nuclear.

 • Arrows represent electricity exchanges between entities. An exchange may
 involve physical transfer of electricity, a financial exchange for a quantity of electricity,
 or provision of an electrical service (e.g., utility service). Two arrows pointing in opposite
 directions indicate a bidirectional exchange. Each arrow is labeled with a letter
 that serves as a reference in this section.

 • Colors are assigned to each entity and documented in the legend. These colors
 also indicate ownership or management of circled power sources and grid segments.

U.S. power sale can be divided into two main components: wholesale markets, which facilitate
bulk power transactions between generators, BPA, and utilities; and retail markets, which
govern how end-users purchase electricity from utilities

1.5.1 Contracts Between BPA and Utilities
All of Lane County’s electric utilities have contracts with BPA, with some buying almost all of
their power from the organization. This section focuses on BPA contracts with COUs. COUs are
BPA’s “preferred customers” 1, meaning they get an allotted amount of the power generated by
the federal system 2. COUs purchase power at cost-based rates, meaning that BPA sets rates
such that they can recover the costs of generating and transporting power. The cost-based
rates are relatively low and called “Tier 1” rates.

The allotment of power that a COU can buy at Tier 1 rates is referred to as the utility’s Contract
High Water Mark. The Contract High Water Mark for the contract period from 2011 to 2028 was
established through a process BPA called the Tiered Rate Methodology. For contracts
beginning in 2028, BPA will replace this Tiered Rate Methodology with the Public Rate Design
Methodology. There are some differences between the two methodologies, but generally the
process remains the same. BPA guarantees that they will always be able to provide power up to
the utility’s Contract High Water Mark.

Utilities that require more electricity than the Contract High Water Mark Tier 1 electricity that
they are allotted can elect to meet their additional need through BPA at the Tier 2 rate.
Alternatively, a utility can purchase electricity from non-federal sources or a combination of Tier
2 and non-federal sources. The Tier 2 rates tend to be significantly higher than the Tier 1 rates,
but may be slightly less than market rates.

To purchase power from non-federal sources, a utility needs to have the capacity to conduct
market activities. In Lane County, EWEB is the only COU that buys power directly off the
market, while some of the other utilities participate in the market through organizations (such as
PNGC Power) that purchase on behalf of numerous COUs. Many COUs in Lane County meet
their additional electricity need through BPA at the Tier 2 rate. Figure 18 shows how a COU
might determine whether they purchase Tier 2 power.

1 See Section 1.5.1 for an overview of the policies that established COUs as BPA’s preferred customers
2 Typically referred to as their “slice of the system”

 33

Figure 18: Flow Chart Showing Utility Decisions Regarding Power Purchase from BPA.

 34

 How do BPA Tier 1 and Tier 2 Rates compare to market rates?
 BPA’s published average rates (for fiscal years 2026–2028) for COUs who purchase
 only Tier 1 power is $37.77/MWh. For COUs purchasing both Tier 1 and Tier 2 power,
 the average rate is $40.16 per megawatt-hour (MWh). 1 Figure 19 shows wholesale
 electricity prices by region in the United States. The additional green line is $40/MWh,
 exemplifying the relatively low cost of Tier 1 power.

 BPA sets Tier 2 rates based on the actual or forecasted price paid by BPA to meet
 demand.

 When BPA determines utilities’ Contract High Water Marks, they do so based on
 conservative estimates of the power that will be available from the federal dam system
 (see Section 3.1 for discussion of what factors impact the amount of available power).
 Because of their conservative estimate, there is often more power available than is
 contracted to utilities. BPA can then sell that power at Tier 2 rates.

 When there is enough power in the federal system to meet demand, Tier 2 rates will
 often be less than the market rate for electricity. However, in cases where water levels
 behind the dams are low and/or demand is high, BPA Tier 2 rates will be closer to or
 above market prices because BPA will need to purchase power off the market to deliver
 to its customers.

 Figure 19: EIA U.S. Regional Wholesale Electricity Prices Comparison with BPA Tier 1 Rate 2

1 Bonneville Power Administration. https://www.bpa.gov/
2 Image adjusted from U.S. Energy Information Administration, “Regional Wholesale Markets: April 2026,”

June 2026. https://www.eia.gov/electricity/monthly/update/wholesale-markets.php

 35

BPA’s tiered approach to rate setting is meant to prevent BPA from independently shouldering
unbound market costs, enable utility customers to choose how they want to meet their end user
customers’ load, and insulate customers from costs associated with the choices of other
customers 1 (see Section 1.2 for how and why this tier system was developed).

Utilities secure electricity supply through contracts with BPA that lock in the rate structure and
power delivery agreements. The Tier 1 rate is set for a rate period of two years, meaning Tier 1
rates can change many times over the course of a contract period. Rates change according to
the costs incurred by BPA to supply power and maintain the transmission system. The current
contracts (known as Regional Dialogue contracts) span the period from 2011–2028; utilities
are currently renegotiating contracts for 2028–2044 (called Provider of Choice contracts).

Distribution utilities pay both flat rate charges and use-based charges set by BPA. They may
also pay charges for load shaping and/or risk adjustments, depending on the contract and
power delivery product. Those charges vary between BPA’s priority customers, which is why
rates are given as averages. Full details on BPA charges can be found in the BPA rate
schedule, currently available for the fiscal year 2026. 2

Under extended contracts with BPA, utilities select BPA power delivery product options tailored
to meet their needs. 3,4,5 There are two product types that a utility can choose for their BPA
contract: Load Following and Planned Products. The fundamental difference between
Planned Products (which include Block and Slice/Block products) and the Load Following
product is how (or to what extent) they deal with hourly changes in demand. For the Load
Following product, BPA serves the utility’s load on an hourly basis, regardless of the size of the
load. For a Planned Product, BPA serves a pre-defined amount of the utility’s load, and any
peaks above that amount need to be served by additional resources dispatched or purchased
by the utility.

Planned Products give utilities autonomy in how they serve their peak loads; however, they
come with associated risks and requirements. For example, if a utility plans to serve their peak
loads with their own generation, and something happens to limit the availability of that
generation, they may be exposed to high market prices to meet peak loads or may be unable to
meet those loads, leading to outages. A utility choosing a Planned Product must have control of
dispatchable generation or the ability to purchase power in real-time markets so that they can
meet peak demand.

Load Following products shift the responsibility of meeting peak demand to BPA, enabling the
utility to allocate their effort to tasks other than dispatch or purchasing. That said, Load
Following services come with additional charges from BPA. BPA demand charges apply to only
their Load Following and Block with Shaping Capacity products. Other charges like the Load

Shaping charge and the Risk Adjustment apply across products but will be applied more often
for products that include more load shaping capabilities.

For the 2028–2044 contract period, BPA offers versions of their planned products with partial
load shaping services. These products are called Block with Shaping Capacity and a Block with
Shaping Capacity + Peak Load Variance. A summary of available products, their load shaping
capabilities, and their resource flexibility is provided in Figure 20.

 Figure 20: BPA Product Options

A utility’s product choice can take many factors into consideration, including uncertainties (such
as future load). Table 3 shows some of the factors that would go into a utility’s product choice.
Operational and cost-related factors are relatively straightforward, but a utility’s specific goals
may also impact their product choice. For example, if a given utility expects many electric
vehicles to come online in their service area, they may strategically choose a Load Following
product that can handle large spikes in load. Alternatively, they might decide to dedicate more
time and resources to establishing customer programs that reward customers for charging their
electric vehicles during off-peak hours (an example of a demand response program, further
discussed in Section 3.3) and investing in their own generation, in which case, they might be
inclined to choose a Block product.

Residential rates cover single-family, multifamily, and manufactured homes, where electricity is
used for heating, lighting, cooking, and appliances. Lane County utility residential rate structures
are presented in Table 6. Pacific Power is the only utility that differentiates between single and
multi-family customers by offering a lower fixed charge for multi-family customers to promote
affordability. All utilities apply an energy charge, but with varying complexity. EWEB, EPUD and
Central Lincoln PUD include a flat energy charge—a single, fixed rate per kWh regardless of
when or how much electricity is consumed. This provides customers with simple and predictable

 40

billing but offers little financial incentive to reduce or shift consumption. SUB’s seasonal rising
block energy charge includes a higher winter kWh usage threshold, meaning that customers
can consume more electricity before reaching the higher-priced block. Lane Electric’s seasonal
blocks have identical kWh usage thresholds but reduced prices in the winter to ease the cost of
seasonal heating demands. Pacific Power is the only utility offering on-peak charge and off-
peak credits to encourage demand flexibility and load shifting. No utilities use a residential
demand charge.

Together, Table 6 and Table 7 show that Lane County utilities deploy a variety of rate design
elements. Pacific Power, Lane County’s only IOU, offers the most complex rate structures,
indicating a difference between large IOU and small COU approaches to rate design. Lane
County COUs have an opportunity to adopt rate design strategies already used by other local
utilities to address new community priorities, regional challenges, or energy and operating costs.

Average electricity rates in Lane County are lower than in Oregon statewide, which in turn is
lower than the U.S. average. This is due to Oregon’s access to low-cost federal hydropower,
and Lane County’s high concentration of consumer-owned utilities that predominantly purchase
BPA power at Tier 1 rates. Figure 21 shows average rates compiled from the Oregon
Department of Energy 2024, 1 EIA Residential 2025, 2 FindEnergy.Com 2024, 3 and
EnergySage.Com 2025. 4

 Figure 21: Average Electricity Rates in Lane County, Oregon, and the U.S.

1.6 What Electricity Generation Sources Supply Lane County
Each Lane County utility determines its own electricity resource portfolio. Most utilities in Lane
County purchase nearly 100% of their electricity through long-term contracts with BPA, which is
typical for small Pacific Northwest COUs. Utilities may also own local power generation facilities,
sign contracts with independent generators, or participate in wholesale markets. Lane County
utility resource mixes are documented in Table 8.

1 Oregon Department of Energy, “Lane County”, Oct. 2024. https://energyinfo.oregon.gov/energy-

The categories used to define electrical generation resources delineate based on size, location,
owner, or other factors. A single generation source can fit into multiple categories. For example,
a rooftop solar photovoltaic (solar PV) installation is small-scale, behind-the-meter, and
considered a distributed energy resource. A large nuclear power plant is both utility-scale and
transmission-connected. A local energy resource, like the Short Mountain Power Plant, is utility-
scale and distribution-connected.

1.6.1 BPA Electricity Resource Mix
BPA electricity is generated from a mix of hydroelectric, nuclear, and renewable resources
(Figure 26). The vast majority (74.4%) of BPA’s 2024 generation is from federal hydroelectric
power. Non-specified purchases include transactions within the Western Energy Imbalance
Market and other systems without knowledge of specific energy sources. 1

 Figure 26: BPA 2024 Resource Mix 2

megawatts. Table 9 lists grid-connected hydroelectric, biomass, and natural gas power plants
operating within Lane County. Ownership and selling structures vary. Some local generation
resources are owned and operated by a utility like EWEB or EPUD. These resources are
connected to the utility distribution system and supply power exclusively to utility customers.
Several local hydroelectric dams are federally owned and operated by BPA. University of
Oregon’s 10 MW combined heat and power plant is a distributed energy resource not connected

 48

Figure 28 maps behind-the-meter solar PV installations across Lane County, scaled by annual
generation in MWh. Most solar PV production is concentrated in Eugene, with a single large
commercial plant in the Wolf Creek area generating just over 1 gigawatt-hour (GWh) annually.
This solar PV plant produces enough power to meet the average annual electricity consumption
of 88 Lane County households. 1

Construction of behind-the-meter solar PV installations in Lane County has accelerated in
recent years with 55 new installations added in 2021 and 76 in 2022.1 Lane County currently
hosts 1,297 solar PV sites, of which 190 are commercial and 1,107 are residential. These
installations are connected to the grid through “net metering” where power from the solar
panels is used first to power the residence or business and then any excess flows to the grid; in
cloudy periods or at night power is pulled from the grid for the residence or business. The
electricity bill is based on the net difference between power used and power exported to the
grid. If more power is produced than used, then the customer gets credit from the utility.
Commercial solar facilities collectively produce 7,371.5 MWh per year, while residential systems
contribute an additional 6,313.7 MWh. 2

profiles-2024/lane
2 Northwest Power and Conservation Council, ibid.

None of the locally owned generation goes onto the bulk grid (though the USACE hydropower
generation does). Section 3.4 and Chapter 6 discuss options for local generation on the
distribution system, meaning electricity generated and used in Lane County.

Potential projects to develop utility scale projects that sell to the regional grid face several
barriers. Any new power generating facility feeding onto the transmission system must obtain
permission from the transmission line owner to connect to the grid. This process often requires
lengthy studies to identify infrastructure upgrades and additions. As of 2025, around 200,000
MW of renewable energy projects were waiting for BPA approval, a process that can take many
years. Public reporting on the interconnection queue for BPA has since driven state lawmakers
in Oregon and Washington to take steps to speed up the build out of renewable energy
generation. 1 For example, in 2025 Governor Tina Kotek released Executive Order 25-25 titled
Accelerating Wind and Solar Energy Development in Advance of Elimination of Federal
Clean Energy Tax Credits. 2 The order establishes directives to expedite the permitting and
development process for wind and solar energy projects to target as much new construction as
possible before July 4, 2026 to leverage soon-to-be-terminated federal renewable energy tax
credits. 3

Interconnection requests for IOUs (in Lane County, Pacific Power) follow the policies put in
place by Oregon Interconnection procedures AR 521 if they are less than 10 MW, 4 and
information about interconnection of large projects can be found on BPA and Pacific Power
websites. 5,6 If a generation project requires transmission upgrades, the project developer may
be required by the transmission utility to pay for some or all upgrades. From what we know of
transmission in Lane County, there are not currently major capacity constraints, but BPA is
performing Interconnection Cluster Studies to build understanding of possible capacity
constraints relative to interconnection requests in the queue. 7 The Interconnection Guidebook
published by the Energy Trust of Oregon provides additional information. 8

 Chapter 2
 What Policies Impact Lane County’s
 Grid?
Adrienne Rackley, Ali Trueworthy, Brooke Marten, and Ellie Estreich

Policies made by federal and state officials impact how Lane County utilities buy power;
where and under what restrictions local governments, utilities, and developers can build
new large loads or generation; how local governments and utilities pursue energy
efficiency and conservation efforts; and where and how local governments,
organizations, consumers, and utilities can find support efforts related to energy. This
chapter provides an overview of policies in each of these categories.

2.1 Policies Governing BPA Ratemaking
All Lane County utilities purchase power from BPA. The collective impact of policies governing
BPA ratemaking is that BPA sells, markets, and transmits electricity at the lowest feasible cost
with preference to public utilities and toothwest Pacific Northwest customers. 1 BPA is mandated
to set rates such that they can recover costs. They have no ongoing revenues from the federal
government. They are not appropriated funding from Congress but can borrow money from the
U.S. treasury. Current BPA rate structures for Lane County utilities are described in Chapter 4.
The historical statutes defining BPA’s role in marketing federal hydroelectric power and the way
that BPA sets its rates today are:

The Reclamation Act of 1902 required that the benefits of federal power be provided to the
public at the lowest possible cost. 2

The Bonneville Project Act of 1937 directed BPA to prioritize public bodies and consumer-
owned utilities in power sales (“preference customers”), specifically those in the Pacific
Northwest. The Act mandated BPA to set rates in order to recover the costs related to producing
and transmitting energy from the Federal Columbia River Power System. 3

1 Bonneville Power Administration, “Bonneville Power Administration Rates Overview,” Sep. 2024.

https://olis.oregonlegislature.gov/liz/2023I1/Downloads/CommitteeMeetingDocument/285681
2 J. Hammarlund, “Oregon’s Role as an Energy Innovator,” FORUM.

https://digitalcollections.willamette.edu/bitstreams/de2efbd2-14e5-49a6-8bb3-b3b409830210/download
3 Northwest Power and Conservation Council, “Bonneville Power Administration: History,”

https://www.nwcouncil.org/reports/columbia-river-history/bpahistory/

 52

The Flood Control Act of 1944 directed BPA to market power “in such a manner as to
encourage the most widespread use thereof at the lowest possible rates to consumers
consistent with sound business principles.” 1 The PNW Consumer Power Preference Act of
1964 limited BPA power sales outside of the Pacific Northwest to surplus energy, ensuring
priority to regional customers. 2

The Federal Columbia River Transmission System Act of 1974 gave BPA’s administrator
the authority to integrate and transmit power from sources beyond the federal dam system. This
means that they can buy electricity on behalf of their customers. The Act also created the BPA
Fund, which enabled BPA to manage funding and repayment obligations based on rates rather
than on annual appropriations from congress. 3

The Pacific Northwest Electric Power Planning and Conservation Act of 1980 (also known
as the Northwest Power Act) aimed to balance the need for affordable electricity with the
obligation to protect fish and wildlife by establishing guidelines for cost allocation, encouraging
transparent ratemaking processes, and allowing the BPA to prioritize energy conservation
before pursuing new power sources. 4 The Northwest Power Act also created the Northwest
Power and Conservation Council, discussed in the next section.

https://digitalcollections.willamette.edu/bitstreams/de2efbd2-14e5-49a6-8bb3-b3b409830210/download

 Policies Governing BPA Ratemaking

2.2 Policies Impacting Large Load Development
Large new developments within COU service territories are constrained in how they can acquire
and pay for electricity service. They do not always get access to federal hydropower resources
at priority rates like many of the existing COU customers. This section reviews the policies
governing these large developments (see Section 3.3.2 for more). Overall, these policies strive
to protect consumers from bearing the costs of large, energy-intensive developments.

BPA’s New Large Single Load (1980) policy was established under the Northwest Power Act
and describes how BPA defines, monitors, and sets rates for New Large Single Loads
(NLSLs). The designation applies to any significant new load or expansion at a single facility in
BPA service area that increases by 10 MW or more within any consecutive 12-month monitoring
period. Once a facility is designated as an NLSL, the classification remains in effect regardless
of future reductions in the load size. 1,2

NLSLs are served at BPA's marginal resource cost-based rate, known as the New Resources
Firm Power rate, rather than the lower Priority Firm Power rates that apply to preference
customers. In 2026, the New Resource Firm Power rate is almost triple the Priority Firm Power
rate. 3 The NLSL rate structure is designed to address the potential relocation of energy-
intensive industries to the Pacific Northwest in pursuit of BPA’s low-cost federal hydropower
rates. The NLSL approach may also help ensure the costs of generating and delivering power
are appropriately allocated when new, energy-intensive loads come online.1,2

BPA actively oversees its NLSL determination process. Utilities are required to notify BPA of
any developing or planned NLSLs, as failure to report them may result in back billing to recover
the difference in rates. BPA collaborates with utilities to establish service arrangements and
monitor changes. 4,5 Facility types that frequently meet the 10 MW load threshold include data
centers, heavy-duty fleet electric vehicle charging stations, and large industrial plants with
substantial energy requirements.

The NLSL policy can both protect and constrain communities. The rate policy discourages
energy-intensive industries from relocating to the region purely to take advantage of BPA’s
inexpensive federal power. While this may impede the development of industries or facilities
with high energy demands, the policy also helps protect existing BPA customers from rate
increases that could be triggered by new large loads. Additionally, it encourages investment in
new, non-federal generation sources, greater energy efficiency, and demand-side management
strategies. 6,7

1 Bonneville Power Administration, “Bonneville Power Administration New Large Single Load Policy,” Apr.

2001. https://www.bpa.gov/-/media/Aep/power/regional-dialogue/nlsl-policy-04-2001.pdf
2 Bonneville Power Administration, “Fact Sheet: New Large Single Load,” Oct. 2020.

https://www.bpa.gov/-/media/Aep/about/publications/fact-sheets/fs-202011-New-Large-Single-Load.pdf
3 Bonneville Power Administration, “Power Rates,” https://www.bpa.gov/energy-and-services/rate-and-

tariff-proceedings/power-rates
4 Bonneville Power Administration, Apr. 2001. Ibid.
5 Bonneville Power Administration, Oct. 2020. Ibid.
6 GDS Associates, https://www.eesconsulting.com/bonneville-power-administration-bpa/large-industrial-

load-analysis.html

https://www.tricitiesbusinessnews.com/articles/4193

 Policies Impacting Large Load Development

 54

POWER Act (HB3546, 2025), an Oregon state law, establishes new requirements for large
energy-using facilities in the state, aiming to increase electricity service transparency,
equitability, and accountability for facilities consuming 20 MW or more annually. The bill focuses
on balancing the costs associated with powering these facilities while protecting other electricity
consumers, promoting renewable energy integration, and ensuring compliance with Oregon's
clean energy targets. 1The Act directs the PUC to create policies holding large energy users
accountable for their costs. 2

While BPA’s NLSL policy applies to public preference customers (i.e., a publicly owned utility or
cooperative) and governs the rate at which BPA supplies federal power to new large loads, the
POWER Act applies to investor-owned utilities and directs the Oregon PUC to ensure that the
costs of serving large energy users are not shifted onto other ratepayers. 3

2.3 Policies Affecting Energy Efficiency and Conservation
An exhaustive history of policies that prioritize or otherwise impact energy efficiency and
conservation measures is beyond the scope of this report; however, we present several
important federal policies focused on energy efficiency and conservation, as well as several
more recent state policies that set requirements for local governments and utilities in Oregon.

The Northwest Power Act (1980), 1 a federal law, prioritized energy efficiency as the primary
method to meet future energy demands and encouraged utilities to adopt cost-effective
measures rather than relying on new power plant construction. The Act also mandated the
protection of fish and wildlife affected by Columbia River hydroelectric development and other
Northwest waterways and created a fish and wildlife program dedicated to mitigating the
environmental impacts of hydropower dams and ensuring that ecological concerns are
integrated into energy system operations.

The Energy Policy Act (2005), a federal law, addressed energy production, conservation, and
energy efficiency issues across the United States. The Act introduced a wide array of federal
incentives for renewable energy development, promoted energy efficiency programs, and
supported innovation in energy technologies. These incentives included tax credits for
renewable energy projects, loan guarantees for energy infrastructure, and energy savings
performance contracts for conservation measures at federal facilities. 2 While some provisions of
the Energy Policy Act have since been superseded or expanded by more recent legislation,
such as the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) of 2022, 3 the framework continues to inform and
support energy policy in Oregon and across the country.

The Energy Independence and Security Act (2007), 4 a federal law, was designed to enhance
the nation’s energy security through renewable energy sources and energy efficiency standards.
The Act introduced national standards for federal agencies, the automotive industry, and other
sectors, prioritizing technology advancements in transportation energy, such as biofuels, electric
vehicles, and renewable energy technologies. Energy efficiency provisions encouraged federal
agencies and the private sector to integrate energy-saving practices such as improving fuel
efficiency for vehicles, implementing stricter energy codes for buildings, and designing energy-
efficient products. 5

The Oregon Renewable Portfolio Standard (2007) was first adopted in 2007 and expanded in
2016 (Senate Bill 838 and 1547, respectively). The RPS set requirements for electric utilities to
procure increasing percentages of their electricity from qualifying renewable resources, such as,
wind, solar, biomass, and small hydropower. Large investor-owned utilities, like Pacific Power,
must meet a RPS target of 50% renewable energy by 2040, while large consumer-owned
utilities, such as EWEB, must reach 25% by 2025, as long as achieving the target does not

1 Northwest Power and Conservation Council, “Northwest Power Act,”

https://www.nwcouncil.org/reports/columbia-river-history/northwestpoweract/
2 U.S. Federal Legislation, “H.R.6 - Energy Policy Act of 2005,” 2005. https://www.congress.gov/bill/109th-

congress/house-bill/6
3 U.S. Department of Energy, “Inflation Reduction Act of 2022,” Sep. 2023.

https://www.energy.gov/lpo/inflation-reduction-act-2022
4 U.S Environmental Protection Agency, “Summary of the Energy Independence and Security Act,” Dec.

2007. https://www.epa.gov/laws-regulations/summary-energy-independence-and-security-act
5 Oregon Department of Energy, “Biennial Energy Report – Chapter 6: Energy Efficiency,”

http://www.oregon.gov/energy/Data-and-Reports/Documents/BER-Chapter-6-Energy-Efficiency.pdf

 Policies Affecting Energy Efficiency and Conservation

 56

force them to reduce their Tier 1 purchases from BPA. Smaller utilities, such as EPUD and Lane
Electric Cooperative, are obligated to secure at least 5% of their electricity from renewables
starting in 2025, with the same protection of their Tier 1 allocation as above, reflecting the
program’s tiered approach to renewable energy adoption across Oregon. 1

Governor Brown Executive Order 20-04 Oregon Climate Action Plan (2020), 2 a state policy,
directed Oregon’s state agencies to accelerate actions to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG)
emissions and regulate climate change impacts by mandating a broad set of actions across
energy, transportation, land use, and public health sectors to achieve Oregon’s climate and
resiliency objectives. 3, 4,

Oregon Clean Energy Targets Bill (2021) is a state law that sets clean energy targets, and
mandates increasing percent reductions in GHG emissions relative to a baseline. PGE, Pacific
Power, and Electricity Service Suppliers 5,6must reduce GHG emissions resulting from electricity
sold in Oregon to: 80% below baseline emissions by 2030, 90% below baseline emissions by
2035, and 100% below baseline emissions 2040. 7

The Inflation Reduction Act (IRA, 2022) established or expanded several categories of tax
credits and incentives related to energy efficiency and energy conservation, including consumer
tax credits for energy efficiency investments, clean energy systems, and alternative fueling
infrastructure; tax credits and deductions for developers meeting certain standards or achieving
energy usage reductions; and tax credits for purchases of qualified electric vehicles. The IRA
was a reconciliation bill with the original legislation slated to allow these provisions to continue
until 2032.

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA, 2025) sped up the expiration of several tax credits
incentivizing investments in energy efficiency and conservation as specified in the IRA (2022),
including consumer tax credits for energy efficiency investments (including for residential solar
and storage) and the electric vehicle tax credits.

This section highlights recent state policies dedicated funding to or indicated funding priorities
for local energy resilience projects. Section 2.5 identifies areas where Lane County might
expect funding or project opportunities in the near future.
