https://www.oregon.gov/deq/ghgp/pages/clean-energy-targets.aspx

 Policies Supporting Local Energy Resiliency Efforts

 57

Oregon HB 3409 (2023), a state law, was a major omnibus bill 1 that declared a state climate
emergency and introduced several new policies and programs related to climate, energy, and
the environment. The bill proposes resilience hubs and directs the Oregon Department of
Human Services (DHS) to provide financial and other support for development of these hubs in
Oregon. The bill requires ODOE to “reduce […] barriers to home energy efficiency and
resilience,” and otherwise states that resilience can be a benefit to communities. 2

Oregon HB 3630 (2023), a state law, directed the ODOE to expand energy planning and
customer support through new programs, grants, and a centralized information portal. 3 It
required ODOE to develop and periodically update a comprehensive statewide energy strategy.
HB3630 also authorized counties to develop energy resilience plans and incorporate them into
their natural hazard mitigation plans, while requiring public utilities to use reasonable efforts to
provide counties with requested energy-infrastructure information. This bill also established an
assistance program for energy projects in environmental justice communities (implemented by
ODOE as the Community Navigator Program 4) and authorized a grant program to help counties
cover the cost of developing qualifying energy resilience plans; nineteen counties applied to
participate in the program providing up to $50,000 per county, with plans due by June 30,
2026. 5 In addition, HB 3630 established programs supporting home energy-efficiency upgrades
and electrification rebates (HOMES and HEAR) and directed ODOE to create a one-stop
resource to help Oregonians navigate incentives, technical assistance, contractors, and
financing, now provided through the Hub for Incentive Programs + Projects in Oregon (HIPPO). 6

The Oregon Energy Strategy (2025) was developed by ODOE as directed in HB 3630 and
was submitted to the Governor and Legislature in November 2025. The Strategy outlines a
comprehensive plan for achieving a clean, reliable, and affordable energy future in Oregon
across different sectors and pathways. 7 The Strategy emphasizes strengthening resilience
across all levels of the energy system and notes that several types of targeted investments—
including grid hardening and modernization, batteries and other distributed resources, and
weatherization—can enhance resilience. The Strategy does not establish or mandate any
specific measures for Lane County; however, it provides information on the State’s strategic
direction and possible future opportunities. We discuss potential opportunities for Lane County
in alignment with The Strategy in Section 2.5.

Governor Kotek’s Executive Order 25-29 Reducing Greenhouse Gas Emissions and
Advancing Oregon’s Clean Energy Future (2025) furthered Oregon’s commitment to reduce
emissions and transition to a clean energy economy. 8 The Order emphasizes implementation of

 Policies Supporting Local Energy Resiliency Efforts

the Oregon Energy Strategy’s five least-cost pathways while prioritizing affordable, reliable
clean energy and resilience. Key provisions include advancing energy efficiency, facilitating
renewable energy project development, growing a resilient clean energy economy, and
improving accountability and tribal engagement.

2.5 Opportunities for Lane County Communities to Follow State-
 Level Strategies
Lane County has several opportunities to pursue planning and policy development that align
with state-level strategy. Chapter 9 reviews existing energy and resilience plans for the county
and localities. The current state level strategies can be used as a guide to update local and
county strategies. Major areas of current state-level strategy that could align with county of city
action are outlined below:

Investments in renewable energy development, demand management, and infrastructure.
Several state-level strategies, including the Oregon Energy Strategy and Executive Order 25-
29, promote investments in renewables to meet growing electricity demand and electrification
goals. State-level strategies also underscore that generation expansion requires investment in
transmission and distribution system infrastructure to expand network capacity and optimize
existing capacity. Demand flexibility is highlighted in the Oregon Energy Strategy as a way to
increase reliability, support renewable energy procurement, and potentially lessen
environmental and cost burdens. Specific gaps and opportunities relevant to this area of
potential state support are discussed in Sections 4.2, 4.3, and 4.4, and Chapter 7.

Strengthening system resilience through targeted investments, including backup power
options for critical infrastructure (system hardening). Critical infrastructure can benefit from
mobile sources of firm, dispatchable power, like energy storage, during outages. A better
understanding of the potential impacts of grid outages or other adverse events on critical
infrastructure and the role of different backup power arrangements could help inform county risk
mitigation. Access to affordable, secure, or renewable backup power options also dovetails with
the Oregon Governor’s Resiliency 2025 Vision published in 2018, which focuses on a critical
energy infrastructure hub to “prevent and mitigate catastrophic failure and ensure fuel supplies
and alternate energy sources are available to responders and the public.” 1 Specific gaps and
opportunities relevant to this area of potential state support are discussed across Chapter 4,
Chapter 5, and Chapter 6, and Section 8.3.

Expansion and enhancement of electric vehicle charging infrastructure. The Oregon
Department of Transportation Climate Action Plan notes that expanding electric vehicle
infrastructure along major thoroughfares could align with growing county- and state-level electric
vehicle adoption and meet public demand for charging stations. Growing electric vehicle
adoption can support state climate and decarbonization goals if managed in a way that
prioritizes renewable electricity sources and is integrated with demand-side resource planning. 2
Adoption of zero- and low-emission vehicles could alleviate impacts of ambient air pollution.
One of the main sources of funding for charging infrastructure in Oregon comes under the
National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure (NEVI) program, which was established under the 2021

1 State Library of Oregon, “Oregon Energy Strategy,” Oct. 2018.

https://digitalcollections.library.oregon.gov/nodes/view/121526
2 Massachusetts Institute of Technology, “Are electric vehicles definitely better for the climate than gas-

powered cars?” Oct. 2022. https://climate.mit.edu/ask-mit/are-electric-vehicles-definitely-better-climate-
gas-powered-cars

 Opportunities for Lane County Communities to Follow State-Level Strategies

 59

Bipartisan Infrastructure Law and provides Oregon with $52 million over five years for
implementing charging infrastructure. Oregon has completed one round of funding solicitations
for NEVI and plans to roll out projects along several main roads or highways including I-5 and
US101 in Lane County 1; the second solicitation phase is currently under way. Electrification of
other transportation infrastructure, such as public bus transportation, has been identified in
Executive Order 25-29 and could complement efforts to expand charging infrastructure. 2 While
enabling electric vehicle adoption is a state strategy, Section 4.2.1 describes that most
localities have opted instead to focus on conservation by supporting bikeable and pedestrian-
safe city planning. The supply chain for critical minerals needed to make electric vehicle
batteries is an area of major concern both in terms of environmental impacts, energy security,
and human rights. 3 Specific gaps and opportunities relevant to this area of potential state
support are discussed in Section 4.2.

 Figure 31: Electric Vehicles Charging in Downtown Portland 4

Expanding access to residential energy efficiency and weatherization services and
programs. Oregon has recently strengthened the policy foundation for energy efficiency and
weatherization through HB 3409, 5the Oregon Energy Strategy, and Executive Order 23-04, 6

 Opportunities for Lane County Communities to Follow State-Level Strategies

 60

which directs state agencies to accelerate building decarbonization and reduce energy burden
for low-income households. Building on these directives, Lane County could explore targeted or
expanded retrofit incentives for income-qualified residents, partnerships with community action
agencies to scale weatherization outreach, and local utility demand-side management funding
to subsidize insulation, air-sealing, and heat-pump installations. Specific gaps and opportunities
relevant to this area of potential state support are discussed in Section 4.3.

Job creation and workforce development for energy transitions. The Oregon Energy
Strategy is focused on pairing state energy policy objectives with economic and workforce
development goals. It highlights the state’s potential to pilot and deploy emerging technologies
and strengthen its leadership in the energy sector. Executive Order 20-04 similarly stresses
that clean energy research and development is linked to broader state competitiveness by
acting as a potential catalyst for attracting investments. The Oregon Department of
Transportation Climate Action Plan and Climate Protection Program also note that
expansion of clean energy infrastructure can support green job creation and encourage
workforce development. Existing workforce-related efforts in cities in Lane County are
summarized in Chapter 9.

Exploring alternative fuels for hard-to-electrify sectors. The Oregon Energy Strategy
positions alternative fuels—including those with lower lifecycle greenhouse gas emissions such
as biofuels and synthetic or electricity-based fuels (“e-fuels”)—as a necessary part of an energy
procurement strategy to meet state decarbonization goals in sectors that are challenging to
electrify. Alternative fuel development also aligns with the Oregon Clean Fuels Program,
which requires fuel suppliers to reduce the carbon intensity of fuels. Executive Order 25-29
specifically directs Oregon’s Department of Environmental Quality and the Environmental
Quality Commission to pursue rulemakings that strengthen the Clean Fuels Program, such as
strengthening carbon-intensity reduction requirements for certain transportation fuels. Lane
County’s agricultural and forestry industries could have an important role to play in the state’s
alternative fuels strategy, such as supplying waste for biogas and woody biomass for advanced
biofuels, pursuing or incentivizing farming techniques that lower the carbon intensity of crops,
and sequestering carbon through forest preservation or planting trees. This document does not
discuss alternative fuels, with the exception of biomass energy potential in Section 7.3.

Part II: Lane County Utilities Preface
The job of electric utilities is to maintain balance: the balance of electricity load and generation
to keep the grid working, and the balance of costs and revenues to keep their organization
working (Figure 32). They maintain these balances while working within the constraints of
regulations (Chapter 2), the broader regional grid system (Chapter 3), customer expectations
(Chapter 4), environmental realities (Chapter 5), and available technologies (Chapter 6).

 Figure 32: The Balancing Act of Utilities

The balancing of load and generation happens across a variety of timescales (Figure 33). At
every instant, load and generation need to match for the grid to continue to function. Every time
you turn a light on, the load on the grid increases by a tiny amount. It does so too quickly for any
person to “turn on” the equivalent amount of generation. This sort of instantaneous balancing is
done automatically by the technologies that make up the grid 1. For larger fluctuations over
longer periods of time, on the order of hours to days, grid operators are responsible for
maintaining the balance of generation and load. They do so by “dispatching”—turning on/off—
generation resources, controlling loads, purchasing electricity on the market, or implementing
other management strategies. On even longer timescales, from weeks and months to years, a
larger group of people is responsible for maintaining balance. Planners and analysts use historic
load information, weather forecasts, system information, and other resources to predict loads,

1 To learn more about this instantaneous balancing, see resources on electric grid inertia, synchronous

generators, and automatic grid controls, such as:
• Denholm, Paul, Trieu Mai, Rick Wallace Kenyon, Ben Kroposki, and Mark O’Malley. 2020.
• Inertia and the Power Grid: A Guide Without the Spin. Golden, CO: National Renewable
• Energy Laboratory. NREL/TP-6120-73856. https://www.nlr.gov/docs/fy20osti/73856.pdf.

 Part II: Lane County Utilities Preface

 72

inform operators, and make decisions about how to balance generation and load within the
constraints of the grid equipment. Regional planning groups that include utilities, independent
power producers, state officials, and others are responsible for ensuring that enough generation
and transmission resources are available to meet electricity demand. Having enough generation
to meet estimated is called resource adequacy (see Section 3.2).

 Figure 33: Ways of Balancing Load and Generation on Different Timescales

Chapters in Part II of this Handbook are organized by the external factors that utilities face,
including regional dynamics, customer expectations, hazards, and available technologies. For
each factor, we discuss the impacts on the utility and what utilities can do to manage those
impacts while still balancing both load and generation, and costs and revenues.

 Part II: Lane County Utilities Preface

 73

 Chapter 3
  Regional Dynamics Impacting Lane
 County Utilities
 Haley Ross and Ian Hermanson

Lane County utilities face challenges that stem from a regional system of technologies,
actors, and policies as described in Chapter 1 and Chapter 2. In this section, we discuss
how Lane County utilities deal with four of those challenges: Western electricity market
restructuring, regional resource adequacy, local and regional operational adequacy, and
increasing costs. For Lane County, resource adequacy may eventually pose a threat to
reliability, but is not one that we have seen to-date. Reliability risks due to lack of
transmission redundancy are present in some parts of the county. The likely sources of
distribution reliability challenges include heavily vegetated terrain and hard-to-reach
service lines.

3.1 Power Market Restructuring
California ISO (CAISO), a California-based independent system operator (ISO), and Southwest
Power Pool (SPP), a regional transmission operator (RTO) based in the central United States,
operate regional real-time and day-ahead energy markets that compete for voluntary
membership from large utilities and balancing authorities. Since 2014, BPA participated in
CAISO’s Western Energy Imbalance Market (WEIM), a real-time energy market representing
nearly 80% of the Western Interconnection’s energy demand. 1 SPP launched a comparable
real-time market in 2021, called the Western Energy Imbalance Service (WEIS) market. In the
early 2020s, both CAISO and SPP began developing day-ahead market expansions to their
respective real-time markets. CAISO launched the Extended Day-Ahead Market (EDAM) on
May 1st, 2026, while SPP plans to launch an alternative day-ahead market called Markets+ in
2027. After a lengthy decision process, the Bonneville Power Administration (BPA) announced
in 2025 that it would leave WEIM to join the new SPP Markets+ day-ahead market. 2 Many large
Western investor-owned utilities (IOUs), including Pacific Power and Portland General Electric
(PGE), chose to join CAISO’s day-ahead market. While a Pacific Northwest power market shift
will have regional impacts, the specific effects remain uncertain.

1 California ISO, “ISO Western Energy Imbalance Market Benefits Report Q3 2025,” Oct. 2025.

https://www.westernenergymarkets.com/documents/iso-western-energy-imbalance-market-benefits-
report-q3-2025.pdf
2 OregonLive, “BPA opts to join controversial energy market despite criticism that it will raise rates,” May

2025. https://www.oregonlive.com/environment/2025/05/bpa-opts-to-join-controversial-energy-market-
despite-criticism-that-it-will-raise-rates.html

 74

Under BPA’s founding statute, BPA will continue to prioritize sales to Pacific Northwest
consumer-owned utilities (COUs) through long-term contracts, including Lane County utilities.
But the new market split means many large utilities neighboring BPA territory will now operate in
a different market than the BPA.

Additionally, a different and expanded set of customers outside BPA’s territory—primarily
utilities operating within SPP’s Markets+ footprint—may gain streamlined market access to BPA
hydropower. At the same time, CAISO-based utilities will face new costs and market
complexities when buying BPA power.

The BPA’s move could impact Lane County utilities by influencing regional wholesale electricity
costs. Multiple analyses, including a BPA commissioned study, found that BPA leaving CAISO
to join SPP could raise costs for Pacific Northwest ratepayers. 1,2 Some Oregon and Washington
legislators, public utility commissioners, and major IOUs (including Pacific Power) are
concerned BPA’s decision could impact Western Interconnection grid reliability and complicate
coordination efforts (including making it hard to address transmission constraints). 3 BPA officials
argue that because CAISO is partially governed by the California Legislature, California has
excessive governmental control over western energy exchange. SPP is governed by a board of
power producers and utilities.3 Emerald People’s Utility District (EPUD) has expressed support
for BPA’s decision, citing concerns with organized markets. 4

SPP Markets+ will be rolled out in two waves, one that will go live in 2027, and the second in
2028. BPA expects to begin operating within SPP during the second wave in October 2028. 5
Because the consequences of BPA’s decision are not fully understood, the resulting regional
long-term uncertainty could complicate decision-making for small utilities that rely on BPA
power, including which, if any, day-ahead market to participate in.

3.2 Resource Adequacy
The North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) defines resource adequacy as
having sufficient generating and transmission facilities to “provide customers with a continuous
supply of electricity at the proper voltage and frequency, virtually all of the time” 6 If generation
and transmission capacity are insufficient to meet demand, load must decrease to keep grid
physics intact and electricity flowing to end users. If the Pacific Northwest were to face
generation or transmission capacity shortages, utilities could be responsible for facilitating the

1 OregonLive, ibid.
2 Washington State Standard, “BPA announces intention to split with California and join new Southwest

energy market,” Mar. 2025. https://washingtonstatestandard.com/2025/03/10/bpa-announces-intention-to-
split-with-california-and-join-new-southwest-energy-market/
3 OregonLive, “BPA plan to leave Western energy market sparks worries of rate increases,” Mar. 2025.

https://www.oregonlive.com/pacific-northwest-news/2025/03/bpa-plan-to-leave-western-energy-market-
sparks-worries-of-rate-increases.html

energy market,” Mar. 2025. https://washingtonstatestandard.com/2025/03/10/bpa-announces-intention-to-
split-with-california-and-join-new-southwest-energy-market/

 Resource Adequacy

 75

necessary decrease in load. During a minor shortage, utilities would likely call for residential
customers to conserve energy and for industrial customers to go offline (see Section 4.3 for
more on demand response). During a major shortage, utilities may need to institute rolling
blackouts. A rolling blackout, also known as rotational load shedding, is when a utility cuts
service to different areas, one or a few at a time, to reduce load. Utilities will periodically change
which areas area shut off to reduce the outage duration for any given customer.

3.3 Studies of Resource Adequacy in the Pacific Northwest
This section reviews four major investigations into Pacific Northwest resource adequacy and
find that while the Pacific Northwest is likely to be resource adequate in the next 1-3 years,
depending on both the rate of generation resource additions and load growth—both of which are
highly uncertain—there could be resource adequacy challenges in the future. We then discuss
how Lane County utilities could prepare and respond to a potential resource inadequacy event.

Understanding if a region is and will remain resource adequate requires two main pieces of
data:
1. How much load there is and will be.
2. How much generating capacity there is and will be.

For this reason, all resource adequacy studies try to predict a wide range of scenarios to
characterize both load and generation over the study’s time horizon. The different methods and
assumptions used to make these predictions naturally lead to different results. However, overall
trends for the Pacific Northwest are consistent: increasing uncertainty in load growth
projections and the rate of new generation additions could increase risk of resource
adequacy shortfalls over time.

Resource adequacy studies identify when and how much additional generation or transmission
capacity that bulk system planners must bring online to support long-term grid reliability. These
studies are a preliminary assessment of the system’s future and a starting point in the planning
process. Other studies, such as capacity expansion planning, are used to determine the location
or type of generation facilities needed to meet future demand.

Load and generation predictions are challenging because both vary moment to moment,
day to day, month to month, and season to season. On cold, dark winter mornings, people
typically turn up their electric furnaces, heat pumps, water heaters, and lights, causing demand
to be higher than it would be later in the day when people are at work, later in the winter when
the sun rises earlier, later in the year on mild summer mornings when the house can be
passively heated and lit. Hydropower generation peaks in spring when snowmelt fills reservoirs
and declines in fall after summer demand depletes reservoirs. Extreme summer heat can
decrease the power generated by thermal resources like natural gas. 1 Solar and wind
generation also vary seasonally. Wind generation, for example, peaks during the blustery spring
and fall seasons, and short and cloudy winter days reduce solar output.

1 Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, “Extreme Heat, Hurricanes, Wildfires: How Summers’ Extremes

Disrupt the Power Grid,” Jul. 2024.
https://www.pnnl.gov/news-media/extreme-heat-hurricanes-wildfires-how-summers-extremes-disrupt-
power-grid

 76

 In addition to quantifying load and generation, resource adequacy studies define the time
 period, assume weather conditions, and predict the type and number of new generation
 sources. All these assumptions add uncertainty. Many of the studies reviewed in this section
 include a low-probability, worst-case scenario.
 We examined study results from several Pacific Northwest grid planning entities. Study
 footprints and methodologies varied. Key study factors are summarized in Table 10.

 What is an average megawatt (aMW)?
An aMW represents the energy deficit over a year (in megawatt-hours [MWh])
divided by the number of hours in a year (8,760) to give a power rating to the
deficit. One aMW is equivalent to one million watts being consumed 24 hours a
day for a year.

3.3.1 Resource Adequacy Studies Key Takeaways
Together, these four reports characterize a Pacific Northwest that:
1. Can easily balance load and generation in the near term (1–3 years) but faces significant
 uncertainty in the ability to maintain balance in the future.
2. Has sufficient plans to expand generation resources to meet forecasted demand.
 a. If new generation additions are behind schedule challenges will likely arise. Generation
 additions can be hindered by supply chain issues, challenges getting permission to
 connect to the grid (referred to as the interconnection queue), siting concerns, and
 increases in development costs (see Section 4.4)
3. Faces highly uncertain load growth that will depend on factors like data center development,
 electric vehicle adoption, building electrification, and continued economic growth.
 a. If load growth (discussed further in Section 4.2 for Lane County) is faster than
 forecasted, future resource adequacy challenges are more likely.

Reporting from Oregon Public Broadcasting in May 2025 highlighted that BPA’s interconnection
queue took 15 years for new generation projects to get approval to interconnect. 1 In 2024 the
BPA has implemented changes to their study process to reduce the backlog by studying
projects in clusters instead of individually, which will help mitigate study delays. 2

Many entities in the Pacific Northwest—from researchers to state governments—are exploring
novel solutions to combat resource adequacy challenges. For example, a recent study by
Sylvan Energy suggested that requiring new large loads to curtail their demand could protect
other customers from major impacts of generation supply shortages. 3 Washington state is
exploring strategies such as incentivizing utilities to develop new transmission, exploring
microgrids and other distributed energy sources that can bypass the interconnection queue, and
attempting to streamline state permitting for new energy projects. 4 Executive Order 25-29
Reducing Greenhouse Gas Emissions and Advancing Oregon’s Clean Energy Future,
signed by Governor Tina Kotek in 2025, seeks to address siting concerns in Oregon (see
Chapter 2).

3.3.2 How Being Resource Inadequate Could Impact Lane County
 Utilities
While resource adequacy is generally a planning metric, in real terms, being resource
inadequate can translate into operational challenges, some of which can impact local utilities

1 Oregon Public Broadcasting, “How the Pacific Northwest's dream of green energy fell apart,” May 2025.

3.3.3 Other Resource Adequacy Efforts
The Western Power Pool (WPP) is launching the Western Resource Adequacy Program
(WRAP), which establishes shared standards for capacity planning and supply sufficiency
across participating utilities. WRAP is the first regional program in the west focused on reliability
planning and compliance. 2 WRAP aims to improve understanding of regional resource needs
and supply and enable members to collaborate to efficiently use current and future resources.
WRAP has two parts, a near term (7 month) forecast, and an operational component that allows
members to pool and share resources. 3 Financial penalties will apply if a member fails to meet
contract obligations. WRAP is not an organized market and is intended to be compatible with
both CAISO- and SPP-operated markets. 4 Full WRAP implementation has faced delays as
several balancing authorities and utilities have withdrawn from voluntary participation. BPA
remains a participant, while utilities such as Pacific Power and EWEB exited over concerns
around the timing of financial risks such as BPA contracts and the development of regional day-
ahead markets. 5

3.4 Operational Reliability
Operational reliability refers to the grid’s ability to withstand sudden disturbances such as
short circuits or acute extreme weather events, 6 and depends on factors including the design
and implementation of power system protection schemes, class and placement of infrastructure
(poles, lines, substations, etc.), and redundancy. This section describes available levels of
redundancy at the transmission level and explores public reliability data at the distribution level
for Lane County.

customers can be found in Section 4.4.1.

https://www.ferc.gov/OPP/western-markets-explainer
3 Western Power Pool, “WPP Notice to WRAP Resource Adequacy Participants Committee” Oct. 2025

3.4.1 Transmission System Reliability
While most power outages originate on the distribution system, 1 transmission infrastructure also
plays a role in system reliability. Though U.S. distribution and transmission grid designs differ,
distribution systems are often designed radially, with power lines extending outward from a
central node. Transmission systems are normally designed as a mesh network, where multiple
routes connect locations, however, occasionally radial branches occur. The redundancy of
mesh networks means that if one route is interrupted, power can continue to flow to the same
area via an alternate path. Using this general understanding, we can recognize where Lane
County has more resilient infrastructure, and where there may be vulnerabilities.

Mapping Lane County transmission line voltages and routes (Figure 34) provides insight into
local grid capacity and where power interruptions would have the greatest impact. In addition,
understanding the operator and service areas (Figure 35) of each transmission line can reveal if
the line serves a local or regional purpose.

1 National Laboratory of the Rockies, “Top 10 Things To Know About Power Grid Reliability,” Jan. 2024.

https://www.nlr.gov/news/detail/program/2024/top-10-things-to-know-about-power-grid-reliability

 88

 Figure 34: Map of Transmission Infrastructure for Lane County 1

1 Data (as of March 2026) from Open Infrastructure Map, https://openinframap.org/#2/26/12

 89

Figure 34 shows five high-voltage transmission lines enter Lane County from the north. Two of
these transmission lines are double circuit—an infrastructure configuration in which two
separate lines share each tower—providing seven total paths for power to flow into the county
from the north. To the south, transmission lines cross the Lane County boundary in four places.
Two of these lines are double circuit, totaling six paths for power to flow into Lane County from
the south.

While a similar number of transmission routes serve Lane County from north and south, the
voltage of these lines varies. Higher voltage lines generally carry more power and over longer
distances due to reduced heat losses. Voltage classes of transmission lines are defined in
kilovolts (kV), where a higher number represents a higher voltage and higher power transfer
capacity. From the north, two lines are 500 kV, four are 200–299 kV, and one is 100–199 kV.
From the south, only one line is 500 kV, three are 200–299kV, and two are 100–199 kV.
Overall, Lane County has greater power transfer capacity from the north than from the south,
indicating that Lane County likely gets a higher fraction of its energy from generation sources to
the north.

Eugene and Springfield are served by a dense mesh of transmission infrastructure, and
Florence and Cottage Grove are served by multiple transmission routes. The city of Oakridge,
communities in the McKenzie River Valley, and communities around Veneta and Elmira are
served by only one 115 kV transmission line, increasing likelihood of disruption. Oakridge and
the McKenzie River Valley are served by long-distance radial transmission lines and face the
highest disruption risk because they cannot benefit from the redundancy of the mesh-network.

Figure 35 labels Lane County transmission line operators. Cross-referencing Figure 34 with
Figure 35 shows that BPA operates most of the lines that cross Lane County’s boarders. Pacific
Power only operates three transmission lines: a 200–299 kV line exiting to the north, a >400 kV
line exiting to the south, and a 200–299 kV line exiting to the south.

Within the county, the operators of different lines are more diverse, reflecting the different
utilities’ needs to serve their local customers. Relatively inexpensive, low-voltage transmission
lines are typically used to carry power over short distances to smaller loads. Distribution utilities,
use low-voltage transmission lines to transfer power from major interregional substations to
customers with minimal power loss. All the lines that do not cross the county border are
operated by local distribution utilities, are 100–199 kV or 200–299 kV, travel short to moderate
distances, and serve small to medium local loads.

 90

 Figure 35: Map of Transmission Line Operators in Lane County 1 (as of March 2026)

1 Data (as of March 2026) from Open Infrastructure Map, https://openinframap.org/#2/26/12

 91

3.4.2 Distribution System Reliability
Reliability of the distribution system is generally measured through three major metrics. Those
are:
 The System Average Interruption Duration Index (SAIDI) measures the average minutes of
 interruption experienced by each customer
 The System Average Interruption Frequency Index (SAIFI) measures the average number
 of interruptions experienced by each customer.
 The Customer Average Interruption Duration Index (CAIDI) measures the average
 interruption (time to restoration) length experienced by the average customer, and is
 calculated by dividing the SAIDI score by the SAIFI score. 1

For all three metrics, lower numbers represent better performance.

Investor-owned utilities must submit—and many others voluntarily submit—reliability data to the
U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA) through an annual survey called Form EIA-
861. These results are public. Small utilities may use a short version of the form, called EIA-
861s, that is submitted less frequently and includes less reliability data. Since Lane County’s
small COUs submit Form EIA-861s data, they are not required to submit every year, resulting in
years without reported results.

Figure 36, Figure 37, and Figure 38 plot distribution reliability data for six Lane County utilities
submitted to the EIA between 2013 and 2024. Data for utilities with service territories beyond
Lane County, like Pacific Power, represents results for their entire service area, not just Lane
County. EIA national average data is only available from 2014 through 2024. Utility data in
Figure 36, Figure 37, and Figure 38 excludes outages that occurred during major event days 2,
such as severe weather, to provide a more accurate understanding of day-to-day reliability. But
utilities in this dataset use different metrics to identify major event days, which means results
may not be a true apples-to-apples comparison of performance.

1 U.S. Energy Information Administration, “Electric Power Annual – Table EPA 11.1 (Electric Power

Industry Summary Statistics),” 2025.
https://www.eia.gov/electricity/annual/html/epa_11_01.html
2 Oregon has formally adopted the definition of major event days formally laid out in IEEE 1366-2012,

which defines a major event day as one where the SAIDI exceeds a statistical threshold value called
TMED, which is calculated based on a logarithmic normalization of historic SAIDI values. Because of this,
some severe weather events may trigger a major event day, while others may not depending on their
impact on customer outages, via Oregon Secretary of State, “Public Utility Commission,”
https://secure.sos.state.or.us/oard/viewSingleRule.action?ruleVrsnRsn=221205, Electrical and
Electronics Engineers Standards Association, “IEEE Guide for Electric Power Distribution Reliability
Indices,” 2012. https://standards.ieee.org/ieee/1366/4602/

 92

Figure 36: Annual Lane County SAIDI Data Reported to EIA vs National Average

Figure 37: Annual Lane County SAIFI Data Reported to EIA vs National Average

 93

 Figure 38: Annual Lane County CAIDI Data Reported to EIA vs National Average

Figure 36, Figure 37, and Figure 38 show that EWEB, Pacific Power, and Springfield Utility
Board (SUB) have historically performed below or near the national average for all three
reliability metrics. These three utilities experience similar outage numbers and durations of as
the national average, or they are performing better by having fewer outages of shorter durations
both overall and on a per customer basis than the national average. While EPUD data was only
available from 2013 and CAIDI data is missing, the available data shows that EPUD performed
near the national average.

Data for Blachly-Lane is only available for 2019, and shows that while SAIFI was near average,
SAIDI and CAIDI were significantly above. This could indicate that in 2019 Blachly-Lane had an
average number of outages but took more time than average to restore power.

Lane Electric performed consistently above the national average for all three metrics during
available data years. This indicates Lane Electric customers experienced more outages that
lasted longer on a system and a per-customer basis than the average U.S. utility. While we do
not have enough information to determine exactly why Lane Electric is an outlier, Lane Electric’s
reliability challenges may be partially related to transmission supply. Figure 39, a map of Lane
County’s transmission infrastructure overlayed with Lane County utility service territories, shows
that the eastern half of Lane County, primarily Lane Electric service territory, is served by two
long radial transmission lines. Because there is no transmission redundancy, an outage on
either transmission line will interrupt power for all the customers.

 94

Because Lane Electric is a small, rural utility, they maintain a lot of equipment to serve relatively
few customers, with approximately 9 customers connected per mile of infrastructure. For
comparison, EWEB has approximately 76 customers/mile, and SUB has 44 according to
publicly reported values of miles of infrastructure and numbers of customers. Only Blachly-Lane
has a similar rural system to Lane Electric’s with about 4 customers/mile. One outage will more
heavily impact their customer averages than a less rural utility. Lane Electric’s service territory is
also spread out compared to others in the county, which can lengthen response times. Their
equipment runs through hard-to-access topography and is heavily tree covered which may
result in more tree- and animal-caused outages that are hard to restore.

 95

Figure 39: Map of Utility Service Territories and Transmission Infrastructure for Lane County 1,2

3.5 Regional and National Rate Drivers
While Lane County utility rates are ultimately set by the local distribution utility, they are
impacted by several forces beyond local control. The average U.S. electricity rate has steadily

1 Data (as of March 2026) from Oregon Department of Energy,

https://www.oregon.gov/energy/Pages/default.aspx
2 Data (as of March 2026) from Open Infrastructure Map, https://openinframap.org/#2/26/12

 Regional and National Rate Drivers

 96

increased since 2020. 1,2 While Oregon’s average electricity rate is below the national average
(see Section 3.5.2), the state has experienced a significant (26.2%) rate increase over the last
four years—a similar rate of change to the 26.7% national rate increase over the same period. 3
Customers of the large IOUs, PGE and Pacific Power, saw typical residential customer average
bills increase by 50–60% between 2020 and 2024. Both PGE and Pacific Power are proposing
further rate increases to the Oregon PUC. The Oregon Department of Energy identifies three
primary drivers of rising consumer rates, which often interact: ongoing infrastructure
investments compounded by inflation, rising power costs, and increased costs to mitigate and
respond to wildfires and extreme weather.

3.5.1 Ongoing Infrastructure Investments Compounded by Inflation
Electric utilities must constantly invest in grid infrastructure to replace aging equipment, maintain
reliability, and support system resilience. Routine equipment and material costs have grown due
to inflation and supply chain disruptions. The Oregon DOE reports that since 2020, the cost of
power poles increased 35%, overhead conductors 45%, underground power cables 79%, and
single-phase transformers by nearly 200%. Longer lead times and supply delays further
increase project costs. Utilities must increase customer rates to recover these investment costs.
Annual nominal and inflation-adjusted average electricity prices in the United States are shown
in Figure 40: U.S. Average Electricity Prices, 2010-2024. While national-average retail
electricity prices have increased in nominal terms, real prices (adjusted to account for inflation)
have generally decreased 4.

3.5.2 Rising power costs
Utility power costs include the price of electricity and the cost of fuel (such as natural gas) for
utility-owned power plants. Despite recent BPA rate increases (see Section 1.5.1), federal
hydropower is less expensive than other wholesale power sources. Northwest IOUs rely less on
BPA contracts and more on short-term wholesale purchases and natural gas power plants,
increasing their exposure to wholesale market volatility and fluctuating power costs. 1
International changes in energy and fuel prices and unexpected events such as pandemic-
related supply disruptions or international market uncertainty can significantly impact wholesale
power prices and increase customer energy rates.1 Oregon COU rate increases are smaller
than IOU increases because they rely primarily on relatively low-cost BPA hydroelectric power.

3.5.3 Increased costs to mitigate and respond to wildfires and extreme
 weather
Wildfires and extreme weather events like droughts, floods, and heatwaves are increasing in
frequency and severity in the Pacific Northwest. 2 Infrastructure upgrades for mitigating natural
disaster risk and equipment and labor expenditures for recovering from natural disasters add
significant cost pressures on Oregon utilities. 3 Utilities must invest in wildfire risk and extreme
weather mitigation, infrastructure hardening, and respond to and recover from damage caused
by extreme events, as well as respond to regulatory measures, policies, and insurance
requirements. Wildfires, ice storms, and other natural hazards have also contributed to rising
insurance costs, as utilities face greater financial liability. Lane County Emergency Management
staff estimated that the 2024 ice storm cost the county’s electric utilities almost $33 million. We
discuss the impacts of natural hazards further in Chapter 5.

 Chapter 4
Expectations of Lane County Utilities
 Ali Trueworthy

Lane County utilities strive to meet evolving customer and stakeholder expectations
while managing the constraints of the regional grid system. These expectations include,
but are not limited to, expectations of affordable service, ability to serve changing loads,
efficiency and conservation, and reliability and resilience. Each of these expectations
adds a unique challenge to the utility’s ability to balance costs and revenues and/or load
and generation. The challenges they present often overlap. In this section, we present a
variety of utility best practices related to maintaining the balance costs and revenues
and/or load and generation while meeting customer and stakeholder expectations. We
document some of the current practices implemented in Lane County and identify areas
where there are opportunities or improvements.

4.1 Maintaining Affordable Services
Customers expect affordable services from their utilities. Affordability for an individual
customer depends on rates and rate structures, but also on their individual income (see
Section 8.1 for map of energy burden in Lane County). The income generated from customer
rates is the primary source of revenue for distribution utilities. As such, a utility, especially a
COU, strives to maintain affordability while also balancing costs.
Cost factors are only partially within the control of local utilities. Rates are driven by many
factors (discussed in Section 3.5) including the costs of maintaining infrastructure and
mitigating and responding to hazard events, especially in the context of inflation, emerging
technologies, extreme weather, and aging existing assets. In interviews with Lane County
utilities (see Appendix A– Utility Interviews), 50% of utility representatives expressed
uncertainty about the future existence of financial backstops like the Federal Emergency
Management Agency (FEMA) and other grant assistance. Without such backstops, the
financial challenges associated with natural hazard events would be compounded for Lane
County utilities. That said, there are steps that utilities can take to mitigate the impacts of
financial pressures and maintain affordable service.
4.1.1 Best Practices for Maintaining Affordable Service
The American Public Power Association provides a convenient checklist for utilities striving to
maintain operational excellence and financial health (reproduced on the next page). These
practices happen within the utility, though may be tracked by the governing board or council
members. The American Public Power Association divides best practices into three categories,
shown in Figure 41:
1. Focus on key targets and financial areas
2. Establish fair and equitable rate structures
3. Use regular reporting to maintain transparency.

 99

 Focus on Key Targets and Financial Areas

 • Encourage employees and decision makers to learn the purpose of the balance sheet
 and income statement and the important information they convey.
 • Identify key financial targets and how they work together to support short- and long-term
 financial goals.
 • Review metrics that include minimum cash reserve requirements, rate of return, debt
 coverage ratio, debt-to-equity ratio, and age of system.
 • Adopt sound financial policies that support the key financial targets and take action
 when targets are not met.
 • Conduct periodic reviews of industry benchmarks to help ensure operational financial
 efficiencies. Refer to Financial and Operating Rations of Public Power Utilities as a guide.
 • Conduct annual assessment of capital needs and update strategic capital plan
 accordingly.
 • Research and evaluate state and federal funding opportunites that support capital
 goals.
 • Ensure the budget supports the necessary revenues to achieve financial goals and
 capital reinvestment.
 • Ensure any payments in lieu of taxes (aka, PILOT or transfers to the city) are based on a
 sound, defendable formula.

 Establish Fair and Equitable Rate Structures

 • Ensure the necessary expertise is available to help establish fair and equitable rates.
 • Ensure rate structures include components for proper fixed and variable cost recovery
 such as customer (fixed), demand, and kWh (energy) charges, as well as a power cost
 adjustment (PCA).
 • Implement continuous education and improvement of rate structures to account for
 innovative rate design as the industry changes.
 • Implement technologies like AMI to provide data and the means to develop more robust
 rate structures for fair cost recovery.
 • Develop detailed policies on customer connections (line extension, connection, and
 impact fees), additional large loads (e.g. electric vehicle charging infrastructure and data
 centers), and distributed generation.

 Use Regular Reporting to Maintain Transparency

 • Implement internal controls to ensure financial reporting is accurate with proper
 documentation and complies with applicable laws and regulations.
 • Ensure transparancy by making financial data available online and at public meetings,
 providing open and honest dialogue with residents to encourage trust.
 •Commit to timely financial reporting, such as providing monthly reports to decsion
 makers.
 •Conduct an annual audit, perfomed by an independent professional organization, at the
 end of each fiscal year.

  Figure 41: Best Practices for Maintaining Good Financial Health for Public Electric Utilities 1

1 Information from the American Public Power Association, reproduced verbatim.

 100

Focus on Key Targets and Financial Areas

A utility’s focus on key targets and financial areas includes foundational operational practices
like analyzing financial statements, budgeting, tracking key financial metrics, and ensuring
timely maintenance. 1 In addition, it includes more in-depth planning endeavors. These
endeavors help utilities identify feasible timelines for investment, maintenance, and adoption of
new technologies or strategies. Planning can reduce risk, minimize unplanned costs, and
support the prioritization of actions, ultimately helping the utility maintain affordable services.

Strategic Planning

Strategic plans provide the guiding strategy for subsequent planning or decision-making. Utility
strategic planning typically includes high-level goals, actions the utility will take to meet those
goals, and sometimes metrics for measuring progress toward goals. While utilities might do their
own strategic planning, cities or towns also engage in planning related to their energy systems. 2
We discuss city plans in Lane County and best practices for community strategic energy
planning in Sub-Section 9.1.1. When utilities and the municipalities they serve have strong
working relationships, they can align their strategic energy plans.

Capital Planning

Capital planning, also known as system upgrade planning or asset planning, is focused on
utility infrastructure and assets. A capital plan identifies what assets need replacement or
upgrades in the coming years. The plan usually prioritizes asset upgrades based on the
estimated probability and impact of failures, the estimated cost and impact of upgrading an
asset, and feedback from the board or customers. Utilities consider how their capital needs align
with available capital to determine how many projects can be undertaken and on what timeline.
If there are discrepancies between needed and available capital, the utility must explore other
options such as rate changes or grants. For COUs, the prioritization and approval of projects is
typically overseen by the board and therefore is available for public review. For IOUs, utilities
usually need to submit their capital plans to their Public Utilities Commission (PUC).

Integrated Resource Planning

An integrated resource plan is a roadmap for meeting forecasted electricity demand.
Historically, integrated resource plans have been focused on the bulk power system, but it is
becoming more common for distribution utilities with generation resources to create these
plans. 3 We discuss integrated resource plans, also sometimes called integrated distribution
plans, 4 in more detail in Section 4.2.

1J. Keen et al., “Distribution Capacity Expansion Planning: Current Practice, Opportunities, and Decision

Support,” National Laboratory of The Rockies, NREL/TP-6A40-83892, 1898008, MainId:84665, Nov.
2022. doi: 10.2172/1898008.
2 American Public Power Association, “Public Power Forward: A Guide to Utility Operational Excellence,”

2025. https://www.publicpower.org/resource/public-power-forward-guide-utility-operational-excellence
3 U.S. Department of Energy, “COMMUNITY GREENING: HOW TO DEVELOP A STRATEGIC ENERGY

PLAN,” Feb. 2010. https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy10osti/45652.pdf
4 Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, “Best Practices in Integrated Resource Planning: A guide for

planners developing the electricity resource mix of the future,” Nov. 2024.
https://emp.lbl.gov/publications/best-practices-integrated-resource

 101

IOUs in Oregon are required by the Oregon PUC to submit an integrated resource plan every
two years. 1 The process includes establishing and modeling feasible future scenarios for both
generation and load. DOE promotes best practices for integrated resource planning developed
by researchers and practitioners from Synapse Energy Economics Inc. and Lawerence Berkeley
National Lab. The 50 best practices span the categories of stakeholder engagement, resource
adequacy, developing model inputs, designing scenarios and sensitivities, running models,
evaluating portfolio results, communicating transparently to regulators and stakeholders, and
aligning the integrated resource planning process with other utility proceedings. 2

Establish Fair and Equitable Rate Structures

While the best practices in the category focus on key targets and financial areas relate to
keeping operational costs manageable, the best practices in the category establish fair and
equitable rate structures relate to keeping revenues adequate and acceptable. Practices
include rate design informed by data collection and policy setting for customer connections.

Rate Design

Rate design is an important part of maintaining fair and equitable rate structures. Sub-Section
1.5.2 describes the different types of rates and rate structures. Utilities implement different types
of charges (with fixed, energy, and demand charges being the most common) at different rates
or rate schedules that determine when (e.g., in the summer vs. winter) and to whom (e.g.,
commercial vs. residential users) the charges apply. Different rate structures can be used to
pursue differnt goals or incentivize different consumptive habits.

Generally, utilities can pursue fairness in rate design by making the charges to a customer
reflect the cost of serving that customer. Because utilities experience different costs, there is not
a rate structure that is universally fair. Some utilities might choose to vary a fixed charge based
on customer-specific factors like income or the number of families at a residence in an effort to
reduce the energy cost burden on vulnerable customers. Such programs are often mandated by
a government or board. Best practices say that the utilities should have the expertise available
to examine different rate structures, use data to explore options, and track innovations in the
industry. The American Public Power Association provides several rate design case studies
from COUs across the United States. 3

Ratepayers are the primary (often only) source of income for the utility, and rate structures are
the main lever that utilities have to influence customer use behavior. For that reason, the utility
rate data presented in Sub-Section 1.5.2 is a useful to examine Lane County utilities pursuit of
fairness in rates design (this section), rates for large new customers (see Sub-Section 4.1.2),
and rates encouraging energy efficiency and conservation (see Section 4.3).

Connection Policies

When it comes to distribution planning, utilities are faced with complex decisions about which
strategies to implement, where, and when. In addition, they face questions about who bears the

1 Oregon Public Utility Commission, “Energy – Planning”,

https://www.oregon.gov/puc/utilities/pages/energy-planning.aspx
2 U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Energy Efficiency and Renewable Energy, ibid.
3 American Public Power Association, “Public Power Rate Design Case Studies”,

https://www.publicpower.org/resource/public-power-rate-design-case-studies

 102

costs of interconnections and distribution upgrades. When a customer wants to connect to the
grid, or to increase their consumption in such a way that it would require distribution upgrades,
the utility may require that customer to bear a portion of the costs of those upgrades. This is
more often true for components that only serve the customer or the customer and a few others
(like a transformer or feeder), and less often true for major upgrades like replacing a line with
one of increased capacity.

Recently, the practice of passing upgrades costs on to customers has been called into question
for a variety of reasons, including the way that it could stifle electrification and/or equitable
adoption of electrified heating, cooling, or efficiency measure. Critics have also noted that the
practice can deter industry or businesses from locating in certain areas. Because the grid is a
system that so many people use and rely on, determining how to fairly distribute the costs of
maintaining and improving the grid is central to utility policy and rate setting.

Assistance, Rebate, and Monitoring Programs

Utilities can help their customers with their bills through income-based energy assistance
programs. There are national programs like the Low-Income Home Energy Assistance Program
(LIHEAP) 1 that utilities can help their customers find, but utilities may also run their own
programs. Some utilities set aside some of their operating budget to financially assist their low-
income customers, while others have programs that customers can donate to to help their
neighbors. Some utilities partner with local organizations to offer payment assistance. Rebate
programs for energy efficiency measures can help customers take the steps necessary to
reduce their home energy bills.

If utilities have AMI, utilities can use the data in their rate setting to understand the impacts of
certain measures on different customers and explore options for fairer rate setting.

Use Regular Reporting to Maintain Transparency

The final category of actions for maintaining affordable service and good financial health is use
regular reporting to maintain transparency. This category includes practices related to
documenting, reporting, and auditing financial metrics.

Budgets, Audits, Reports, and Newsletters

Reports can take many forms, but publicly available budgets, audits, reports, and newsletters
are some typical modes that utilities might use to communicate their financial situation. Budgets
are typically annual, but a utility may be required to give quarterly budget updates to their board.
Audits by outside organizations are also commonly annual and may be required by the board or
a PUC. Annual reports and/or newsletters are flexible ways of communicating with customers.

4.1.2 Approaches Applied in Lane County
This section presents the best practices implemented by Lane County utilities. The assessment
of utility practices is based on publicly available information only and does not judge the quality
or impact of the practices for each specific utility.

1 Low-Income Home Energy Assistance Program, “What is LIHEAP,” https://www.liheap.org/about

Establish Fair and Equitable Rate Structures

In 2025, the State of Oregon passed Oregon HB 3179 (2025), the FAIR Energy Act (Fairness
and Affordability in Residential Energy Regulation) which adds rate transparency
requirements for IOUs (see Sections 2.3 and 2.4). The policy does not apply to COUs, whose
community ownership and elected board members are in place to maintain acceptable rate
structures. The policy requires the Oregon PUC to consider the cumulative economic impact of
an electric or natural gas company’s proposed residential rate change. If deemed unfair, the
utility may be required to modify rate changes. Aspects of the law aim to limit how frequently
utilities can raise rates, prevent rate increases during high-demand months, and communicate
incoming rate changes. 1

Rate Design

Rate structures for COUs must be proposed to and approved by the utility’s board. The board
can direct the utility to consider alternate rate structures. Sub-Section 1.5.2 shows the
residential and commercial rate structures for each utility. In Lane County, all utilities have
experience with all three of the different rate types (fixed, energy, and demand). For residential
rates, all utilities implement a fixed charge, typically to distribute fixed operation costs to
customers, regardless of electricity usage. Demand charges in Lane County are reserved for
commercial customers, and sometimes (like for EPUD), only large commercial customers.
Section 4.3 discusses demand response programs, which may include demand charges.

Connection Policies

Within their customer policies, Lane County utilities outline costs incurred by new and
substantially growing customers. These policies generally take the same form for all Lane
County utilities: the utilities provide a cost estimate for the customer that defines the upfront
charges that the customer is responsible for. Upfront charges cover components such as
transformers or line extensions. Utility policy allows the utility to make determinations on a case-
by-case basis. The policies are intended to protect the utilities’ existing customers (especially
residential customers) from bearing costs driven by large industrial or commercial users.

Lane County utilities protect customers from shouldering the costs of new customers is through
new large load policies. For example, EPUD’s new large load policy makes clear that loads over
1MW and introduced after 2013 are responsible for paying for power at the non-federal
resource, or Tier 2, rates. Because EPUD uses all their allotted Tier 1 power, any new loads will
be met at Tier 2 rates. As the percentage of electricity purchased at Tier 2 rates increases, the
average cost of electricity purchased by EPUD will increase. Their policies ensure that this
additional cost is recovered not through rate increases for all their existing customers, but
through EPUD’s charges to the new customer. EPUD’s policy concludes, “District reserves the
right to pass through any costs incurred that are driven solely by a [new large load] customer.” 2

1 Oregon Governor’s Office, “Governor Kotek Signs Bills to Lower Energy Costs for Oregonians”, Aug.

2025. https://apps.oregon.gov/oregon-newsroom/OR/GOV/Posts/Post/governor-kotek-signs-bills-to-lower-
energy-costs-for-oregonians
2 Emerald People’s Utility District, “Emerald PUD Customer Service Policies and Procedures,” Mar. 2026.

https://www.epud.org/wp-content/uploads/Customer-Service-Customer-Service-Policy-Procedures-2026-

is 10 MW, but some Lane County utilities set lower thresholds. Most utilities use the metric of
average additional load over a 12-month period to determine if a customer constitutes a new
large load, and most utilities do not give those loads access to Tier 1 rates. Finer details of each
utility’s policies vary slightly, and threshold as of 2025 are shown in Table 13.

 What about connecting data centers to the grid in Lane County?
 A potential source of new large loads that has been getting a lot of attention recently is
 data centers. While there are not public plans for data centers in Lane County, popular
 news coverage has brought the issue to the fore. Pacific Power, the only IOU that serves
 both regionally and in Lane County, has some new policies for large-load customers that
 are meant to protect the broader customer base. In late 2024, the Oregon PUC
 approved Pacific Power’s rate charge that penalizes large-load customers for energy
 use that does not align with the forecasted energy use that they submit to the utility. This
 policy is meant to prevent situations where the utility invests in grid infrastructure that is
 inappropriate for the load they need to serve. It was opposed by data center companies
 and interest groups, but went into effect in February 2025 under the name “Excess
 Demand Charge and Capacity Reservation Charge.” 1 Pacific Power has also eliminated
 their “line extension allowance” for new customers seeking service over 25 MW. This
 change means that large customers will have to take on a larger share of the grid
 investments necessary to serve them. As policies change for investor-owned utilities in
 Oregon, updates can be found on the Oregon PUC website. 2

 The issue of data centers highlights an important difference between IOUs and COUs.
 COUs have little-to-no incentive to absorb the infrastructure costs of serving new loads,
 especially in Oregon where those utilities have preferential rights to BPA’s Tier 1
 resource. For IOUs, the situation is a bit more complicated. When IOUs make capital
 investments, they can use those investments to justify rate increases across their
 customer base. In Oregon, IOU rate changes must be approved by the PUC. So, in
 some cases, IOUs might be inclined to take on a higher portion of the costs of new
 service so that they can then get rate increases approved and ultimately increase return
 to their investors. That has not seemingly been the case for PacificCorp, as exemplified
 by rule changes discussed in the previous paragraph.

 Assistance, Rebate, and Monitoring Programs

Table 14 shows other utility practices related to fair and equitable rate structures. All utilities in
Lane County have assistance programs for low-income customers, as well as incentives and
rebate programs. EWEB, EPUD, Central Lincoln PUD, and Blachly-Lane have low-income
assistance programs that are funded partially by the utility’s budget and partially by customer
donations. SUB and Central Lincoln PUD partner with a local organization to provide
assistance. All utilities have information on their websites that helps customers to connect to
state/federal assistance opportunities. Although most utilities use AMI, the extent to which
utilities use their AMI data for rate design is unknown (see Chapter 6 for more on AMI).

4.1.3 Gaps and Opportunities
Lane County utilities, on average, offer low rates compared to both the state and national
averages (see Figure 21). That said, they and their customers still face many of the same cost
pressures as other utilities within the region and nation. Lane County utilities implement many of
the recommended best practices for maintaining financial health; however, there are
opportunities for improvement. Continuation and improvement of these practices may be better
achieved in the near term through:
 Planning Support for smaller utilities for strategic and/or capital planning efforts
 Rate design studies that explore options for meeting utility goals
 Increased accessibility of utility planning documents

1 Note that Blachly-Lane audits are available to customers upon request.

 109

4.2 Serving Changing Electric Load
The distribution utility needs to make sure that the combination of loads and generation on their
grid does not damage any of their equipment, cause outages, or lead to excess costs for their
customers. As we have discussed, imbalances of load and generation can cause problems for
the grid. However, there are other constraints as well. Figure 42 outlines the three types of load
constraints, their technological origins, and the ways that they are typically dealt with.

 110

Building new homes, businesses, or electricity generation changes the way power
ﬂows on the grid. Limitations of one or more components can make adding to the
  grid difficult. Below are reasons why your utility might limit new construction.

1. The amount of load and generation need to match
 There must be enough total generation to exactly match total load every minute of
 every day.
 If the load and generation don’t match, it could harm the generators and cause
 outages for customers.

  It is the job of bulk (transmission) grid planners to predict loads in the long term and make sure that
  there is enough generation to serve loads.
  Planning horizons typically look out 20 years or more and involve the construction of new electric
  generation

2. Grid components can only handle certain voltages
 The voltage drop along lines that are too long or have too much load can lead to low
 voltage issues at the end of a line, far from the generation or substation.

 Conversely, too much generation on a distribution line, such as generation from
 solar panels, can cause over-voltage.

  Distribution utilities can manage voltage issues by adding a range of different components, such as
  capacitor banks, or by replacing the line.
  Due to the wide range of potential solutions, voltage issues are generally the easiest and least
  expensive to resolve. For that reason, the planning horizon for these issues can be as little as months to
  a few years in the future.

3. Grid components are limited by temperature
 Every piece of equipment, including transformers, wires, fuses, circuit breakers, and
 more, has a temperature rating.

 Thermal issues arise when too much current passes through a piece of equipment
 and causes it to get too hot. The temperature can physically damage the equipment.

  Fixing thermal issues typically requires utilities to replace the piece of equipment or add new
  substations or lines. These actions can be expensive, so it is best to plan ahead to avoid placing too
  much load on a component.
  Planning horizons for these issues tend to be 1 -10 year.

Figure 42: Types of Physical Constraints on the Electric Grid that can Limit Load Growth

 111

Of the distribution utilities that were interviewed in the early stages of this work (see Appendix
A), only one expressed concern about serving load growth within their service territory, while
another noted that they do not have general capacity concerns, but are worried about their
ability to serve step function industrial growth. “Step function” refers to a significant increase in
load all at once, as opposed to steady load growth over time. Figure 43 gives examples of
customer actions that might result in gradual/steady changes to load, small steps, and large
steps.

 Gradual Small Step Large Step
 •Home efficiency •Electric vehicle chargers •New industrial
  improvements •Heating electrification developments
 •Energy conservation •Rooftop/ residential solar •New housing
  efforts developments
 •Small changes in •New commerical
  household consumption developments
  habits, such as
  appliances
 •Population growth

 Figure 43: Examples of Gradual, Small Step, and Large Step Load Growth

Gradual changes come from trends in habits at the household level, like adoption of home
efficiency measures, efforts to save energy, and the adoption of different appliances such as
replacing a gas stove with an electric one. They also come from population trends—more
occupied houses lead to more electricity use. Utilities can track gradual trends using year-to-
year data. Those trends can help them to predict when usage might approach a threshold for
their system. They can use those predictions in capital planning (see Section 4.1)

Small step changes in load might be the least predictable, depending on utility policies (for
instance, if a utility doesn’t require customers to inform the utility of the customer’s adoption of
an electric vehicle charger or heat pump). The size of the change in load is small, meaning that
it is likely to only pose challenges for utility assets that are nearer to the end user, such as
transformers, unless an asset is already near capacity.

Large step changes are predictable in the short term, in that new developments go through long
planning processes, so they are unlikely to come as a surprise to utilities. However, large step
changes are harder to predict long-term, as they are usually brought on by singular events
rather than long-term trends. For instance, the sale of a property might initiate industrial
development, or the election of new city councilors might inspire the reinvigoration of a
commercial district. These are the sorts of changes to load growth that might require substantial
investment in or planning of grid infrastructure.

In the case of step changes (large or small) to load, any upgrades to the distribution grid needed
to serve that load are paid for according to the policies in place by the utility. The costs could fall
on the customer, the utility (and therefore passed on to utility customers), government
subsidies, or a combination thereof.

This section reports potential indicators of changes to load within Lane County and overviews
the best practices for distribution utilities preparing for changing loads. Examples of how Lane

 112

County utilities are moving toward adoption of best practices are presented, and opportunities
for improvement are identified.

4.2.1 Indications of Changing Load in Lane County
Table 16, Table 17, and Table 18 indicate which cities in Lane County have goals, strategies,
or published plans related to different factors that could lead to changes in load. The information
in these tables is from published plans from each city (Appendix B). It is important to note that
the date of creation of these plans varies widely, engagement associated with their creation
varies, and cities use the plans in different ways. Whether or not a certain strategy is found in
these plans should not be interpreted as a firm indicator of community interest or action.

The tables indicate whether a strategy will increase or decrease load, and the extent to which
the city is pursuing the strategy using the following key:

 = actively pursuing strategies that will decrease load

 = assuming, predicting, or passively mentioning strategies that will decrease load

0 = not mentioned

 = assuming, predicting, or passively mentioning strategies that will increase loads

 = actively pursuing strategies that will increase loads

For example, “” is used for energy efficiency if a city wrote about a strategy for energy
efficiency that they are actively pursuing. If, instead, the city only mentions energy efficiency as
a possible strategy or predicts that their residents would adopt certain energy efficiency
measures, “” is used.

Gradual Changes

Table 16 shows which communities have plans that could result in gradual changes in load.
Lowell, Eugene, and Florence have energy efficiency strategies, and all cities include energy
conservation in their plans, with Coburg, Cottage Grove, Creswell, Eugene, Junction City,
Lowell, Oakridge, Springfield, and Westfir having specific goals or strategies. It is notable that
energy conservation is Goal 13 of statewide planning goals, and many cities model their
Comprehensive Plans off the statewide plan. 1 Conservation measures vary, but include things
like encouraging pedestrian and bike paths, public transit, energy efficient land use planning,
and waste management. Veneta and Lowell are actively trying to draw in new residents, while
Coburg, Creswell, Eugene, Florence, and Springfield acknowledge population growth is likely.

Small Step Changes

Table 17 shows which communities have plans that could result in small step changes in load.
For small step loads, Eugene is the only city actively pursuing strategies to increase electric
vehicle adoption. Junction City notes that though the use of electric home heating is popular in
the region, their residents are switching to natural gas. It is notable that the most recent update
to the Junction City Comprehensive Plan is from 2010. Overall, home heating electrification is
not commonly mentioned in city plans.

Most cities mention the possibility of exploring distributed solar options, but Cottage Grove,
Eugene, and Junction City are the only three actively pursuing solar as part of a broader
strategy. Cottage Grove’s Strategy 19 in their Vision and Action Plan is “Be a leader in the
development, adoption and utilization of clean, alternative energy sources and water resources.”
They call out plans to investigate solar power potential at their wastewater treatment plant, and
they aim to “establish a policy to incentivize the use of solar panels for at least some percentage
of new homes’ and new commercial and public buildings’ power source.”

Eugene’s 2010 Community Climate and Energy Action Plan calls for expanding local renewable
energy, as does their 2017 Comprehensive Plan. Junction City’s 2013 Comprehensive Plan
discusses “presently exploring the economic feasibility of using a solar collection system to heat
water used in the community swimming pool.”—although that section of the plan was last
updated in 2010. They also encourage the use of solar water heating in homes. This is not the
same as distributed solar electricity production through photovoltaic solar panels, but it would
reduce electric demand from water heaters. Most cities at least mention solar potential. While
distributed solar could pose some challenges for the grid (discussed in Sub-Section 4.4.2), in
the context of load growth, installations would result in decreases.

Historic Trends

Another way to see indicators of electricity use within Lane County is in the historic trends of
electricity sales. Figure 44 shows a plot of the annual electricity sales for Lane County’s utilities.
Also note that the y-axis is in log scale. Most of the utilities have not experienced significant load
growth in the past 20 years. Some have even experienced decreases.
