 Figure 44: Annual Electricity Sales for Lane County Utilities, 2004–2024 1

Due to the introduction of distributed energy resources such as residential solar or battery
storage and the electrification of vehicles and heating systems, utilities are challenged to
manage uncertainty in the size, location, and timing of future loads. In the Willamette Valley,
higher temperature extremes in the summer and milder winters with occasional extreme winter
weather events are leading to changes in heating and cooling patterns. Some Lane County
utilities are beginning to see their highest loads (peak loads) in summer rather than in winter.
Winter peaks have been historically typical. The uncertainties around electric load call for more
innovative, dynamic, and cost-effective solutions for future capacity needs. 2 The local
uncertainty is reflective of the broader regional uncertainty discussed in Section 3.3.

1 Data from Energy Information Administration Form EIA-861 https://www.eia.gov/electricity/data/eia861/

Blachly-Lane Electric Co-Op only provided data through 2019.
2 J. Keen et al., “Distribution Capacity Expansion Planning: Current Practice, Opportunities, and Decision

Support,” NREL/TP-6A40-83892, 1898008, MainId:84665, Nov. 2022. doi: 10.2172/1898008

 116

Ultimately, future loads depend on actions taken within Lane County at individual, community,
and county levels, and depend on how people choose to use and regulate energy. The data
presented here suggests that there may not be a pressing need to serve significant load growth.
However, cities have many goals related to industrial, housing, and commercial growth.
Achieving these goals could result in large step changes in load on specific parts of the grid.
Additional data collection to estimate future loads is beyond the scope of this Handbook.
Section 4.2.2 discusses the ways in which geographically specific load forecasts are becoming
more important for utilities.

4.2.2 Best Practices for Serving Changing Loads
The best practices for distribution utilities to serve changing loads are evolving as the situations
that drive changes in load evolve. Figure 45 is from a publication that describes existing
practices, gaps, and opportunities in distribution grid planning, and shows the “existing”
framework for distribution system planning alongside their proposed “future” framework. 1 There
are several key differences between the frameworks that indicate gaps in existing utility
practice. The “future” framework highlights how best practices can evolve to fill those gaps.

 Figure 45: Existing vs. Future Approaches to Distribution System Planning

1 Image from J. Keen et al., ibid.

 117

The “future” framework for distribution system planning shown in Figure 45 includes best
practices related to load forecasting, planning timelines, scenario development, technology
integration, and metrics.

Customer-driven, bottom-up load forecasting

Load forecasts are predictions of future loads. The “existing” framework uses system-level load
forecasts. This means that the forecasts are typically single-number forecasts for load growth
and distributed energy resource (DER) growth, estimated for a utility’s entire service territory.
System-level load forecasts typically come from long-term trends in customer usage. In the
“future” framework, the load forecasts are bottom-up and geospatially granular. This means that
the forecasts come from predictions of individual behavior or adoption of technologies by
customers within the utility’s service territory. Those behavioral changes can give location-
specific estimates of load growth. For large step growth, it is important for utilities to work with
city planners to stay informed about potential developments.

Longer planning timelines that align with government policies

Planning long-term to meet policy goals set out by local, state, or federal governments help the
utilities to align with those government goals. Currently, as discussed in Chapter 2, Oregon’s
Renewable Portfolio Standards, Clean Energy Targets, and Senate Bill 762 are important
policies with which utilities should align their planning. The “future” framework suggests utilities
extend capacity planning timelines out from 10 years to 30 years.

Integrated planning and scenario development

The “future” framework outlines a planning approach that is more coordinated between utility
departments. The coordination leads to the exploration of solutions and strategies that are
topological (related to the way power flows on the grid, dictated by switches and controls),
infrastructural, and non-wire (battery energy storage, demand response, or energy efficiency).
The introduction of future scenarios to the planning process helps utilities to investigate the
impacts of implementing various combinations of these strategies.

Advanced data and technology integration

Integrated data and technology enable a utility to collect data and use that data to guide
decision making across departments. For example, in Sub-Section 4.1.2, we show which
utilities have AMI systems but note that we do not know if those utilities use the systems for rate
design. Beyond rate design, AMI data can be used for distribution capacity planning, although it
rarely is. 1 There is a need to ensure that new data collection technologies are paired with data
management and decision-making frameworks, so that the data can be utilized. On the flip side,
it is important that the decision to adopt new technologies is supported by good data.

Additional metrics to align with stakeholders

Additional metrics of success for distribution planning can lead to decision-making frameworks
that reflect the diverse set of stakeholders. Currently, most utilities make decisions that minimize
utility capital and operational costs while maintaining N-1 circuit-level reliability (the ability to
remain operational in the instance of a single component failure). In the “future” framework,

1 J. Keen et al., ibid.

 118

utilities weigh other metrics such as hosting capacity, cost of service and avoided costs,
resiliency, equity, energy efficiency, and/or greenhouse gas emissions. Utility governing bodies
can direct utilities about what metrics to prioritize. Similarly, they may give the utility guidance
about how to prioritize potential projects. For example, EWEB’s 2018–2028 strategic plan
directs electricity investments to “be managed by prioritizing high-customer impact assets and
those systems that increase resiliency to community-critical locations.” 1

4.2.3 Approaches Applied in Lane County
Generally, Lane County utilities implement planning processes closer to the “Existing” process
shown in Figure 45. We do not have sufficiently detailed information about utility practices to
compare them to the five categories of best practices discussed, though we do highlight a few
specific practices.

The utilities that published Integrated Resource Plans (Table 12) all engaged in some level of
load forecasting, but the depth of their analyses varies. For example, SUB produced a forecast
of annual average power demand that extended historical trends, 2 whereas EWEB has
undertaken more substantial modeling and analyses to make predictions about total demand,
peaks, and suitable resources. 3 For the other utilities, BPA and/or PNGC (a generation and
transmission cooperative co-owned by 25 PNW distribution utilities) make forecasts of total
annual power delivery to the distribution utilities. 4 The load forecasts being used by Lane
County utilities for resource planning do not consider address-level load changes within the
distribution system.

As noted in Sub-Section 4.1.2, existing utility plans are on the order of 5-10 years. We cannot
speak to individual utilities’ scenario development or integration of data and technology. When it
comes to integrating metrics of stakeholder interest, we similarly cannot speak to individual
utility approaches. That said, at the state level, steps are being taken to add performance
metrics. Oregon SB 688 – Performance-Based Regulation of Electric Utilities (effective
January 2026) allows for performance-based regulation of electric companies to better align the
electric utility’s operations with the public’s interests. Performance outcomes of interest include
reliability, affordability, renewable energy adoption, and wildfire safety. 5 Avoided cost metrics
are applied by all utilities for setting net metering rates (see Sub-Section 4.4.2).

4.2.4 Gaps and Opportunities
Lane County utilities have been serving loads that, for the past two decades, have changed very
little. However, changes to loads, both in terms of quantity and timing, are expected in the near
future. These changes could be driven by electrification, adoption of distributed energy
resources, change to weather patterns, efficiency and conservation efforts, state and federal

1 Eugene Water & Electric Board, “Eugene Water & Electric Board 2018-2028 Strategic Plan,”

https://www.eweb.org/documents/Reports%20and%20Publications/eweb_strategic_plan_2018_2028_20
21_2022_2023_2024_update.pdf
2 Springfield Utility Board, “SUB 2025 Integrated Resource Plan,” 2025. https://www.subutil.com/wp-

content/uploads/2025-SUB-Integrated-Resource-Plan_FINAL.pdf
3 Eugene Water & Electric Board, “Integrated Resource Planning,” www.eweb.org/projects/energy-supply-

planning
4 PNGC Power, https://www.pngcpower.com/
5 Oregon Legislative Information, “2025 Regular Session – SB 688 Enrolled,”

https://olis.oregonlegislature.gov/liz/2025R1/Measures/Overview/SB688

 119

policies, development, and more. Lane County utilities could benefit from exploring evolving
best practices, and local and county governments could support the adoption of best practices.
Some opportunities for utilities or the organizations who collaborate with or support utilities
include:
 Countywide load forecasts and a load forecasting approach that can provide bottom-up
  estimates for each service area.
 Planning integration between county, city, or community priorities within a service territory
 and utility plans, forecasting, and rate design.
 Improved grid planning processes for some or all utilities:
 – Beginning with utilities that have near-term concerns about serving load growth.
 – Creating guidelines for utilities interested in improving grid planning processes that
 include estimates of costs, workforce needs, technology needs, etc. to build their
 understanding of the risks and potential benefits of more detailed distribution planning.
 – Fostering collaborations between utilities and city planners to keep well informed of
 potential developments or other step changes to load within a city.
 – Support utilities and electric boards in adopting planning metrics such as hosting
 capacity, cost of service and avoided costs, resiliency, equity, energy efficiency, and/or
 greenhouse gas emissions.
 Evaluation of utilities based on their adoption of best practices for distribution planning and
 making improvement plans with individual utilities. This would include analysis of how well
 utility planning aligns with policy timeframes, how utilities perform planning and scenarios
 development, utility data and technology integration, and utility metric adoption.
 Peak load scenarios prediction can help Lane County utilities prepare for peak load
 situations without requiring full load forecasts.

4.3 Promoting Energy Efficiency, Conservation, and Peak
 Management
Promoting energy efficiency and conservation are priorities at state and local levels. By some
estimates, energy efficiency has the potential to cut U.S. energy use in half. 1 In the Northwest,
efficiency measures have been promoted by BPA, utilities, federal and state standards, the
Northwest Energy Efficiency Alliance, and the market (people electing to purchase products with
higher efficiencies). Whereas energy efficiency is concerned with using less energy to do the
same things, energy conservation is concerned with replacing habits or practices with less
energy intensive ones.

The Northwest Power and Conservation Council tracks “savings” from energy efficiency
measures, shown in Figure 46. The Council assumes that energy demand is given (i.e., people
would use energy for the same things regardless of whether the efficiency measure was in
place) and that cause-and-effect of energy efficiency can be clearly established, both of which
are assumptions with meaningful limitations. That said, what Figure 46 makes clear is that utility
efficiency programs can have meaningful impacts in terms of electricity use. Today, energy

1American Council for an Energy-Efficient Economy, “Halfway There: Energy Efficiency Can Cut Energy

Use and Greenhouse Gas Emissions in Half by 2050.” Sep. 2019. https://www.aceee.org/research-
report/u1907

 120

efficiency is increasingly considered an energy resource and a means of avoiding the need to
build additional generation.

 Figure 46: Impacts of Energy Efficiency Programs in the Pacific Northwest 1

Improving energy efficiency and conservation is not necessarily in the direct interest of the
utility. After all, they generate revenue through the sales of electricity. For that reason, federal,
state, and local policies have a major influence on what programs utilities might take on. Studies
show that national energy efficiency standards lead to hundreds of dollars in savings for the
average consumer per year. 2,3 Utility-related policies and programs typically come from the state
level.

1 Image from Northwest Power and Conservation Council, “Energy Efficiency,”

https://www.nwcouncil.org/energy/energy-topics/energy-efficiency/
2 Public Interest Research Group, Reducing costs across America, August 25, 2024.

https://pirg.org/resources/reducing-costs-across-america/
3 Andrew deLaski and Joanna Mauer, “Energy-Saving States of America: How Every State Benefits from

National Appliance Standards,” Feb. 2017.

 121

As a state, Oregon ranks 9th on the American Council for an Energy-Efficient Economy’s 2025
State Score Card. 1 This score card ranks states according to the strength of their energy
efficiency policies, incentives and standards related to utility programs, construction, and the
transportation sector. The study notes that the benefits of energy efficiency programs are “most
pronounced where states and utilities have invested in energy efficiency as a resource by
factoring efficiency as an integral part of utility energy resource planning and decision making in
much the same way as resources such as power plants, wind turbines, and solar panels.”

Energy conservation efforts from community or individual perspectives include things like
planning bikeable cities and then choosing to walk or bike instead of drive. They deal with
questions about how and for what ends humans use energy. In 1974 the State of Oregon
Adopted Statewide Planning Goal 13: Energy Conservation. 2 The goal directs: “Land and uses
developed on the land shall be managed and controlled so as to maximize the conservation of
all forms of energy, based upon sound economic principles.”

Utilities might encourage conservation through modern peak management practices.
Historically, utilities responded to peak loads with new transmission or generation capacity.
Now, given the suite of distribution side and consumer grid technologies, utilities have options to
encourage alternative usage patterns. In this section we discuss best practices for energy
efficiency, conservation, and peak management by utilities. We then consider practices applied
by utilities in Lane County and future opportunities for improvement.

 Why might utilities implement energy efficiency or conservation
 programs?
 A utility might choose to implement programs to reduce energy usage. There are four
 main motivations for utilities to introduce these programs:
 1. The utility has a physical asset on the distribution network that is working at or near
 capacity (as described in Figure 42). In some cases, implementing demand
 response programs could reduce peak loads and avoid the need for costly upgrades.
 2. The utility wants to avoid buying electricity at high costs from the market. For Lane
 County’s utilities who buy power securely within their Tier 1 allotment, this is an
 unlikely driver, but for utilities buying substantial amounts of Tier 2 power or buying
 from the market, reducing customer demand could save money for both the utility
 and the customers.
 3. The utility might want to increase revenues by selling on the market. If customers
 use less power, a utility with generation assets could have more power available to
 sell on the market. In most circumstances, utilities can sell on the market at higher
 rates than they would sell to their customers. Given that many Lane County utilities
 do not have generation assets, this is an unlikely driver for them.
 4. A utility might be required (or incentivized) by federal, state, local, utility commission,
 or board of director policies to implement demand response or energy efficiency
 programs.

1 American Council for an Energy Efficient Economy, 2025 State Energy Scorecard, Mach 18, 2025.

https://www.aceee.org/research-report/u2502
2 Oregon Planning Department of Land Conservation and Development, “Goal1 13: Energy

Conservation,” https://www.oregon.gov/lcd/op/pages/goal-13.aspx

 122

4.3.1 Best Practices
Utilities have levers to promote energy efficiency and conservation both within their organization
and with their customer base. These include rate structures (see Sub-Section 1.5.2 for rate
schedules for Lane County Utilities and Section 4.1 for an introduction to rate design); demand
response strategies; formal board policies that promote efficiency; and educational, outreach, or
incentive programs.

Rate Structures for Energy Efficiency and Conservation

Of the basic rate structures introduced in Sub-Section 1.5.2, rising block energy charges are
the most direct way to penalize high energy use. With rising block rates, the more energy a
customer uses in a month, the more they pay for each unit of energy. Demand charges may
lead to reductions, but they can be difficult to understand for consumers. 1 Moreover, demand
charges are intended to reduce peak demand, so are best aimed at the customers that
contribute to peaks. More rate structures are discussed in the next section on demand
response.

Demand Response and Peak Management

In demand response programs, the utility essentially pays customers not to use electricity at
certain times, typically during peak demand. Demand Response programs can take several
forms, typically categorized as incentive-based or price-based (Figure 47). Incentive-based
programs offer direct compensation to consumers who join the program and shift their demand.
Price-based programs use price signals or tariffs that increase prices when demand is high.
These could include demand charges, but unlike normal demand charges, customers typically
have a choice about whether they want to participate in demand response programs. Table 19
gives a description of each type of program.

1 American Public Power Association, “Peak Load Management Strategies for Public Power,”

https://www.publicpower.org/resource/peak-load-management-strategies-public-power

 125

In an interruptible load program, a customer (usually commercial, industrial, or public facilities)
agrees to reduce their usage—“shed load”—during instances of high demand (this is usually in
non-emergency situations or situations where the distribution utility is required to shed load by
BPA as discussed in Section 3.2). These customers are compensated for their status as
“interruptible loads,” regardless of whether they are interrupted during a high-demand event.
Typically, an interruptible load customer has at least 100kW of usage that can be powered down
on command. The technology supporting interruptible load programs can be as simple as a
telephone (the grid operator calls the customer) or can be a part of an automated metering and
load control system. Because of their simplicity, interruptible load programs are the most used
demand response programs.

While many of the programs in Table 19 only require advanced metering technologies, they can
be made more effective with additional technologies. For example, residential customers might
change their behaviors as part of demand response programs, but there are a variety of
technologies (like smart thermostats) that can help them.

Beyond technology requirements, demand response programs also vary in the necessary effort
to stand up the program. For instance, the demand bidding program requires that there is a real-
time market to which customers can bid. On the other hand, time of use pricing can fit easily into
a utility’s existing rate-setting practice. When choosing a demand response program, a utility
weighs the required effort and technology investment against the peak management benefits.
The utility can predict potential benefits by modeling scenarios using historic data and various
levels of customer participation. They may choose to survey customers to gauge interest in
participation before introducing a program.

Demand response programs can lead individual residents or businesses to implement a
technique called “peak shaving.” Peak shaving is when the customer reduces their electricity
demand on the grid by switching to the use of on-site energy storage or generation systems
during periods of peak demand to reduce their electricity costs without having to reduce their
usage. Peak shaving is a customer response to demand response programs, not a utility
strategy; however, it may be helpful for distribution planning purposes for the utility to be aware
of which customers implement peak shaving strategies. Customers without energy storage or
generation systems may respond to demand response programs by reducing consumption or
moving high-energy activities (like running the dishwasher or charging an EV) to off-peak hours.
Utility-owned or operated distributed energy resources can also be used for peak management
and are discussed further in Section 4.4.

Formal Board Policies for Energy Efficiency and Conservation

For COUs, the board of directors plays a significant role in determining utility priorities, goals,
and direction. Formal board policies prioritizing energy efficiency and/or conservation are a way
to steer the utility toward efficiency, conservation, or demand response measures.

Education, Outreach, and Incentives

Customer efficiency incentive programs can take several forms including cash rebates,
financing assistance, and/or energy audit programs (Figure 48). These incentives might apply
to things like smart thermostats, weatherization, electric vehicle chargers, and lighting upgrades.

 126

 Figure 48: Overview of Energy Efficiency Programs

Utilities, or utility websites, are one of the primary places that many people go for information
about electricity. Utilities can supply resources on their websites promoting efficiency and
conservation, including informational resources, connections to government programs, and
details about utility efficiency programs. They can host educational events and reach their
customers via newsletter. In some cases, utilities will provide lists of contractors who install
certain equipment or who meet certain energy efficiency standards.

4.3.2 Approaches Applied in Lane County
The Northwest Power Act requires that the Northwest Power and Conservation Council
prioritizes energy efficiency and conservation in their resource strategy for the Pacific
Northwest, BPA, the Northwest Energy Efficiency Alliance, and the Energy Trust of Oregon are
all organizations working to meet the Northwest Power and Conservation Council’s energy
efficiency targets. BPA funds energy efficiency programs for their preferred customers through
the Energy Smart Reserved Power Program, and the Energy Trust of Oregon funds programs
for customers of the state’s IOUs. A list of available incentives can be found online on the
Oregon Department of Energy’s Energy Hub for Incentives Programs + Projects in Oregon. 1

In Lane County, peak management through demand response and energy efficiency strategies
could be impactful for utilities with Load Following product contracts with BPA (SUB, Central
Lincoln PUD, Lane Electric, Blachly-Lane, and in 2028 EPUD) and utilities that are using
electricity beyond their Tier 1 allotment (EWEB and EPUD). Given that Load Following contracts
include demand charges, reducing peak demand can decrease those demand charges. That
said, implementing programs may prove more costly than the charges. For utilities beyond their
1 Oregon Department of Energy, “Incentive Finder,” https://incentives.oregon.gov

 127

Tier 1 allotment, power provided during peak demand comes at a higher cost (Tier 2 or market
rate) than base Tier 1 power. In these cases, if peak management strategies can be
implemented at a lower cost per kWh than Tier 2 or market rates, they stand to save the utility
and their customers money. For all utilities, regardless of their BPA contracts, peak
management and energy efficiency programs have the potential to decrease consumption in
ways that reduce or defer the need for infrastructure upgrades, but fully understanding that
possibility requires detailed, spatially resolved information about loads and individual grid
component capacities.

Table 20 shows which Lane County utilities offer energy efficiency related programs/ services.
Lane County utilities ubiquitously offer education and outreach, customer connections to
external incentives, and internal incentive programs (some supported by BPA). Though only
EWEB, SUB, and EPUD have easy-to-access customer energy audit programs on their
websites, there are other programs in Oregon that offer the service. Pacific Power doesn’t offer
audits specifically, but customers can access detailed records of their own energy use online.
Those records come with customized tips for energy savings. Pacific Power is the only Lane
County utility that offers demand response programs, with commercial and industrial incentives
programs and voluntary time-of-use programs for residential customers. In their strategic plans,
EWEB, SUB, and EPUD mention that, to meet future goals, they want to explore options related
to demand response. EWEB recently completed a Demand Side Potential Assessment, looking
at conservation potential, demand response potential, electrification potential and solar PV
potential 1.

EWEB, SUB and EPUD include energy efficiency as a stated central aim in either their board
policy (SUB, EWEB) or strategic plan (EPUD). SUB’s Board Policy Manual encourages rate
design modifications to promote energy efficiency and “encourages staff to pursue actions that
encourage energy efficiency where practicable—including, but not limited to, offering home
energy audits, publicizing the financial and environmental benefits associated with making home
energy efficiency improvements, and educating homeowners about all existing federal and state
incentives, including the availability of low-cost loans, that make energy efficiency improvements
more affordable.” SUB already implements seasonal rising block charges (Table 6), and that
costs are different in summer vs. winter. Lane Electric and Blachly-Lane also implement rising
block energy charges.

EPUD’s strategic plan discusses their previous demand response pilots, through which they
determined that despite the potential savings, the costs for rolling out the programs and for
retrofitting homes were too large to offer the program permanently. 1 Nevertheless, their strategic
plan acknowledges the need to further explore incentives, time-of-use rates, and other demand
management options.

4.3.3 Gaps and Opportunities
For Lane County’s COUs, there are numerous opportunities to investigate energy efficiency,
conservation, and demand management strategies, especially with the utilities who have
documented their interest (EWEB, SUB, EPUD).
 Rate design studies for demand response and energy efficiency program options that model
  customer response scenarios, energy savings, and utility costs
 Assess the potential of energy efficiency and conservation across the county, modeling
  different customer behavior scenarios
 Collaborative peak management programs could enable multiple utilities to stand up peak
  management programs while sharing the costs of staff training, customer outreach, and
  other expenses associated with initiating and running the program
 Demand response operational training for all the utilities, aimed at helping them to minimize
  the costs of implementing demand response or other energy efficiency/ conservation
  programs; and for customers, aimed at helping them to participate in programs and adopt
  the associated technologies
 Educational efforts and surveys of Lane County electricity consumers can help utilities to
 understand their customers’ desire to participate in energy efficiency or conservation
 programs

4.4 Investing in Energy Resilience and Generation
As we have noted in previous sections, Lane County’s COUs have priority access to BPA
electricity from the federal dam system. This electricity comes at a price that does not
necessarily incentivize utilities to build their own generation within the county or buy from any
other provider because that power will likely cost more than Tier 1 power. During normal
operating conditions, this situation enables Lane County utilities to keep rates relatively low (see
Sub-Section 1.5.2 for rates). However, when we consider the resilience of Lane County’s

1 Emerald People’s Utility District, “2020 Strategic Plan,” Jul. 2020. https://www.epud.org/wp-

 129

energy system, the lack of incentives to build out generation compounds the dilemma that
challenges all resilience investments: it can be difficult to commit funds today to prepare for
potential future disaster events. In Chapter 5 we discuss specific hazards in detail and the best
practices for utilities to reduce risk and improve their resilience to those hazards. In this section,
we review several general resilience measures that are relevant across all hazard types.

4.4.1 Best Practices
The following best practices for utilities. That said, boosting energy resilience is only the utilities’
responsibility. Individuals, governments, organizations, and others also have roles (see Chapter
9). For their part, utilities can work to manage risks to the system, perform customer outreach to
understand willingness to pay for resilience investments, track vulnerable populations within
their service territories, and support the development of distributed generation.

Risk Management and Hardening

Risk management strategies involve identifying and assessing risk, planning risk mitigations,
and communicating risks to the public and relevant stakeholders. Guidance and toolkits for
utilities interested in pursuing risk management strategies can be found from the American
Public Power Association. 1 Risk management strategies applied to system assets can serve as
an input to the distribution system planning discussed in Section 4.1, helping utilities to identify
their most at-risk assets and how they can mitigate that risk. Having built a deeper
understanding of risk, utilities can take measures to harden infrastructure against those risks
(see Section 6.1 for a description of grid hardening strategies).

Customer Outreach

Efforts to build resilience can be costly and might not always provide immediate benefits to
customers. For example, undergrounding an electric line reduces the risk that the line is
damaged during a storm as well as the risk that the line causes a wildfire. However, the costs of
undergrounding a line are typically high, and the benefits to customers during normal operating
conditions are minimal. Customer surveys can help utilities gauge how willing the customers are
to pay for risk-mitigating investments. Surveys can also serve to inform customers about risks.

Valuing Resilience

Utilities should build frameworks for valuing resilience measures by thinking about long term
costs and benefits and the value that customers place on maintaining service, especially in
times of extreme weather. While there is not a single framework considered to be the best
practice for valuing resilience, it is an active area of research and development. 2 Frameworks
for specific technologies or hazard events have been proposed. They include the valuation of
resilience during extreme temperature events, 3 the valuation of the resilience benefits provided

1 American Public Power Association, Risk Management Toolkit for Public Power Utilities.

https://www.publicpower.org/resource/risk-management-toolkit-public-power-utilities
2 Poudel, Shiva et al. A Framework to Design Consumer-Centric Operational Strategies for Resilience

Enhancement, IEEE Transactions on Industry Applications, September 25, 2023/
https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/10262165
3 “A valuation framework for customers impacted by extreme temperature-related outages,” Appl. Energy,

vol. 368, p. 123450, Aug. 2024, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.apenergy.2024.123450

 130

by different forms of solar PV generation, 1 and the valuation of resilience benefits from utility
building retrofit programs. 2 Frameworks for valuing resilience should be locally relevant, built
with customer engagement, and transparent.

Customer Vulnerability Tracking and Load Shedding Preparedness

Utilities can help to mitigate the impacts of outages for their customers if they know what
customers are especially vulnerable. They should track factors such as who among their
customers has a well for water (well pumps require electricity), uses electric medical devices, or
has limitations on mobility. They can use that knowledge to prioritize system recovery efforts,
connect their most vulnerable customers to further assistance, or prioritize areas of investment
in distributed generation, back-up power, or microgrid technologies. Utilities can use these lists
to avoid turning off power to vulnerable customers during emergency load shedding events like
those described in Section 3.2.

Winter Storm Uri, which hit Texas in 2021, was a real-world demonstration of what the third
level of emergency load shedding event could look like. The after-action reporting from the
Americal Public Power Association 3 offers some lessons for utilities. The report emphasizes that
although load shedding procedures are in place for many utilites, they are rarely used and in
need of refinement. Steps such as regularly updating critical infrastructure and customer lists,
evaluating potential enhancements to existing load shedding computation and software tools,
and performing regular drills, simulations, or table-top exercises can all help a utility be better
prepared and reduce impacts on their customers (Figure 49).

Distributed Energy Resources

The National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners defines a distributed energy
resource (DER) as:

 “a resource sited close to customers that can provide all or some of their
 immediate electric and power needs and can also be used by the system to
 either reduce demand (such as energy efficiency) or provide supply to satisfy the
 energy, capacity, or ancillary service needs of the distribution grid. The
 resources, if providing electricity or thermal energy, are small in scale, connected
 to the distribution system, and close to load. Examples of different types of
 distributed energy resources include solar photovoltaic (PV), wind, combined
 heat and power (CHP), energy storage, demand response (DR), electric vehicles
 (EVs), microgrids, and energy efficiency (EE).” 1

DERs can provide resilience benefits if they are able to operate during an outage. Developers,
local governments, private property owners, utilities and other organizations might consider
investing in distributed energy resources, but since there are not yet standardized approaches
for valuing energy resilience, it can be difficult to determine who covers what costs. 2 Utilities can
support DER developments by providing clear net metering and interconnection policies.

Blackstart resources for system resilience

Blackstart resources can restart the power grid after it has experienced a blackout. Blackstart
capability can be developed and maintained through investments in specific, usually inverter-
based, generation resources that have the capabilities to start without the support of the grid.
Microgrids can also serve as a blackstart resources and operate islanded from the rest of the
grid during a blackout. Blackstarting the grid is not just a matter of “turning things back on.” As
the grid comes back online—even just in a local area—the same physical dynamics discussed
in Section 3.2 apply. Load and generation must be balanced. Therefore, a system with
blackstart capabilities must be able to control the restarting of both generation and the
connected loads. Imagine, if you have one small generation source, but hundreds of homes are
electrically connected to that source, the source might become quickly overloaded and fail in
minutes (or seconds!) after it restarts.

4.4.2 Approaches Applied in Lane County
Risk management, customer outreach, and valuing resilience

In the areas of risk management, customer outreach, and valuing resilience, we are unable to
characterize approaches applied by Lane County utilities. That said, in the area of valuing
resilience, we know there is an emerging interest among utility and local officials to improve
their shared understanding of the value of resilience, as well as that of the residents of Lane
County. Lane County utilities have a variety of approaches to risk management documented in

1 NARUC Staff Subcommittee on Rate Design, “NARUC Manual Distributed Energy Resources Rate

Design and Compensation,” The National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners, Washington,
DC, Nov. 2016. NARUC Manual on Distributed Energy Rate Design and Compensation
2 W. Rickerson, J. Gillis, and M. Bulkeley, The value of Resilience for Distributed Energy Resources: An

Overview of Current Analytical Practices, National Renewable Energy Laboratory, June 2024.
https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy24osti/90139.pdf

policies, 1 strategic efforts, 2 and hazard specific documents that we discuss in greater detail in
Chapter 5. EWEB has published multiple versions of a Climate Guidebook that synthesizes
how they expect climate change to impact the resiliency of the utility. 3 EPUD has recently
surveyed their customers about their interest in resilience investments.

Customer vulnerability tracking and load shedding preparedness

In the area of customer vulnerability tracking, only one of Lane County’s utilities—that we know
of given publicly available data sources—actively collects information on residents most
vulnerable to outages. EWEB encourages customers who rely on electrically powered medical
devices to contact their customer service team to be placed on the utility’s Critical Care list. 4
The remaining utilities do not actively track medically vulnerable customers and instead
encourage those individuals to prepare for emergencies independently, such as by having a
generator or battery backup available. 5 Notably, EWEB and SUB offer a generator loan program
to help customers enhance their personal resilience during outages. 6

Distributed Energy Resources

In the areas of distributed energy resources, we see in Figure 68 that there is a significant
amount of residential solar within Lane County. In fact, 6,313.7 MWh of solar energy was
generated by residential systems in Lane County in 2022. 7 All Lane County utilities have both
net metering and generation interconnection policies. Table 21 shows the maximum allowed
size of a residential solar installation (first column). It is mandated by the state that utilities allow
residential installations of 25kW AC/ 200kW Nameplate or smaller. 8 Most utilities use that
threshold as their maximum, but EPUD allows larger installations.

Table 21 also shows the rates (as of 2026) for net metering and the rates for direct generation.
Net metering rates are the rates that the utility pays customers for the excess solar generation
that they add to the grid. Direct generation rates are the rates that the utility pays other non-
residential distributed solar installations for the power they put onto the grid. The fourth column
of Table 21 gives the methodology that the utility uses to set the rates. All utilities use an
avoided cost methodology which is the practice of setting compensation rates based on the
cost the utility would have otherwise incurred to generate or purchase that energy from another
source. In other words, customers are compensated at the rate the utility “avoids” paying by
receiving the customer’s solar generation instead of procuring that power itself.

All utilities have programs that allow customers to choose renewable energy. Most programs
take the form of a surcharge added to the customer’s bill either as a block charge (meaning the
customer pays a set rate for a pre-determined block of renewable generation) or as an
upcharge per kWh used. The rates are listed in the final column of Table 21.
Central Lincoln PUD has incentives for various types of distributed systems, including battery
storage, small hydropower, solar electric, and wind electric. They also started a community solar
project in 2019 that allows customers to purchase shares of a shared solar installation rather
than installing rooftop panels. 1 Then, each month, participating customers receive bill credits for
the solar energy their portion of the solar installation generated. The project was funded in part
through grants from the Bonneville Environmental Foundation and the Oregon Department of
Energy, which allowed panels to be offered at a fraction of the cost of traditional rooftop
installations. The program has since sold out of available panels, however the project features a
live dashboard showing the generation from the existing solar panels. 2

EPUD operates the GREEN Grant program, 3 which is a voluntary customer-funded program
that provides up to $40,000 annually for the construction of new renewable energy projects at
eligible schools and non-profit organizations within EPUD’s service territory. GREEN
subscribers vote to select the winning project each year. Past recipients include a 10.78 kW
solar array installed on a farm. EWEB has a similar program 4.

Blackstart resources for system resilience

Currently, none of the generation resources in Lane County (listed in Table 9) can provide
blackstart capabilities. This means that, in the event of a regional grid outage (see Section 3.3
for discussion of the likelihood of such an event), none of the generation resources in Lane
County would be able to deliver electricity to end users. Because so much of Lane County’s
electricity comes from outside the county, in the event of a regional outage, the generation
sources within Lane County could not come close to meeting the demand within the county.
Therefore, the grid would not be able to stay balanced and operating. To be able to use the
local generation in the case of an outage, the available generation resources would have to be
electrically connected to a limited amount of load (likely critical facilities discussed in Section
8.3). Doing this would require the design of a microgrid that can be islanded from the rest of the
grid during an outage.

4.4.3 Gaps and Opportunities
General resilience and local generation efforts are difficult to characterize completely with the
available public information, but given that many of these best practices are novel or emerging,
there are numerous opportunities to support their implementation.
Risk management evaluations for utilities can enable assessment and improvement of risk
  management practices. As we will discuss further in Section 5.2, asset-specific risk
  assessment and mitigation is especially important for minimizing natural hazard risk impacts.
Multi-utility customer outreach campaigns can be more cost effective for utilities who want to
 assess their customers’ willingness to pay for resilience measures.
Value of Resilience Models can quantify and integrate numerous metrics that capture the
  value of resilience to communities in Lane County. A quantified measure of resilience benefits
  can then be used by decision makers to decide if the measure is worth its costs.
Critical customer tracking and load shedding plans updated yearly and shared between
  utilities and the county could help to keep both parties informed of specific vulnerable
  populations within the county and plans to serve those populations. Utilities and/or external
  assessors could explore options for integration of load shedding software tools. The county
  could integrate utilities’ load shedding plans into emergency response drills and emergency
  plans.
Islanded resources and blackstart planning could provide the ability to supply some
 electricity in the event of a regional outage or other emergency situation using existing
 generation within the county. We further discuss such planning as it relates to wildfire
 mitigation and critical facilities in Sections 5.2 and 8.3, respectively.

 Chapter 5
 Utility Hazard Mitigation and
 Preparedness
Brooke Marten, Tasnima Naoshin, Ian Hermanson, Candace
 Briggs, and Ali Trueworthy
This chapter discusses the hazard posed by wildfires, earthquakes, winter storms, and
physical and cyber security threats. These hazards were selected because of the risk
they pose to Lane County utilities and grid reliability. Where possible, hazard probability
and intensity are reviewed, and the best utility practices for risk mitigation are outlined.
This section begins with an overview of weather monitoring capabilities in Lane County
because weather monitoring is critical to natural hazard mitigation.
Risk to any person, population, facility, utility asset, or other entity is a function of hazard and
vulnerability (,Figure 50). For example, the wildfire risk to a building would be the probability
and probable intensity of a fire around the building, multiplied by the building’s exposure and
susceptibility to a fire.

5.1 Weather Monitoring to Prepare for Weather-Related Hazards
Our interviews with Lane County utilities revealed that weather monitoring is increasingly critical,
but Lane County’s systems are underdeveloped. Utilities recognize gaps in local data coverage
and see operational and safety benefits to improving weather and fire modeling capabilities.
Frequent extreme weather and wildfire events created concern.

Most Lane County utilities rely on external data sources such as the National Oceanic and
Atmospheric Administration (NOAA), the National Weather Service (NWS) and the Oregon
Department of Forestry (ODF) for forecasts and warnings. Some utilities depend exclusively
on these state and federal systems. Pacific Power and EWEB maintain the most developed
internal weather networks (mapped in Figure 51), while some smaller utilities operate basic
local sensors mentioned in their wildfire mitigation plans (not mapped in Figure 51).

Figure 51 displays all existing weather monitoring stations in Lane County along with their
operators. Wildfire mitigation plans, utility websites and google maps were used to compile a
dataset of weather monitoring station locations. There are 19 weather monitoring stations in the
County, with eight of those in the Eugene-Springfield metropolitan area. Pacific Power has the
most extensive network with nine stations in Lane County. 1 EWEB operates two 2 NWS operates
three at the Eugene, Creswell, and Florence airports. 3 Pacific Power and EWEB operate their
own weather stations, and some other utilities have low-cost weather sensors placed within their
territory. Currently, Pacific Power and EWEB do not share data from those stations with other
utilities but have expressed openness to doing so. The University of Oregon weather station is
used by the University to prepare for outdoor events 4 and data is available online.

ALERTWest is a situational awareness platform using cameras and sensors for AI wildfire
detection. 5 It is used to coordinate wildfire detection across more than 50 agencies in Oregon,
including local governments, utilities, and federal land managers. 6 EWEB hosts one camera in
the ALERTWest network on their Carmen Smith Hydropower Facility. Data from four
ALERTWest stations in Lane County is publicly available. Coverage gaps are especially
pronounced in rural and high-risk wildfire zones, leaving limited visibility into localized
conditions. 7
Integration of weather data into operations (e.g. PSPS decisions, predictive modeling) is
inconsistent across Lane County utilities. Table 22 summarizes utility monitoring assets and
capabilities, as well as data gaps identified through a review of utility wildfire mitigation plans.

 Weather monitoring and forecasting serve different purposes. Local weather monitoring can
 give immediate, local data about conditions on the ground. This data could impact operational
 decisions such as when and where to send crews or whether to de-energize a specific line (e.g.,
 due to high local winds). Local monitoring data is not typically sophisticated enough to make
 forecasts but can add granularity to local forecasts. For example, a national forecast might
 predict the general start time of a storm, while a local asset would provide information about
 exactly when winds increase. For weather forecasts, utilities rely on larger regional and national
 networks.

 Weather Monitoring to Prepare for Weather-Related Hazards

Lane County utilities report a growing urgency to strengthen weather and wildfire monitoring as
climate-related hazards intensify. Of these hazards, utilities view wildfires as the highest-impact
threat. Utilities generally agree that improved access to real-time, localized data on wind,
humidity, and temperature, integrated directly into GIS and operational systems is needed to
support power outage management decisions, prevention strategies, and crew safety. Many
utilities are exploring improved detection tools—such as AI-based fire cameras, remote sensors,
and custom forecasting—and are increasingly collaborating with state agencies, universities,
and neighboring utilities to share data and modeling resources. However, most utilities
acknowledge that current capabilities are limited or uneven across the region. Utilities have a
shared interest in a coordinated regional monitoring framework that expands coverage,
enhances predictive capability, and supports long-term system resilience.

5.2 Wildfires
Frequent droughts, changing wind patterns, and rising global temperatures have increased both
the frequency and severity of wildfires in the Pacific Northwest. Figure 52 shows annual wildfire
counts and acres burned since 2010, revealing a clear upward trend over the past decade.
Transmission and distribution-level grid infrastructure can both ignite and be damaged by
wildfires, posing significant risks to public safety. While distribution systems cause more wildfire
ignitions than transmission systems, transmission infrastructure often ignites more catastrophic
fires due to high voltages and remote terrain. 1

Lane County and utilities have pursued several planning endeavors associated with assessing
and mitigating wildfire risk, resulting in the Lane County Natural Hazards Mitigation Plan
(NHMP) 2 and the Community Wildfire Protection Plan (CWPP). 3 Lane County’s NHMP
identifies both the probability of wildfire in Lane County and Lane County’s vulnerability to
wildfire to be high. Dry conditions and forested landscapes contribute to the high probability of
wildfire. An extensive wildland-urban interface—the area shared by human developments and
undeveloped wild lands—is responsible for the high vulnerability. A high wildfire risk can have
consequences like home loss, vegetation loss, soil damage, wildlife death, food and habitat
loss, and air pollution.

3 Lane County, “Lane County Community Wildfire Protection Plan (CWPP)”

Lane County’s CWPP includes a wildfire risk assessment and an associated action plan
focused on coordination, fuels management, and community education and support. The risk
assessment for the CWPP relies on data from the Oregon Wildfire Risk Explorer, 2 which
presents information from the 2023 PNW Quantitative Wildfire Risk Assessment (QWRA),
performed by researchers at Oregon State University and the U.S. Forest Service. 3

The QWRA quantifies “integrated expected wildfire risk” geospatially across Lane County. That
risk metric combines the burn probability of a land area along with the presence and
susceptibility of transmission lines, substations, and power plants (among other infrastructure).
It does not, however, quantify risk to specific utility assets. Figure 53 maps wildfire burn
probability as defined by the QWRA as the average annual likelihood of wildfire. The highest
burn probabilities are in eastern and southeastern Lane County, with the lowest probabilities of
0% can be seen in the Eugene/Springfield area.

percentages of their territory land with burn probabilities over 1%. SUB has the lowest burn
probabilities across their area with nearly 95% of land falling below 0.02% probability.

Regardless of the probability of wildfires within their service territories, Lane County's electric
utilities are required to submit Wildfire Mitigation Plans (WMPs) to the Oregon Public Utilities
Commission, as mandated by Oregon Senate Bill 762 2 This bill passed in 2021 following the
2020 Labor Day fires, which were the most destructive wildfires in Oregon’s history, and
included the Holiday Farm Fire in Lane County. The Holiday Farm Fire burned 173,439 acres on
both the North and South sides of Highway 126 and the McKenzie River, caused the death of
one person, and devastated the community of Blue River, OR. 3,4

Although full investigations of the Holiday Farm Fire have not yet been released, news from
ongoing litigation (February 2026) highlights the role and potential liability of electric utilities in
wildfire scenarios. In a lawsuit claiming negligence of BPA, EWEB, and Lane Electric, evidence
presented from the U.S. Forest Investigations indicates that the Holiday Farm Fire ignited in two

That said, utilities also have the responsibility to minimize the damage to their infrastructure

prepare and protect communities from the risks brought on by wildfires. Support for wildfire
mitigation is also becoming central to federal legislation and grantmaking. The implementation
of the best practices for utilities that we describe below may be supported by these funding
sources.

Wildfire mitigation and preparedness add costs for utilities in multiple ways, which can be
especially difficult for COUs like those in Lane County. Direct spending on wildfire mitigation
currently is ~2-5% of utility capital spending, but the year-over-year spending for wildfire
mitigation is growing. 1 Other costs include contributions to state wildfire funds, liability
(especially for utility-caused fires) and increased insurance costs, and the increased cost of
capital due to the increased risk that utilties face. We discuss best practices for minimizing the
impacts of these costs as well.

5.2.1 Best Practices
Science and best practices for utility wildfire mitigation are constantly improving. The best
practices we present in this section come from a 2025 publication from Pacific Northwest
National Laboratory that draws “from publicly available utility WMPs from the United States and
Canada, recent findings from wildfire risk reduction research, and industry engagement.” 2 The
publication was funded by and created in response to Trump Executive Order 14308:
Empowering Commonsense Wildfire Prevention and Response. 3 In their WMP, SUB writes
“SUB is aware of the need to act on wildfire risk management planning despite the lack of an
industry framework and operational specifics.” Their perception of the unsettled state of the
science and practice of wildfire mitigation planning is accurate. There are not yet industry
standard frameworks or specific best practices. Instead, we have a menu of common practices
that can be combined and innovated upon as appropriate for the individual utility (Figure 56).

Wildfire risk mitigation and preparedness activities happen before, during, and after a wildfire
event, and involve dealing with the additional costs incurred from mitigation and preparedness
measures.

Pre-event Mitigations: The pre-event phase begins years before the event, and the activities in
this phase may last years. This phase is concerned with anticipating risk (e.g., risk modeling,
weather forecasting, inspections, benchmarking), proactively decreasing vulnerability and the
probability of utility-caused ignitions (e.g., system hardening, vegetation management, asset
management, supply chain management), and increasing preparedness of different human
actors (e.g., employee training, multi-entity coordination, customer engagement).

During Event Practices: During a wildfire, utilities first implement their emergency response
then move into service restoration. They need to make real-time decisions to minimize public
safety impacts, infrastructure damage, and overall risk. Utilities rely on weather monitoring tools,
grid data, and system survelliance to rapidly assess conditions. Based on this information,
utilities can deploy teams, adjust grid settings, or de-energize specific portions of the power
system. A variety of technology and operations options support during-event wildfire response.

1 Barlow J.T., Powell D.W., Kincaid J.B., Abernethy-Cannellla K.G., and Boff D.S., “Utility wildfire industry

trends review,” Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, PNNL-SA211619, 2025.
2 A. M. Coleman et al., “Current Best Practices on Wildfire Risk Reduction for Electric Transmission and

Distribution Systems,” Nov. 2025. https://www.pnnl.gov/projects/wildfire-risk-resilience/best-practices
3 J. Wendel, NEW PNNL Report Aids Utilities in Preparing for Wildfires, Pacific Northwest National

Laboratory, January 14, 2026. https://www.pnnl.gov/publications/new-pnnl-report-aids-utilities-preparing-
wildfires

Post-event Recovery: Post-event, utilities focus on inspection, restoration, and recovery to
safely restore electricity and improve future system resilience.1 After-action reporting and
emergency preparedness planning are important for making sure that lessons from the event
can be internalized and used to improve future responses.

Cost impacts: Ways for mitigating the impacts of the additional costs associated with wildfire
damage or mitigations include cost-sharing arrangements, prioritization of measures based on
cost and impact, and collaborations with landowners or other utilities.

Figure 57 illustrates the system performance during each phase and the types of activities that
occur. Figure 56 lists specific measures that are considered best practices within each phase.
Details about each of these measures, including descriptions, applications, risk management
benefits, implementation challenges, examples, and potential future directions, are provided in
Coleman et al. (2025). 2 Chapter 6 describes many of the specific technologies that can support
those measures and activities that are considered best practices within each phase.

1 Information from A. M. Coleman et al., ibid.
2 Adapted from A. M. Coleman et al., ibid.

 147

 Figure 57: Impact Event Timeline for Risk and Resilience Mitigation

Scientists, technology developers, utilities, regulators, and other stakeholders continue to
improve and establish best practices. Technological improvements will become available to
utilities, fragility curves will support a better understanding of the susceptibility of different utility
assets to wildfires, and risk forecasting will continue to improve with each fire season. Keeping
up with changes to best practices may be difficult for small utilities, but support can be provided
by interest groups, governments, and technical assistance. Planning on a regular cycle should
include learning about new and emerging practices.

5.2.2 Mitigation Approaches Applied in Lane County
Each utility in Lane County has a WMP, although the methods they used to create the WMPs
vary. They are all required to include a risk analysis, mitigation strategies, engagement
strategies, and performance tracking plans. Full guidance for WMPs is provided by the Oregon

 148

PUC. 1 In this section, we analyze WMPs from Lane County’s utilities, including EWEB, 2 SUB, 3
EPUD, 4 Central Lincoln PUD, 5 Lane Electric, 6 Blachly-Lane, 7 and Pacific Power. 8

Table 24 through Table 36 show wildfire mitigation and preparedness strategies, along with
whether individual utilities are using those strategies according to their WMPs, and Table 23
provides a key for interpreting those tables.

 Table 23: Key for Interpreting the Letters and Colors in Table 24 through Table 36.
  A Active: The utility is actively and presently pursuing this strategy

  P Planned: The utility is planning to pursue this strategy, or they are currently testing pilots

  C and/or its appropriateness of implementation. This strategy may be mentioned in the

  N Not Mentioned: The utility does not mention this strategy in their WMP

Pre-event Mitigations

Pre-event mitigations account for most of the wildfire mitigation and preparedness best
practices. This aligns with the primary goal of wildfire mitigation planning: to prevent utility-
caused ignition. Lane County utility pre-event mitigation practices are shown in Table 24
through Table 31.

Planning, Evaluation, and Analysis

Table 24 shows major planning, evaluation, and analysis practices implemented by Lane
County utilities. All Lane County utilities have WMPs, and all those WMPs acknowledge public
safety power shut-offs (PSPS) as a wildfire mitigation strategy and document their PSPS
protocol. In Lane County, only EWEB and Lane Electric have ever implemented protocol (on
September 9–11, 2022). Enhanced Powerline Safety Settings (EPSS) are only specifically
mentioned in two utility documents (Blachly-Lane and Pacific Power); however all utilities note

their abilities to set their protective equipment (relays and reclosers) to be more sensitive during
fire season, which constitutes EPSS.
Only EWEB mention hiring independent evaluators to review utility wildfire mitigation processes.
Pacific Power notes that upon completion of the next wildfire risk model, third party consultants
will review and evaluate its current modeling strategy and compare that strategy to industry best
practices. Independent evaluations can be expensive and time-consuming because external
consultants must learn the details of utility system and operational processes. That said, if
utilities need additional support for mitigation planning, independent evaluation can be a good
choice.
Consequence analysis is the analysis of the potential harms of a utility-caused ignition. While
there is no standard workflow for performing consequence analysis and quantifying potential
impacts can be challenging, consequence analysis could help utilities justify mitigation
investments like grid hardening. Pacific Power is the only utility WMP to mention regular
consequence analysis, which is incorporated into their wildfire risk modeling that considers risk
to be the product of wildfire consequence and wildfire likelihood.

Asset and Supply Chain Management

Asset management practices (Table 25) involve evaluating the status of utility assets. All Lane
County utilities include some form of an asset inventory in their WMPs, but only some evaluate
asset risk. Evaluation of asset health and risk could help utilities better prioritize investments.
While all utilities do proactive inspections, only three use advanced inspection technologies
such as unmanned aerial vehicles, LiDAR, or thermographic imaging of equipment (i.e., infrared
inspections).

Supply Chain Management (Table 26) involves maintaining up-to-date knowledge of the
availability, sources, and pricing of electrical infrastructure. Utilities can collaborate and share
information about new components or technologies through shared databases. Supply Plan
management is barely discussed in Lane County WMPs. EPUD, a utility with a relatively large
service area, mentions the importance of geographic distribution of spare components (see
Figure 58). Implementing a distributed component storage approach helps to ensure that
components are available when and where they are needed. For utilities with smaller service
territories, this might not be a priority. Pacific Power emphasizes the value of sharing
information with other utilities, noting that a goal of its mutual aid program is to exchange best
practices and technologies that strengthen the industry’s ability to prepare for and respond to
emergencies. The company also cites its participation in the Oregon Wildfire and Electric
Collaborative as a forum to share lessons learned with other utilities, though it does not specify
whether a formal shared database exists.

Hardening and Grid Modernization

Utility plans for infrastructure hardening and control reveal information and capacity gaps
(Table 27). Undergrounding was consistently identified as an effective but expensive wildfire

 151

mitigation measure, though some utilities have other motivations to underground lines. The
cities of Lowell and Veneta both prioritize undergrounding as a community resilience strategy
(see Table 59 in Section 9.2). Most utilities prioritize higher-risk assets for undergrounding.

All utilities mention covering or insulating conductors (“tree wires”) as a mitigation measure. The
layer of insulation (compared to standard bare wires) allows wires to withstand initial contact
with vegetation or debris. Covering conductors is often cheaper than undergrounding but does
come with challenges. Covers can hide power line degradation, trap moisture, and add weight. 1

All utilities identified pole upgrades as a potential mitigation strategy, but approaches varied.
Many mentioned reviewing pole options, and some utilities are actively replacing certain poles.
Some utilities mentioned lightning arresters and wildlife guards. Bird nests on pole structures
can exceed pole weight limits, and nest materials contacting electrical equipment can cause
outages. To address this, EWEB installed 28 Osprey nesting platforms throughout their service
territory. This unique approach redirects nesting activity away from electric infrastructure while
preserving habitat.

Sectionalizing and Islanding as well as topology optimization (Table 28) are system level
mitigation strategies that go relatively undiscussed in Lane County utility WMPs. Sectionalizing
and Islanding refer to a utility’s ability to separate a section of the grid independent of the rest of
the grid—sectionalizing typically refers to a section that can be independently de-energized
whereas islanding refers to a section that can be energized independent of the larger grid.
Topology optimization is an approach used to design the physical layout of the grid to mitigate
or reduce the impacts of PSPS events. This can be done by strategically sectionalizing,
islanding, or adding redundant power flow pathways. These mitigation strategies involve
complex planning and operation and are generally considered secondary measures to be
implemented after extreme risks are addressed. Additionally, these approaches might not
always make geographical sense. For instance, EPUD notes that their “electric facilities are also
likely to cross boundaries of evacuation zones as they are created and adjusted during a wildfire

1 Intelligent Network Diagnostic Technology. “To insulate or not – The Hidden Risks of Covered

Conductors”. Aug. 2025. https://ind-technology.com/news/to-insulate-or-not-insulate-that-is-the-question/

 152

event. Therefore, it is unlikely that there will be an isolation point (e.g., recloser, switch, fuse) at
a weather zone or evacuation zone boundary. As a result, operational responses will likely
affect District energy facilities and customers in areas beyond just the area of immediate
concern.” 1 Pacific Power's WMP discusses use of sectionalizing as a mitigation strategy through
their use of relay devices that automatically sectionalize the grid to protect downstream circuit
segments.

Lane County utilities mention proactive equipment and device settings, adaptive reclosers, and
advanced fire-safe devices for monitoring and control to varying extents across WMPs. These
three technology categories allow utilities to cautiously automate and monitor equipment and
control schemes during high-risk periods. Some of the specific technologies are described in
Chapter 6, while details on the application, benefits, and challenges of these mitigation
strategies can be found in Coleman et al. (2025). 2 Generally speaking, they are all strategies
that allow more cautions equipment monitoring and control schemes during high-risk periods, in
increasingly automated ways. As technology and practices in these areas evolve, utilities should
actively monitor new developments.

Vegetation Management

Table 29 shows utility implementation of various vegetation management practices. Trimming
and inspection cycles are effective industry standards defined and required by the NESC and
OPUC to mitigate utility-caused ignitions. Integrated vegetation management is a long-term
species management approach that includes site assessment, control, evaluation, and
maintenance. None of the Lane County utilities participate in vegetation management
accreditation programs. Only EWEB and EPUD mention cooperative agreements with other
land managers (e.g. government agencies, conservation organizations, and private landowners)
in their WMPs. Central Lincoln compares annual vegetation-trimming investments with tree-
related outages to evaluate the cost effectiveness of its trimming program.

Hazard Modeling and Forecasting

Table 30 shows practices related to hazard and weather modeling and forecasting. Nearly all
utilities use QWRA data to characterize the wildfire risk in their service territories. Only EWEB
and Lane Electric completed data- or image-driven wildfire risk assessments, which require
capacity to manage large datasets, analytics, or GIS tools. Utilities lacking the technical or
funding capacity for a full data-driven risk assessment sometimes rely on expert perspectives
from their employees who work in the field to identify high risk assets.

Most of the Lane County utilities do not have wildfire spread models that can help them predict
how specific ignitions could impact electrical assets and communities, in-house simulation
capabilities, or practices to detect emerging wildfire risk. The QWRA data that utilities use is
from 2023 even though wildfire risk prediction is based on probabilistic models and therefore
requires data inputs on a yearly basis. Lane Electric documents significant fires up to 2024, but
they are not quantitatively integrated into their risk assessment.

During a recent wildfire event, Pacific Power used wildfire spread and consequence modeling to
identify the circuits of risk several days in advance of the wildfire threat. As a result, they could
identify the areas where extreme winds and extreme fire potential risk existed and could
consider that in their PSPS decision making. Pacific Power also recently implemented FireSight,
a commercially available wildfire simulation tool that combines the utility's asset information with
public data (i.e., community characteristics, terrain, vegetation, and weather) to forecast the
consequence or impact of a potential wildfire from a given ignition point. Pacific Power's WMP
describes how the utility has its own Meteorology Department for managing day-to-day threats,
providing recommended changes to operations based on conditions, and identifying and
subsequently closing forecasting data gaps. All utilities use red flag warnings to inform their
decision making, but only EWEB, Blachly-Lane, and Pacific Power have any of their own
weather monitoring infrastructure.

Training, Coordination, Benchmarking, and Engagement

About half of Lane County utilities have some wildfire-related training or tabletop exercises for
their employees, while all mention some form of coordination or mutual aid agreements in their
WMPs. Customer engagement was a part of most, but not all, WMPs, and EWEB, Blachly-
Lane, and Pacific Power highlighted utility benchmarking practices.

During-event Best Practices

During a wildfire event, utilities can continue to take steps to reduce risk to assets, customers
and employees. Lane County utility during-event mitigation practices are shown in Table 32
through Table 34.

Situational Awareness and Operations

Enhanced situational awareness strategies include data collection and integration processes
that enable “comprehensive understanding of the working environment, including real-time
wildfire risk and weather, fuel, and system conditions.” 1 Such comprehensive integration was

1 A. M. Coleman et al., ibid.

not apparent in any of the WMPs. However, achieving improved situational awareness using
daily risk forecasts was an articulated goal articulated in Pacific Power's WMP.

All utilities include plans for activating PSPS, engaging customers during an event, and de-
energizing lines to mitigate further incidents (Table 32).

Monitoring Tools and Assessments

All utilities rely on publicly provided weather monitoring and emergency alert systems for
meteorological data, and as discussed, Pacific Power has its own Meteorology Department. For
the most part, utilities do not use or plan to use visual surveillance system or overhead remote
sensing during events. However, Pacific Power does have a continuous improvement strategy
targeting an expanded camera network to enable better visibility across its service territory.
Most utilities have a network of sensors throughout their system (SCADA) that can provide
basic information about the status of assets through the measurement of voltage, current, and
frequency. Pacific Power is also considering how remote sensing technology could augment
and inform traditional vegetation management inspections (Table 33).

Adaptive Grid Operations

Almost all Lane County utilities outline different device settings used in high risk and wildfire
event scenarios in their WMPs. Many have adaptive reclosers that can automatically or

 156

remotely change settings (Table 34). Utilities’ consideration or implementation of fire-safe
devices mostly includes the replacement (or consideration of replacement) of conventional
expulsion fuses with fire-safe current-limiting fuses or reclosers because conventional fuses
expel hot particles when they are operated.

Post-Event Recovery

Lane County utility WMPs vary in their post-event planning, especially for actions beyond
system restoration. Reporting, metrics tracking, and improving emergency plans based on
lessons learned are all important aspects contributing to continuous improvement in the face of
wildfire risk. Post-fire vegetation management is not specifically discussed in many of the
WMPs, but general vegetation management practices are discussed (usually in detail). Lane
County utility post-event mitigation practices are shown in Table 35.

Cost Mitigations

Wildfire mitigations can be costly, but few of Lane County’s utilities mention cost mitigation
measures in their WMPs (Table 36). Except for Pacific Power, none mention cost share
arrangements, and few indicate how they prioritize actions. There are a few mentions of
landowner or cross-utility collaborations. For example, Pacific Power has a Tree Voucher
Program where landowners can receive tree replacement vouchers. Overall, the utilities
seemed to approach their WMPs—and the mitigations in them—independently. However,

 157

EWEB led tabletop PSPS exercises in 2024 and 2025, extending invitations to other utilities to
participate. Pacific Power participates in a range of cross-utility collaborative events including
IEEE working groups related to protection for wildfire risk and joint IOU meetings.
