5.2.3 Gaps and Opportunities
Overall, utility WMPs vary in both approach and quality, and county residents and risk managers
would likely benefit from a guided, uniform approach. Lane County utilities include many basic
mitigation strategies in their WMPs but have yet to plan integration of more advanced systems
or practices. This status is commensurate with their relatively small sizes and with the state of
knowledge on best practices. To reiterate, the best practices presented in Chapter 5 constitute
a menu of options more than a checklist of must-haves. None-the-less, there are some places
where additional efforts or collaboration across utilities could create widespread benefit.
 Wildfire Risk Assessments based on the most up to date information are foundational to
  wildfire mitigation planning. The basic process of risk assessment can be improved through;
 – Improved Hazard Characterization: Burn probability and fire intensity estimates based
 on trends analysis, historic information, and up-to-date geospatial data, modeled on an
 annual basis
 – Improved Vulnerability Characterization: vulnerability of individual utility assets based
 on their location, age, exposure, material, and asset type
 – Upgrade and system hardening recommendations based on risk assessment
 Consequence Analysis Framework to help utilities and local governments understand the
  consequences of utility caused ignitions
 Collaborative WMP Innovation performed by utilities, local government, and subject matter
  experts can help to fill some of the gaps that are apparent in current WMPs. Because many
  of the gaps are common among Lane County’s COUs, a collaborative process is likely to
  have wide benefit and potential cost savings. Steps to such a process could include:
 – WMP evaluation: A deeper look at existing WMPs than is provided in this document to
 assess each utility’s status and needs
 – WMP template development: Collaborative design of a WMP template that all utilities
 can use, making it easier for other stakeholders to understand, compare, and learn from
 WMPs

 158

 – WMP integration plans: WMP plans and mitigations will change as new information on
 best practices becomes available. As utilities make system changes, they will often need
 to make corresponding operational changes. For example, if a utility replaces bare wire
 conductors with cover conductors, there is reduced risk that those covered conductors
 will ignite a fire. For that reason, a utility might choose to increase the wind speed
 threshold at which they implement PSPS. A choice to do something like this should be
 made over the course of multiple years and informed by utility data. Tracking data,
 implemented mitigations, and WMP changes over the years requires that WMPs are
 integrated into existing utility processes.
 – Supply chain management database development and benchmarking:
 Collaboration between utilities to track component prices, availability, costs and benefits
 of implementation, and geographic distribution within Lane County. A database, and the
 process of updating the database could provide a central location and opportunity for
 utilities to share their experiences with different system hardening, engineering, and
 monitoring technologies
 – Weather monitoring: Deploying additional local weather stations that can be monitored
 by all utilities
 – After-action reporting standardization: Creating a standard after-action report
 template can set customer and stakeholder expectations for what utilities need to
 provide and customers and stakeholders expect to see following a wildfire event
 – Training and tabletop exercises: collaborations for training can reduce costs and
 enable sharing of lessons learned
 – Customer engagement: Develop customer resources that all utilities in Lane County
 can use
 Sectionalizing and islanding planning may expose opportunities to isolate high-risk/ critical
 portions of the grid in emergency situations
 Enhanced Situational Awareness would include the adoption of new monitoring
 technologies and the integration of the data into operational decision-making.
 Wildfire spread models can be complex and require expertise to create and interpret
  Therefore, it is best for utilities to collaborate and request state/regional support
 Cost sharing arrangements could be explored by interested utilities and local governments

5.3 Earthquakes
Earthquakes and seismic activity can cause severe ground shaking, soil liquefaction, and
landslides. Depending on intensity, secondary impacts can include flooding, dam failures, and
tsunamis. In Lane County, earthquakes could originate from four primary sources: the offshore
Cascadia Subduction Zone (CSZ), the oceanic Juan de Fuca plate, the continental North
American plate, and renewed volcanic activity in the Cascades Mountains. Seismic activity from
the CSZ and the North American plate can impact all regions of the county, although extents
may vary. The Juan de Fuca plate would particularly impact coastal regions, while volcanic
activity would heavily impact inland regions. 1 The greatest hazard comes from the occurrence of
a megathrust earthquake on the CSZ. Figure 59 displays the predicted earthquake intensity of

1 Data from Oregon Department of Land Conservation and Development, “Oregon Natural Hazards

Mitigation Plan,” Sep. 2020. https://www.oregon.gov/lcd/nh/pages/mitigation-planning.aspx

 159

a CSZ event across Lane County. Seismic severity is highest near the coastline, rated “Severe,”
and gradually decreases inland to “Very Strong” and “Strong.”

In Lane County, coastal communities are at highest risk from a CSZ earthquake. Florence,
Glenada, and areas along the Siuslaw River estuary are expected to experience the greatest
impacts from severe ground shaking, tsunami inundation, and liquification. The Willamette
Valley is expected to experience greater impacts than the Cascades region of the county, which
is more likely to be impacted by low magnitude crustal earthquakes. The Willamette Valley in
particular faces moderate to very high susceptibility to ground shaking amplification—a
phenomenon where seismic waves increase in intensity as they travel through the ground. The
Lane County NHMP identifies three areas in the valley as very high susceptibility: east of
Highway 99 around Junction City, along Highway 126 East between Springfield and Walterville,
and southeast of the metropolitan area along Highway 58 and Interstate 5 near Pleasant Hill
and east of Creswell.

The probability of damage from seismic shaking was recently increased in the non-coastal
portion of Lane County, due to newly discovered faults. In the state NHMP, earthquake risk in
Lane County is given the highest possible hazard classification. 1 The county NHMP assigns the
region a catastrophic hazard classification. Multiple utilities identify their systems—including
poles, transformers, substations—to be susceptible to moderate to high risk from earthquakes,
with severe events leading to projected outage durations of weeks to months. 2

1 Oregon Department of Land Conservation and Development, ibid.
2 Lane County Emergency Management, “Lane County Multi-Jurisdictional Natural Hazards Mitigation

Plan,” Oct. 2023. https://cdnsm5-
hosted.civiclive.com/UserFiles/Servers/Server_3585797/File/Government/County%20Departments/Emer
gency%20Management/Hazard%20Mitigation%20Plan/20180828__MASTER_MJ-
NHMPUpdate_Final.pdf

5.3.1 Best Practices
Best practices for utility earthquake resilience were synthesized primarily from the Oregon
Department of Energy’s Guidebook for Local Energy Resilience 1, a report from the Oregon
Seismic Safety Policy Advisory Commission on risk and recovery from a Cascadia Subduction
Zone event 2, and a DOE-sponsored report on multi-hazard resilience of the United States
electricity system. 3
A megathrust CSZ event is expected to cause a range of severe and sudden energy system
impacts. The duration of these impacts is uncertain, which makes it difficult to predict recovery
needs and timeline. Most utilities’ earthquake risk mitigation strategies consist of pre-event
actions and planning to reduce vulnerabilities and enable a flexible response. Earthquake risk
mitigation focuses primarily on the resilience of infrastructure assets, coordination with key
emergency preparedness stakeholders, and establishing preparedness plans for staff and the
community. Figure 60 lists specific measures and activities that are considered best practices
for utility earthquake resilience at each phase of a disaster.
Pre-Event Mitigation: Seismic design standards and regulations regarding siting, design,
construction, and safe operation are periodically updated to reflect new hazard assessment
information. Key utility facilities—such as powerhouses, operation centers, substations,
transmission towers, and distribution poles—should be upgraded to adhere to current
standards. 4 Critical electrical infrastructure should not be sited in high liquefaction risk areas,
when possible. 5 Utilities can evaluate assets in high-risk areas for relocation, retrofit,
replacement or redundancy if already sited or built. Sensors can provide remote monitoring and
advanced warning capabilities. Similarly, remote or manual shutdown procedures can be
established to increase operational flexibility and protect energy infrastructure from cascading
impacts. Critical engineering records should be digitized and made accessible in case of
potential system shutdowns post-event. 6
Additionally, coordination with key emergency preparedness stakeholders is essential for an
earthquake event. Pre-event, utilities can connect with local government and communities to
integrate existing emergency plans, determine contingency plans for critical public services and
hubs, and make strategic investments to prioritize service in the event of disaster. Local or
back-up generation capabilities can be expanded to provide power in case of grid-wide impacts.
Utilities, in conjunction with local or state agencies, can establish mutual aid agreements with
providers outside of the region, creating a pathway to quickly mobilize skilled personnel and
materials to aid in recovery post-event.

2019. https://www.oregon.gov/energy/safety-resiliency/Documents/Oregon-Resilience-Guidebook-
COUs.pdf
2 Oregon Seismic Safety Policy Advisory Commission, “Oregon Resilience Plan,” 2013.

https://www.oregon.gov/oem/Documents/Oregon_Resilience_Plan_Final.pdf
3 B. Preston et al. “Resilience of the U.S. Electricity System: A Multi-Hazard Perspective,” Oakridge

National Laboratory, Aug. 2016.
https://www.energy.gov/sites/prod/files/2017/01/f34/Resilience%20of%20the%20U.S.%20Electricity%20S
ystem%20A%20Multi-Hazard%20Perspective.pdf
4 B. L. Preston et al., ibid.
5 L. Koho et al., ibid.

 Figure 60: Best Practices for Utility Resilience to Earthquakes 1

A final pre-event mitigation best practice is to provide preparedness plans and resources for
staff and the local community. Employee training, interagency tabletop exercises, and
associated resources can produce a more coordinated, streamlined response in the event of a
disaster. Equipping staff and facilities with redundant communication devices, such as satellite
phones and radios, can help maintain critical operations post-event.

During Event Practices: During an earthquake, utilities must promptly implement measures to
protect public safety and minimize infrastructure damage. Real-time monitoring systems are
essential for maintaining situational awareness and providing advanced alerts and data
regarding seismic activity and associated risks. Controlled shutdown procedures are another
critical practice, enabling utilities to isolate affected parts of the system, prevent cascading
blackouts, and protect vulnerable assets. Backup generation systems will play an important role
in ensuring essential services can continue to function.

Post-Event Recovery: Post-event, utilities should conduct comprehensive inspections to
identify damage, prioritize repairs, and ensure safety before restoring electricity across affected
areas. Maintaining clear public communication channels is vital to inform communities about

1 Information from Oregon Seismic Safety Policy Advisory Commission ibid.

 163

repair timelines, available resources, and safety precautions. Mutual aid agreements could be
used to expedite longer-term recovery efforts. While recovery is underway, backup generation
systems continue to provide temporary energy solutions for critical services until primary
infrastructure is repaired or replaced.

5.3.2 Mitigation Approaches Applied in Lane County
This section summarizes Lane County utility earthquake resilience practices across pre-event,
during-event, and post-event phases (Table 37, Table 38, Table 39, respectively). Findings are
based on publicly available materials from EWEB, 1,2,3,4,5 EPUD, 6,7 SUB, 8,9,10 Blachly-Lane, 11
and Central Lincoln. 12 We were unable to locate specific plans from Lane Electric, but
references the emergency preparedness sections of their website when applicable.

Pre-Event Earthquake Best Practices Applied in Lane County

Design to Seismic Standards: One of the most documented pre-event practices across Lane
County utilities is ensuring that new electrical system components meet updated seismic design
standards. EWEB, EPUD, SUB, Blachly-Lane, and Central Lincoln PUD all discuss plans to
expand service and address growth through future facilities, substations, and/or transmission
and distribution lines. New construction is expected to adhere to updated and improved
infrastructure engineering standards.

Proper Infrastructure Siting: In their addendum to the Lincoln County Hazards Mitigation Plan,
Central Lincoln PUD discusses the possibility of constructing a new headquarters facility to
provide safety and continuity of operations after a Cascadia event. Similarly, in their 2020
Strategic Plan, EPUD explores the possibility of developing a backup headquarters location, as
the current headquarters is subject to flood risk from nearby waterways. Although the primary
risk mitigated through EPUD’s action is not earthquake-related, the site of a backup
headquarters building can be selected to address seismic risk as well. SUB is in the process of
updating acquisition plans for future facilities, which could be an opportunity to incorporate
seismic risk as a criteria for site selection.
Relocate, Retrofit, Replace, or Add Redundancy to High-Risk Assets: EWEB, EPUD, SUB,
Blachly-Lane, and Central Lincoln PUD all document system hardening projects, such as
distribution system upgrades and seismic retrofits to substations and facilities, to mitigate
earthquake risk. EWEB plans to “prioritize links between local generation and essential
services,” while EPUD has added redundancy to their system through new feeder ties and
alternating feeds. EPUD also plans to retrofit major substation and headquarters facilities to
meet current seismic standards. New substations from SUB add additional power for growth
areas but also add redundancy and reliability to their existing service area. Central Lincoln PUD
lists priorities of strengthening their grids through redundancy and looped systems, modernizing
systems and components, undergrounding high-risk overhead lines, evaluating relocation of
utility infrastructure, and seismic retrofits. Blachly-Lane is in the process of retrofitting legacy
infrastructure with resilient materials and improved standards.
Asset Management: SUB has documented efforts to increase remote operational capabilities
in the event of a disaster, through the implementation of a mobile device management program
and procurement of a disaster recovery system.
Contingency Plans: EWEB has documented goals in their Business Management System to
improve disruption response plans and mitigate consequential risks through business continuity
and resiliency efforts.
Coordination and Mutual Aid Agreements: SUB, EWEB, Central Lincoln, and EPUD
reference mutual aid as a potential action to undertake in the event of a disaster, whether that is
an earthquake, winter storm, or other hazard event. These same utilities are part of the Western
Regional Mutual Assistance Agreement (WRMAA). 1 Mutual aid is integrated into SUB’s 2025
Emergency Operations Plan and listed as a goal in EPUD’s 2020 Strategic Plan. All utilities
emphasize the importance of coordination.
Employee Training: SUB and EWEB document current staff training through FEMA Incident
Command Structure, an emergency management framework that can be applied in the event of
an earthquake, as well as other hazards. Central Lincoln PUD participates in county tabletop
exercises, but they are indicated to be for seasonal weather hazards only. Additional tabletop
exercises could be implemented to train earthquake-specific coordination and response.
Redundant Communication Infrastructure: SUB has documented efforts to expand crisis
communications and emergency communications plans, which may include redundant
communication infrastructure. SUB’s efforts to increase remote operational capabilities also
illustrate their commitment to preserving communication in the event of an emergency.

Post-Event Earthquake Best Practices Applied in Lane County

Ongoing Communication: All Lane County utilities maintain real-time outage maps for
customer reference. EPUD and EWEB have documented goals of improving customer
communication during service disruptions, with EPUD aiming to provide information on
estimated restoration times. SUB has documented goals of enhancing their outage reporting
system, utilizing new communication technologies to reach customers, and expanding their
crisis and emergency communications plan.

Mobilize Mutual Aid: EPUD and SUB have documented plans and procedures on mobilizing
mutual aid resources in the event of a disaster. Even though, EWEB, EPUD, SUB, and Central
Lincoln, are part of the Western Regional Mutual Assistance Agreement (WRMAA), 1 documents
do not explicitly describe how they would mobilize mutual aid in the event of an earthquake.

Use Backup Generation: EPUD has made efforts to increase local generation through landfill
gas generators, residential and commercial solar, and a community solar program. These
sources have the potential to be utilized as backup generation. EWEB’s strategic plan
emphasizes a goal of increasing local capability to generate emergency power for critical
community loads. Accordingly, EWEB has installed back-up power capabilities to support the
operation of emergency wells in case of large-scale water service disruption at various
educational facilities, conducted a microgrid pilot project, and runs the Backup Power Program –
an affordable loan program to help customers purchase backup power systems.

5.3.3 Gaps and Opportunities
Overall, Lane County utilities appear to implement some foundational earthquake resilience
practices, primarily pre-event strategies such as using updated design standards and relocating,
retrofitting, replacing, or adding redundancy to high-risk areas. All utilities maintain outage maps
and prioritize customer communication. Besides these practices, however, it is difficult to
evaluate gaps in utilities’ earthquake risk management strategies because they are not
documented in a uniform way. An initial opportunity for improvement is to:

1 “Western Region Mutual Assistance Agreement for Electric and Natural Gas Utilities,” 2003.

https://www.westernenergy.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/western-region-mutual-assistance-
agreement.pdf

 167

Standardize earthquake risk management planning for utilities so that widely accessible
mitigation plans exist that can inform local governments, customers, and other utilities.

5.4 Winter Storms
Winter storms bring heavy snowfall, ice and freezing rain, extreme cold, and wind chill
conditions. When coupled with rapid snowmelt, secondary impacts can include flooding and
landslides. Lane County experiences winter storms annually, though storm behavior can vary
between the valley, coastal, and Cascade regions of the county. Ice accumulation and snowfall
from winter storms are more common in the Cascade region of the county. The valley region
experiences less heavy snowfall, but is more susceptible to strong winds, extreme cold, and ice
accumulation. The coast region rarely experiences snowfall, with winter storms typically bringing
strong winds, rain, and hazardous coastal conditions. 1,2

Ice accumulation on utility poles and lines can damage electricity distribution infrastructure and
lead to widespread outages. Storms can also lead to downed vegetation, posing additional risk
to transmission and distribution infrastructure. Extreme weather conditions and impacts to
transportation routes delay power restoration during outages. January 2024 ice storms caused
multiple outages lasting multiple days in Lane County. Major utilities such as SUB and EWEB
experienced outages in nearly half their meters, while more than 90% of EPUD customers lost
service. Restoring power took up to two weeks, as tens of transformers, hundreds of poles, and
thousands of feet of wire needed to be replaced. Related utility ice storm reports are available
for EWEB, 3 EPUD, 4 and SUB. 5

As discussed in Lane County’s NHMP, 6 Lane County faces a high probability of winter storms
and is highly vulnerable to winter storm effects, per both county and state assessments. Winter
storms occur at least once per year in the county, and the valley region historically experiences
a severe winter storm once every 3–4 years. As of 2020, however, there is no statewide
program to study the impacts of winter storms in Oregon, so the probability of regional winter
storm events is unknown. The county NHMP assigns a Level 3 Critical Severity hazard
classification to winter storms, citing risk to public safety and infrastructure failure.

5.4.1 Best Practices
There are several strategies that utilities can take to mitigate the risks caused by winter storms.
Best practices for utilities managing winter storm hazards pre-event, during event, and post-
event are offered in several documents including the National Lab of the Rockies’ (NLR)
document titled “Current Practices in Distribution Utility Resilience Planning for Winter Storms,” 1
The Center for Climate and Energy Solutions’ document titled “Resilience Strategies for Power
Outages,” 2 and in the Oregon Guidebook for Local Energy Resilience. 3 Figure 61 lists specific
measures and activities that are considered best practices for utility resilience to winter storms
at each phase of a disaster.

 Figure 61: Best Practices for Utility Resilience to Winter Storms 4, 5, 6

Pre-Event Mitigation: The NLR report provides several long-term mitigation strategies
including vegetation management, system hardening and undergrounding, network redundancy,
and grid modernization. Short-term operational strategies, focused on communication and
response, can reduce impacts and streamline recovery during and after an event. Long-term
resiliency investments in energy infrastructure increase the overall resiliency of the system.
Vegetation management strategies, such as tree trimming and installation of tree-resistant

1 J. Keen et al., “Current Practices in Distribution Utility Resilience Planning for Winter Storms,” NREL/TP-

-6A40-88974, 2478837, MainId:89753, Aug. 2024. https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/2478837/
2 Center for Climate and Energy Solutions, “RESILIENCE STRATEGIES FOR POWER OUTAGES,” Aug.

2019. https://www.c2es.org/document/resilience-strategies-for-power-outages/
3 Koho et al., “Oregon Guidebook for Local Energy Resilience: for small and medium electric utilities,”

2019. https://www.oregon.gov/energy/safety-resiliency/Documents/Oregon-Resilience-Guidebook-
COUs.pdf
4 Information from J. Keen et al., ibid.
5 Information from L. Koho et al., ibid.
6 Information from Center for Climate and Energy Solutions, ibid.

 169

conductors, can reduce the number of power outages caused by branches falling on power lines
during a storm. System hardening strategies, such as upgrading distribution system
components for increased structural capability in the face of wind gusts and ice accumulation.
Undergrounding distribution lines completely prevents wind- or ice-induced failure. Network
redundancy ensures that critical hubs and infrastructure retain service in the event of a partial
outage. Grid modernization can improve a utility’s ability to gather data and respond to impacts.
Examples of grid modernization technologies include advanced metering infrastructure (AMI),
supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) systems, fault location isolation and
service restoration (FLISR), and remote facility control. Additional modernization technologies
are discussed in Chapter 6. Finally, investments in distributed energy resources, such as
backup generators and portable energy stations, can assist communities during long-lasting
power outages. 1

Outside of electricity system upgrades and operations, utilities can further aid in winter storm
resilience by through coordination and mutual aid agreements with other agencies and
formalizing employee training (e.g., Incident Command System training). Mutual aid agreements
can provide utilities with much-needed personnel and resources to restore service quickly and
efficiently. Both the NLR report and The Oregon Guidebook for Local Energy Resilience 2
discuss how utilities aid overall risk mitigation by establishing mutual aid agreements with other
utilities throughout the state, region, and country. Utilities can deploy Incident Command System
(ICS) training, which prepares staff to implement emergency response operations.

During Event Practices: Short-term operational strategies can reduce impacts and streamline
system recovery. NLR’s report on utility resilience planning for winter storms notes the
importance of maintaining an accurate outage map to inform customers. Other during-event
best practices include customer wellness checks (i.e., emergency outreach programs for
vulnerable customers like the elderly or medically fragile), use of backup generation (e.g.,
distribution of portable generators), and implementation of rotating outages. Utilities can record
vulnerable customers (see Section 3.4) and prioritize those customers for wellness checks,
communication, and power restoration. Portable backup generators and rotating outages can be
deployed in the case of inadequate generation or transmission outages.

Post-Event Recovery: After a winter storm, ongoing communication is essential to inform
customers about estimated restoration times and available resources.1, 2, 3 Development of
“Situation Reports”, often termed “After Action” reports, is another post-event best practice that
encourages utilities to assess their winter weather response. Situation reports help utilities
understand their strengths, weaknesses, and lessons learned to improve storm preparedness.

5.4.2 Mitigation Approaches Applied in Lane County
This section summarizes Lane County utility winter storm resilience practices across pre-event,
during-event, and post-event phases (Table 40, Table 41, and Table 42, respectively). Findings
are based on publicly available materials (e.g., after-action reports, capital improvement plans,

and utility websites) available for EWEB, 1,2,3,4,5 EPUD, 6,7 SUB, 8,9 Blachly-Lane, 10, 11 Lane
Electric, 12,13,14 and Central Lincoln. 15,16,17 Where a practice is marked “not documented,” it
indicates it was not identified in reviewed sources, not necessarily that the utility does not
perform the activity.

Pre-Event Winter Storm Best Practices Applied in Lane County

Across Lane County utilities, the most documented pre-event mitigation approaches are
vegetation management, hardening upgrades, undergrounding, and selective efforts to increase
system redundancy. The extent to which these strategies are formalized varies by utility. In
general, larger utilities appear to provide more explicit documentation of winter storm mitigation
activities, while smaller cooperatives more often reference related actions through broader
reliability or wildfire mitigation efforts rather than through winter storm specific planning.

 171

Vegetation Management: Among the utilities reviewed, vegetation management is one of the
most consistently documented strategies. EWEB, EPUD, Blachly-Lane, Lane Electric, and
Central Lincoln PUD all reference vegetation management in some form, reflecting its
importance as a frontline measure for reducing outages caused by falling limbs and tree contact
during ice and wind events. In several cases, however, vegetation management is described
more broadly as part of routine reliability or wildfire risk reduction rather than as a winter-storm-
specific strategy. This suggests that, in Lane County, vegetation management functions as a
cross-hazard resilience measure with benefits for both wildfire and winter storm risk.

System Hardening: EWEB provides one of the clearest examples of active winter storm
mitigation investment, having secured FEMA funding to support hardening upgrades and
targeted undergrounding projects intended to reduce damage from future ice storms. 1 EPUD
similarly identifies undergrounding and system enhancement as important resilience measures,
and its January 2024 ice storm post-event analysis notes that newer underground facilities
experienced comparatively little damage during that storm. 2 At the same time, EPUD also notes
that undergrounding is not feasible in all locations due to rocky soils and high costs, highlighting
a challenge likely relevant in other parts of the county as well. Central Lincoln PUD also
documents ongoing underground conversions, 3 while Lane Electric and Blachly-Lane reference
undergrounding or hardening more indirectly through wildfire-related mitigation activities that
may also improve winter storm resilience. 4, 5

Network Redundancy: Compared with vegetation management and undergrounding, network
redundancy is less uniformly documented but still appears in several utility materials. EWEB1
and Central Lincoln PUD3 identify redundancy as part of their resilience approach, and EPUD’s
January 2024 ice storm post-event analysis also points to increased redundancy as a needed
system improvement.2 By contrast, SUB’s after-action materials identify insufficient redundant
assets and backup capability as a key weakness during the ice storm response, 6,7 suggesting
that this remains an area of uneven implementation across Lane County utilities.

Grid Modernization: Other best practices appear less consistently documented. Grid
modernization is not commonly described as a winter-storm resilience strategy across utilities,
however the Central Lincoln PUD Hazard Mitigation Plan does include a mitigation action
focused on modernizing the transmission, substation, and distribution system infrastructure.3

Coordination and Mutual Aid Agreements: Mutual aid agreements are documented for
EWEB, 1 EPUD,2 SUB,6 and Central Lincoln PUD, 2 indicating that external restoration support is
an important regional resilience mechanism, particularly for severe events that overwhelm local
staffing capacity. As described in Section 5.4.2, these utilities are also part of the Western
Regional Mutual Assistance Agreement (WRMAA). 3

Employee Training: In contrast, formal Incident Command System employee training appears
to be less consistently embedded. EWEB1 documents active ICS-related preparedness, while
EPUD2 and SUB6 both identify ICS-related refinements or training gaps in their post-event
analyses. This suggests that, while some utilities are using incident command structures during
storm response, the degree of formalization and training varies substantially.

Overall, pre-event mitigation in Lane County shows the strongest alignment around traditional
infrastructure and vegetation strategies, while more organizational and advanced resilience
practices, such as employee training, grid modernization, and formal redundancy planning,
appear less consistently documented across all utilities.

During-Event Winter Storm Best Practices Applied in Lane County

Live Outage Maps: During winter storm events, the most consistently documented utility
practices in Lane County are customer outage maps. EWEB, SUB, Blachly-Lane, Lane Electric,
and Central Lincoln PUD all provide outage maps or online outage information, and EPUD

1 Eugene Water & Electric Board, “The Big Freeze 2024: After Action Report,” https://www.eweb.org/your-

public-utility/news/the-big-freeze-2024-after-action-report
2 Central Lincoln People’s Utility District, “Public Power Helping Public Power,”

https://clpud.org/2026/01/public-power-helping-public-power/
3 “Western Region Mutual Assistance Agreement for Electric and Natural Gas Utilities,” 2003.

identified development of a customer-facing outage map as a planned improvement following
the ice storm that occurred in January 2024. 1 This suggests that real-time or near-real-time
outage visibility is becoming a baseline customer service expectation across Lane County
utilities. At the same time, after-action reporting indicates that simply having an outage map is
not always sufficient; performance, accuracy, and restoration detail remain important issues.
EWEB, for example, identified the need to improve outage map functionality following the
January 2024 ice storm. 2 This points to a broader implementation gap between the existence of
customer-facing tools and their effectiveness during high-impact events.

Wellness Checks, Backup Generation, and Rotating Outages: By comparison, more
resource-intensive practices such as wellness checks, backup generator deployment, and
rotating outages are much less commonly documented. Of the reviewed utilities, EWEB
documentation of the 2024 ice storm indicated that small portable emergency generators were
deployed to homes that house operators, and the utility’s liaison office worked with Lane County
Public Health on welfare checks during the storm. 3 These actions align closely with best
practices for supporting medically vulnerable customers and reducing hardship during
prolonged outages, but similar programs were not identified in publicly available materials for
the other utilities. This may indicate that these activities are less common, more limited in
scope, or coordinated through county or community partners rather than utility-led programs.
For example, Lane Council of Governments Senior & Disability Services reports it conducts
proactive risk assessments and emergency outreach to case-managed medically vulnerable
residents, coordinating support and referrals through the Emergency Operations Center and the
state Emergency Response System. Rotating outages were not mentioned in the reviewed
utility documents. This absence is notable but not surprising, as the most severe winter storm
impacts in Lane County appear to be driven primarily by physical damage to distribution
infrastructure rather than by system-wide generation shortages requiring managed load
shedding. As a result, during-event utility practice in Lane County is centered more on damage
response and outage communication than on demand-management measures.

Taken together, these patterns suggest that during-event practice across Lane County utilities is
strongest around customer outage awareness, but less developed or publicly documented in
direct customer support interventions for prolonged outages.

Post-Event Winter Storm Best Practices Applied in Lane County

Ongoing Communication: Most of the reviewed utilities document some form of post-event
customer communication, whether through websites, social media, alerts, news media, or radio.
EWEB, EPUD, SUB, Blachly-Lane, and Lane Electric all provide evidence of at least some
communication mechanisms to keep customers informed during or after outages, while Central
Lincoln PUD’s communication appears to rely more narrowly on customers checking utility
social media or website updates. 1 Across utilities, communication challenges emerge as a
recurring theme. EWEB’s after-action report identified failures in its text message update system
and noted the need for clearer messaging about repair priorities and improved outreach to
upriver customers. 2 EPUD and SUB similarly identified communication as an area needing
improvement, with SUB specifically noting that communication lacked adequate frequency,
format, and detail during the event. 3 These findings suggest that while customer communication
is widely recognized as essential, major winter storms continue to expose weaknesses in
message delivery systems and public-facing restoration information.

After Action Reporting & Performance Tracking: The use of formal after-action reports is
inconsistent. EWEB, 4 EPUD, 5 and SUB 6 all produced post-event documentation that assessed
response performance and identified lessons learned and improvement actions. In contrast, no
after-action report was identified in reviewed materials for Blachly-Lane, Lane Electric, or
Central Lincoln PUD. While the absence of a publicly available report does not necessarily
mean that no internal review occurred, it does suggest a less visible or less formalized post-

utilities move beyond immediate restoration and into longer-term resilience improvement.

Overall, post-event practices in Lane County show broad recognition of the importance of
customer communication but also reveal persistent communication shortcomings during major
outages. Meanwhile, formal after-action assessment appears to be an area where
implementation is less consistent across utilities, with larger or more highly resourced utilities
appearing more likely to document lessons learned and identify follow-up actions.

5.4.3 Gaps and Opportunities
Overall, Lane County utilities appear to implement several foundational winter storm resilience
practices, particularly vegetation management, selective undergrounding, and outage
communication (i.e., maps and customer updates). However, implementation is uneven across
utilities, and more advanced or formalized practices are less consistently documented. There
are several common gaps, which suggest opportunities for improvement and collaboration:
 Standardized winter storm resilience planning could help utilities and customers
 understand what mitigation, response, and recovery measures are in place.
 Redundancy, backup power, and operational coordination appear to be uneven across
  utilities and may warrant additional planning, particularly for prolonged outages and services
  to critical populations.
 Standardized after-action reporting and communication practices could help utilities
 evaluate storm response more consistently and improve customer-facing information during
 future events.

5.5 Extreme Heat
The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) reported that 2014 to 2024 have
been the warmest years on record, which spans 1850 to 2024. 1 This trend holds true for Lane
County, which has experienced an average warming of approximately 0.03°F per year over the
last 46 years. Lane County’s average annual temperature is projected to continue growing at an

1 NOAA National Centers for Environmental Information, “Global Climate Report – Annual 2024 (Global

Monthly Climate Report 202413)” https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/access/monitoring/monthly-
report/global/202413

 176

increasing rate, 1 with an average warming of approximately 0.07°F per year projected over the
next 73 years (Figure 62 and Figure 63).

Extreme heat can have a several different effects on utility assets. Because the power system is
designed to operate within certain thermal limits, extreme heat can cause protective equipment
to trip, leading to grid faults or component damage. Extreme heat can reduce generation,
transmission, or distribution equipment efficiency, and may require that utilities derate—that is,
reduce the available capacity of—equipment. 2 Generation is less efficient when extreme heat
events occur. Powerplants that rely on water for cooling lose efficiency as cooling-water
temperatures rise, and solar panel efficiency declines if the ambient temperature is too hot. 3
Higher ambient temperatures increase the vibration of the electrons, which slows their transport
in powerlines and leads to more energy loss. Heat also limits the ability of equipment to shed
internally generated heat, lowering the safe operating capacity of equipment. 4 Overall, a Los
Angeles County grid vulnerability assessment found that most grid components were projected
to experience between 2 and 20% capacity loss as air temperatures exceed 104 °F. Damage to
utility equipment from extreme heat is covered in detail by Guddanti et al., (2024). 5

Primer.pdf

Climate Variability,” May 2023.
https://www.pnnl.gov/sites/default/files/media/file/Final%20Report%206_7_2023.pdf

Disrupt the Power Grid,” Jul. 2024. https://www.pnnl.gov/news-media/extreme-heat-hurricanes-wildfires-
how-summers-extremes-disrupt-power-grid

VULNERABILITY TO EXTREME HEAT”, Aug. 2018.

5.5.1 Best Practices
Best practices for utility extreme heat resilience were synthesized primarily from a PNNL report
that compiles best practices for forecasting, resource planning, asset planning, and contingency
planning under climate variability; 1 a California Energy Commission-funded technical
assessment of Los Angeles County’s grid vulnerability to extreme heat; 2 an extreme heat
playbook produced by the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development; 3 and a recent
newsletter article publication, which discusses demand-side strategies to manage heat-driven
load and reduce heat stress on infrastructure. 4 Figure 64 lists specific measures and activities
that are considered best practices for utility extreme heat resilience at each phase of a disaster.

 179

Pre-Event Mitigation: A range of pre-event best practices can be used by utilities to reduce
heat exposure and system stress before an event occurs. Equipment cooling systems can
prevent damage to heat sensitive critical utility infrastructure assets (i.e., degraded performance
or reduced capacity) improved. The California Energy Commission report notes that substations
can be adapted with improved heat sinks, forced air, or water-cooling systems.2

System hardening approaches are another best practice utilities can use to prepare
infrastructure for extreme heat events. Critical equipment (i.e., poles, conductors, transformers,
substation equipment) can be upgraded or replaced to meet higher design standards including
broader temperature ratings.1

Another mitigation approach is mitigating electricity demand surges during extreme heat events.
To lessen energy burden during extreme heat events caused by the corresponding increased
use of mechanical cooling (e.g., air conditioning), changes to land use and building construction
approaches could be considered. Examples of heat-resilient building and land practices include
the use of reflective materials, enhanced insulation, passive cooling techniques, and urban tree
canopy cover.3 These interventions are not inherently utility driven and are most effective when
urban planners, utilities, and governmental entities collaborate.4 Another approach to reducing
the electricity demand during an extreme heat event is through energy efficiency
improvements,2 which utilities can support through targeted programs like audits, retrofits, and
HVAC upgrades.1, 3 Weatherization programs and heat pump installations can also mitigate
health impacts from extreme heat by keeping homes cool, while reducing energy
consumption. 1, 2

Use of energy efficiency and distributed energy strategies can also prevent electric grid
overload during extreme heat events. These non-wire alternatives include decentralized
generation, microgrids, virtual power plants, and increased storage as approaches to reduce
reliance on long transmission paths that are susceptible to extreme heat impacts. 3, 4, 5

Before an extreme heat event, utilities can also form regional cooperation and grid-coordination
agreements to rapidly share capacity by exporting power from unaffected, lower-demand and
high-supply areas to the heat-impacted regions in the case of an extreme heat event. 6

Like many of the other natural hazard events that pose risks to utilities, employee trainings are a
best practice that mitigates extreme heat risk prior to the event. Utility personnel should conduct
emergency training, drills, and preparedness education to ensure field and operations staff can
respond safely and effectively during heat-drive system stress.4

1 Oak Ridge National Laboratory, “Health and Household-Related Benefits Attributable to the

Weatherization Assistance Program,” Sep. 2014. https://weatherization.ornl.gov/wp-
content/uploads/pdf/WAPRetroEvalFinalReports/ORNL_TM-2014_345.pdf
2 Rocky Mountain Institute, “Why Heat Pumps Are the Answer to Heat Waves,” Aug. 2021.

https://rmi.org/why-heat-pumps-are-the-answer-to-heat-waves/
3 Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, ibid.
4 Virtual Peaker, “Using Virtual Power Plants To Meet Demand During Heat Waves,” Jul. 2025.

https://www.pnnl.gov/sites/default/files/media/file/Final%20Report%206_7_2023.pdf
5 PreventionWeb, “Sharing Risk to Avoid Power Outages in an Era of Extreme Weather,” Aug. 2024.

https://virtual-peaker.com/blog/using-virtual-power-plants-to-meet-demand-during-heat-waves/
6 The American Society of Mechanical Engineers, “Preparing the Grid to Deal with the Heat,” Nov. 2017.

https://www.preventionweb.net/news/sharing-risk-avoid-power-outages-era-extreme-weather

 180

During Event Practices: During an event, demand response programs can alleviate heat-
driven grid strain by encouraging temporary reduced use of electricity for nonessential purposes
during peak heat periods.4,1

The use of enhanced situational awareness and monitoring is a during-event best practice
outlined in PNNL’s Emerging Best Practices for Electric Utility Planning with Climate Variability
report. Tools such as geographic information systems (GIS), distribution system supervisory
control and data acquisition (DSCADA), fault location, isolation and service restoration
systems (FLISR), volt-var optimization (VVO), voltage stabilization (e.g., SVC STATCOM),
outage management system, and network monitoring devices allow utilities to quickly identify
stressed system parts in real time, shift or reduce load, stabilize voltage during demand spikes,
detect failures, and isolate problems.4

When experiencing extreme heat events, utilities should adjust operations for the high-
temperature conditions, which often coincide with peak loads and reduced equipment
performance. For example, in anticipation of the increased demand for electricity, a utility may
elect to limit the length of a planned outage during high heat weather or cancel planned
maintenance when temperatures exceed certain thresholds. 2

Like other hazards, utilities can record vulnerable customers and prioritize those customers for
wellness checks, communication, and power restoration.

Post-Event Recovery: The PNNL’s Emerging Best Practices for Electric Utility Planning with
Climate Variability report outlines several post-event best practices.4 One of which, is inspection
and restoration through the use of an asset damage assessment to document what occurred,
quantify impacts, and identify system weaknesses revealed by the heat event. Utilities are also
encouraged to share lessons learned from extreme heat events to support smaller and rural
utilities’ ability to navigate these hazards through after-action reporting and performance
tracking. Lastly, after an extreme heat event, utilities should reassess system vulnerabilities and
upgrade priorities by comparing observed impacts (e.g., outages, load, equipment performance)
against prior assumptions and then update risk assessments and corresponding upgrade
priorities.

5.5.2 Mitigation Approaches Applied in Lane County
This section summarizes how Lane County-area electric utilities apply extreme heat best
practices across the pre-event, during-event, and post-event phases (Table 43, Table 44,

Pre-Event Extreme Heat Best Practices Applied in Lane County

System Hardening: System hardening and equipment upgrades are documented by EPUD,
SUB, Blachly-Lane, and Central Lincoln PUD in their respective strategic plans and natural
hazard mitigation plans. The majority of hardening projects are undertaken to increase
earthquake and winter storm resilience, but actions such as conductor upgrades can add
resilience for extreme heat events as well. However, other potential upgrades specific to
extreme heat concerns are not well-documented.

Energy Efficiency & Distributed Energy: All Lane County utilities offer rebate programs for
residential energy efficiency measures, including weatherization, heat pumps, and appliances.
EPUD, EWEB, Blachly-Lane, and Central Lincoln PUD provide rebate programs for commercial,
industrial, and agricultural customers. Such programs can help reduce electricity demand from
air conditioning and refrigeration during heat waves. EPUD and SUB offer free energy audits for
their residential customers. EWEB and EPUD describe plans and efforts to bring local
generation sources, such as residential solar, into their systems. Central Lincoln PUD also

preparedness training to staff through FEMA’s Incident Command System curriculum. Central
Lincoln PUD participates in interagency tabletop exercises on seasonal weather hazards,
although it is not clear whether extreme heat is an included hazard. Other utilities have not
publicly documented staff training initiatives.

There was no publicly available indication of use of the post-event best practices across Lane

5.5.3 Gaps and Opportunities
Overall, Lane County utilities appear to implement several foundational extreme heat best
practices, particularly grid hardening and equipment upgrades, energy efficiency programs, and
grid monitoring and automation tools. However, these actions are rarely framed or documented
as extreme heat mitigation, and extreme heat is not consistently identified as a priority hazard in
utility planning. For example, in Volume II of the Lane County NHMP, the utility-focused hazard
lists for Blachly-Lane Electric Co-op, Consumers Power Inc., and EPUD include winter storms,
wildfires, windstorms, earthquakes, volcanoes, and floods, but do not include extreme heat. 1 As
a result, current efforts that may incidentally improve extreme heat resilience may not be
targeted, tracked, or scaled to match projected extreme heat risk. These gaps suggest several
opportunities for improvement and collaboration:
 Utilities could assess their risk to extreme heat-related vulnerabilities (e.g., peak-load
 stress, equipment temperature limits) and incorporate extreme heat into hazard mitigation
 plans, emergency plans, and capital planning.

5.6 Digital and Physical Asset Security
Utilities increasingly face security challenges regarding physical and digital assets. Common
cyberattacks such as phishing, ransomware, man-in-the-middle attacks, and wiper attacks can
leak sensitive information to bad actors and breach protected systems. Upgrades to smart grid
technologies, pursued by many utilities to improve service, can introduce further risk through
inherent cyber vulnerabilities and require appropriate management. 2 Physical attacks on utility
assets are also a concern, with DOE reporting at least 175 instances of physical attacks or

1 “Lane County Multi-Jurisdictional Natural Hazard Mitigation Plan,” Lane County, Oregon, Version 4,

2023. https://cdnsm5-
hosted.civiclive.com/UserFiles/Servers/Server_3585797/Image/Government/County%20Departments/Em
ergency%20Management/Planning%20Page/Lane%20County%20MNHMP%20V4_Full%20Plan%20(2).
pdf
2 Kansas Legislative Research Department, “Grid Security,” Dec. 2024. https://klrd.gov/2024/12/18/grid-

security/

 185

threats against critical grid infrastructure in 2024. 1 Critical technology infrastructure, such as
information technology and operational technology, exist to ensure the protection of private
information and reliable operation of assets. However, technologies must be combined with a
variety of best practices to ensure the security of utility assets and service.

5.6.1 Best Practices
The North American Electricity Reliability Corporation (NERC) Critical Infrastructure Protection
Roadmap establishes baseline requirements for utilities on topics of physical and cyber
security. 2 Started in 2005, the Critical Infrastructure Protection Roadmap continues to evolve.
Currently, it provides guidance to utilities on twelve infrastructure protection requirements,
covering topics such as personnel training, security controls, management of physical and
electronic access, incident reporting, and more.

Many physical and cybersecurity best practices are also provided by the American Public Power
Association (APPA), 3 the DOE Office of Cybersecurity, Energy Security, and Emergency
Response (CESER), 4 and the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners
(NARUC). 5 Common risk mitigation efforts listed by the APPA include employee training,
encryption and VPN usage, multifactor authentication, and continuous monitoring. 6 Figure 65
lists specific measures and activities that are considered best practices for cyber and physical
asset security.

Pre-Event Mitigation: Preparation is key to reducing vulnerabilities in both physical and digital
infrastructure. Utilities should use cyber hygiene practices, such as asset identification,
configuration management, defensible network topologies, vulnerability management,
disciplined patching (see the NERC Critical Infrastructure Protection Roadmap for more details).
Other high-priority mitigation approaches recommended by NERC include: use of multifactor
authentication, protection of public network communications, and ongoing risk monitoring for
areas with large loads, such as distributed energy resource aggregators and electric vehicle
supply equipment. In APPA’s Cybersecurity Resource Guide for Public Power Utilities, the first
recommendation is to develop an organizational understanding of the cybersecurity risks
associated with the business context.

Other best practices include: asset risk assessment, development and sharing of organizational
cybersecurity policies and procedures, and establishment of organizational cybersecurity roles
and responsibilities.3

1 National Rural Utilities Cooperative Finance Corporation, “Physical Security Concerns Grow Across the

Utility Industry,” May 2023. https://www.nrucfc.coop/content/solutions/en/stories/energy-tech/physical-
security-concerns-grow-across-the-utility-industry.html
2 North American Electric Reliability Corporation, “NERC Critical Infrastructure Protection Roadmap,” Jan.

2026. https://www.nerc.com/globalassets/our-work/reports/special-
reports/nerc_cip_roadmap_01122026.pdf

https://www.publicpower.org/resource/cybersecurity-resource-guide-public-power-utilities
4 U.S. Department of Energy, “Cybersecurity,” Office of Cybersecurity, Energy Security, and Emergency

Response (CESER). https://www.energy.gov/ceser/cybersecurity
5 National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners, “Cybersecurity for Utility Regulators.”

https://www.naruc.org/core-sectors/critical-infrastructure-and-cybersecurity/cybersecurity-for-utility-
regulators/
6 American Public Power Association, “Common Cyber Risks and How to Mitigate Them.” Oct. 2025.

https://www.publicpower.org/periodical/article/common-cyber-risks-and-how-mitigate-them

 186

APPA’s Cybersecurity Resource Guide for Public Power Utilities 1 recommends protecting at-risk
assets through use of the following best practices: maintaining and controlling audit logs and
supporting artifacts, encrypting sensitive data during transit and at rest, frequently testing,
updating, and automating security software updates, advocating for cybersecurity professional
development, training, and awareness, using security software, regularly backing up and storing
data, and generating formal policies for electronic file and device disposal. Finally, after
identifying and protecting, APPA’s Cybersecurity Resource Guide for Public Power Utilities
recommends detection best practices: monitoring for unauthorized access and investigating all
unusual network activities.

1 American Public Power Association, ibid.
2 Information from American Public Power Association, ibid.

 187

NERC established a reliability standard (CIP-014-3) 1 that outlines requirements applicable to
utilities seeking to implement physical security best practices. Four requirements apply in the
pre-event mitigation stage: (1) perform periodic physical-attack risk assessments for applicable
transmission assets (e.g., substations and primary control centers); (2) obtain unaffiliated third-
party verification of the risk assessment; (3) conduct a threat and vulnerability evaluation that
considers facility-specific characteristics, prior attack history, and threat warnings and
intelligence from sources such as law enforcement; and (4) develop and implement a
documented physical security plan that includes measures designed to deter, detect, delay,
assess, communicate, and respond, including coordination with law enforcement and an
execution timeline.

During Event Practices: APPA’s Cybersecurity Resource Guide for Public Power Utilities1
offers recommendations for responding to a detected cybersecurity incident: investigating and
isolating the attack, testing and planning for continuity of operations, reporting incidents and
attacks to applicable authorities, notifying stakeholders of data losses or data risks, and
preparing for additional events that put data at risk. These align with the best practices for
physical security events; the DOE’s Energy Sector-Specific Plan highlights the importance of
rapidly identifying, assessing, and responding to cascading effects during incidents.

Post-Event Recovery: APPA’s Cybersecurity Resource Guide for Public Power Utilities also
offers several tips related to response and recovery from cybersecurity incidents including:
regularly updating cybersecurity policies and capturing lessons learned, keeping spare parts
and equipment affected by an event, managing public relations, and continually informing
stakeholders of all response and recovery activities. Similarly, for physical security events, the
DOE’s Energy Sector-Specific Plan highlights the importance of learning after incidents have
occurred; as such, similar updates to any established risk assessment, threat and vulnerability
evaluation, and physical security plan are also recommended.

5.6.2 Mitigation Approaches Applied in Lane County
This section summarizes how Lane County–area electric utilities apply digital and material asset
security best practices across the pre-event, during-event, and post-event phases (Table 46,
Table 47, and Table 48, respectively). Findings are based on publicly available materials (e.g.,
strategic plans, memos, and emergency operations plans) available for EWEB, 2 EPUD, 3 and
SUB. 4,5 Where a practice is marked “not documented,” it indicates it was not identified in
reviewed sources, not necessarily that the utility does not perform the activity.

Pre-Event Digital and Material Asset Security Best Practices Applied in Lane
County

The degree of publicly available documentation generally correlates with utility size, with larger
utilities providing more detailed descriptions of their cybersecurity posture and smaller utilities
providing little specific information. Lane Electric, Central Lincoln PUD, and Blachly-Lane do not
discuss cybersecurity or physical security initiatives in their public-facing materials.

Asset Management: Asset inventory is documented by EWEB and EPUD. EWEB describes
multi-year efforts to categorize data and maintain it within an accurate system inventory as part
of broader cyber and data asset security work. EPUD references a survey of system assets as
part of its operational planning. No comparable inventory efforts were identified in publicly
available materials for the remaining utilities.

Create Organizational Cybersecurity Policies: Cybersecurity policy development is
documented by EWEB and EPUD. EWEB reports that an enhanced cybersecurity policy is
being finalized to establish safeguards and guidelines for all staff. EPUD commits to
implementing staff training and new security technology to protect data integrity and
confidentiality. SUB's Emergency Operations Plan (EOP) does not describe cybersecurity-
specific policies, though it identifies cyber-attack as a covered hazard within its broader
emergency management framework.

Establish Organizational Roles and Responsibilities: Cybersecurity roles and
responsibilities are the most broadly documented pre-event practice, with evidence from EWEB,
EPUD, and SUB. EWEB describes plans to develop training on cyber and data security
expectations to ensure all employees understand the risks and their role in mitigating them.
EPUD has increased its internal IT capabilities in recent years, including adding a separate
department with experienced staff dedicated to IT and cybersecurity functions. SUB's EOP
defines a detailed command structure with assigned roles and responsibilities, including
positions such as Incident Commander, Safety Officer, and Liaison Officer, that apply across all
covered hazards including cyber attack. However, none of the three utilities document
cybersecurity-specific roles in their publicly available materials.

Encrypt and Backup Data: Data encryption and backup are documented by EWEB and EPUD.
EWEB describes migrating sensitive data and backup capabilities to fully redundant cloud
environments, adding data center redundancy, and using Multi-Factor Authentication. EPUD's
strategic plan describes a move toward cloud-based technologies to ensure system continuity.

Identify Risks to Assets: EWEB describes considerable focus on identifying and mitigating
organizational risk across information systems infrastructure, applications, and data security.

Update Security Software: EWEB is also the only utility to document the use of security
software updates; a memo describes ongoing monitoring of aging software and infrastructure
and a pivot toward creation of an enhanced cybersecurity program.

Employee Training: EWEB and EPUD document the importance of cybersecurity training to
educate staff on the risks and mitigation strategies associated with new security technology.

Monitor for Vulnerabilities: SUB is the only utility to discuss vulnerability monitoring; the
utility’s EOP describes prevention activities including improved surveillance and security, as well

 189

as protection activities involving awareness of threats and vulnerabilities to critical facilities and
the expansion of voluntary security-related information sharing between government agencies.

Physical Attack Risk Assessment, Threat Evaluation, and Security Plan: Physical security
practices are documented by EWEB and SUB. EWEB maintains a Physical Security Program,
which suggests the presence of associated risk assessments, threat evaluations, and security
plans. SUB's EOP addresses physical security through its prevention and protection
frameworks, including deterrence operations, inspections, investigations to determine the nature
and source of threats, and continuity of operations planning.

There was no publicly available indication of best practices, Maintain Audit Logs and
Standardize Procedures for File/Device Disposal. These practices may be documented
internally by the utilities.

During Event Digital and Material Asset Security Best Practices Applied in Lane
County

During-event practices are minimally documented across Lane County utilities. Of the five
during-event best practices identified in the literature, only two appear in reviewed materials:

Test and Plan for Continued Operations: EWEB describes business continuity planning that
includes practicing data and systems recovery from backup tools and maintaining tested plans
to operate without technology. EPUD's strategic plan states that secondary systems will be in
place to quickly take over in the event of site loss, reflecting a focus on continuity through
infrastructure redundancy, though it does not describe testing of these capabilities.

Report Incident to Authorities: SUB’s EOP, while not specific to cyber-or physical-security
attacks, assigns the Safety Officer and Liaison Officer responsibility for required notifications to
OSHA, the Oregon Public Utility Commission, the Oregon Emergency Response System, and
other government agencies. While not discussed in utility documents, the federal Cyber Incident
Reporting for Critical Infrastructure Act (CIRCIA) directs the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure
Security Agency (CISA) to require covered critical-infrastructure entities (potentially including
utilities) to report covered cyber incidents to CISA generally within 72 hours (and certain ransom
payments within 24 hours), once CISA’s implementing regulations take effect. 1

Notify Stakeholder of Data Losses or Risks: Similar to the best practices on reporting
incidents to authorities, utility documentation does not outline how customers would be notified
of potential data losses or risks, but the Oregon Consumer Information Protection Act (OCIPA)
generally requires entities to provide a notice of a qualifying breach of security involving
personal information to affected Oregon consumers without unreasonable delay and within the
state’s required 45 day timeframe). It is assumed that all utilities are aware of this
responsibility. 2

There was no publicly available indication of use of two of the during event mitigation best
practices, Investigate and Isolate the Attack and Prepare for Related Data Risk Events by
any of the utilities.

5.6.3 Gaps and Opportunities
Similar to earthquake and winter storm hazard mitigation, with the information available, it is
difficult to evaluate gaps in utilities’ digital and asset security risk mitigation strategies. That said,
because cyber and physical security threats are human-caused, it may be that utilities do not
share information about their mitigation strategies publicly. Initial opportunities for improvement
include:
 Utilities could expand their implemented digital and asset security risk mitigation
 strategies to consider what to do after the incident.
 Regional coordination or resource sharing could help smaller utilities adequately plan for
  digital and asset security risks.

 193

 Chapter 6
 Available Technologies for Grid
 Modernization
 Amanda Krelling and Kaiyu Sun

Enhancing grid resilience can be supported by a portfolio of solutions that strengthen
physical assets, improve operational flexibility, and build the utility’s capacity to
anticipate and respond to disruptions. Utilities across Lane County are at varying stages
of modernization, depending on their size, resources, and regional risks. In this section,
to support decision-making and planning, we present a suite of technologies available to
utilities for improving reliability and resilience.

The technologies and strategies presented in this section are organized into four groups
(Figure 66). Technology groups do not prescribe a strict order of implementation. Instead, they
provide a general framework for how technologies relate and enable one another. For example,
some of the items in Group 1 list provide the foundational communication needed for many of
the technologies in Groups 2 and beyond. Similarly, remote devices from Group 2 can be
altered to provide the automatic operation discussed in Group 3. Much of the data, visibility,
automation and control that is provided by items in Groups 1–3 allows the integrated grid
management of Group 4. A utility can benefit from technologies incrementally, deploying them in
specific areas or at specific times to suit their needs.

 Figure 66: Groups of Technologies to Enhance Grid Resilience
 Part II: Lane County Utilities Preface

 194

Table 49 summarizes the technologies and solutions available within each technology group.
The table will help utilities quickly determine:
 Which technologies are already in place.
 What operational capabilities technologies provide.
 What additional upgrades could enhance system resilience.
 Which technologies must be implemented first, based on prerequisite dependencies.

The technology dependencies presented in Table 49 reflect typical implementation pathways.
However, actual requirements may vary depending on the level of system complexity,
integration, and operational objectives. Some technologies can be deployed in a standalone or
limited configuration with fewer prerequisites, while more advanced, fully integrated
implementation may require additional foundational systems. Utilities should therefore interpret
these dependencies as indicative rather than absolute and consider their specific system
architecture and deployment goals when evaluating technology sequencing.

 Solution Software

The following subsections examine each group in detail, summarizing the benefits, challenges,
and implementation considerations associated with the corresponding technologies.

6.1 Group 1: Foundational Operations and Hardening
This group focuses on strengthening distribution system infrastructure and establishing the
digital tools needed for effective system management. Utilities commonly invest in grid
infrastructure hardening measures to reduce the likelihood of equipment damage from storms,
wildfires, or other events. In addition, strategically implementing hardening measures can help
address the challenges of aging infrastructure. These actions can lower the frequency of
outages and decrease restoration time and repair costs when events do occur. Hardening
efforts are typically prioritized in areas vulnerable to environmental hazards or where critical
facilities depend on uninterrupted service.

 Group 1: Foundational Operations and Hardening

implementation challenges.

 Group 1: Foundational Operations and Hardening

 197

Grid hardening can include undergrounding electric lines, installing spacer cable or covered
conductors, and replacing poles. These efforts can make the grid more resilient to extreme
weather and other physical hazards.

In parallel to hardening efforts, utilities can begin digitizing their asset information by
implementing GIS and, in some cases, substation-level metering and SCADA systems. A digital
inventory of assets allows for better planning, maintenance tracking, and coordination with
future data systems such as SCADA and OMS.

Substation metering provides real-time data on voltage, current, and power flows, helping
utilities identify equipment loading, performance trends, and early signs of faults or voltage
irregularities. When paired with a basic SCADA platform, this information can be transmitted
directly to a control center, enabling operators to visualize the operating condition of
substations, monitor breaker status, and respond faster to abnormal events. These early digital
investments provide the visibility, situational awareness, and data organization required to build
more advanced automation and control capabilities in subsequent phases. However, to support
these new control capabilities, utilities must also strengthen their communication networks,
ensuring that substations, field devices, and control centers can exchange data quickly and
reliably.

6.1.2 Regional Experiences with Technologies
Several large investor-owned and regional utilities have publicly reported multi-year
performance data on conductor hardening measures. Utilities such as Pacific Gas and Electric
Company (PG&E), Southern California Edison (SCE), San Diego Gas & Electric (SDG&E),
Pacific Power (PacifiCorp), Bear Valley Electric Service (BVES), and Liberty Utilities have
demonstrated the effectiveness of specific hardening measures in reducing outage risks.
According to their experience, wire insulation provides a cost-effective alternative to
undergrounding (about half the cost) but reduces wildfire risk by 60% to 90% compared to up to
99% for underground systems. These utilities have often adopted covered conductors as an
intermediate solution to areas where undergrounding is impractical or expensive. 1 Pacific Power
also reported that circuits upgraded with spacer cable systems achieved up to a 90% reduction
in vegetation- and animal-related outages between 2007 and 2022 in high-risk areas. 2

Pacific Power has launched a targeted and expansive grid hardening initiative designed to
reduce risks. 3 The program prioritizes replacing overhead lines in locations where wildfire
ignitions could have especially severe impacts, using fire-resistant components such as covered
conductors and undergrounded circuits, and substituting wooden poles with steel and composite
poles. For example, in the Grants Pass area, 4 the utility is rebuilding over 500 miles of
distribution lines across multiple segments. In the “Riverbanks” segment alone, 70 miles of bare
conductors will be replaced with tree-wire and 1,550 wooden poles with steel poles. These

1 U.S. Department of Energy Grid Deployment Office, “Utility Wires Investments”, Sept. 2024.

https://www.energy.gov/sites/default/files/2024-11/111524_Utility_Wires_Investments.pdf
2 PacifiCorp. “2022 WMP Update Progress Report”, 2022.

https://www.pacificorp.com/content/dam/pcorp/documents/en/pacificorp/wildfire-
mitigation/2022_WMP_Update_Attachment_6_CC_Effectiveness_Workstream_R0.pdf
3 Pacific Power. “Strengthening our system, reducing risks”. https://www.pacificpower.net/outages-

safety/wildfire-safety/reducing-risks.html
4 Pacific Power. “Grants Pass system resiliency”. https://www.pacificpower.net/outages-safety/wildfire-

safety/reducing-risks/grants-pass.html

 Group 1: Foundational Operations and Hardening

 198

projects involve collaboration with local agencies and federal land management partners and
are partially funded through DOE grants.

Reported costs for grid hardening and conductor upgrade programs vary significantly depending
on the project scope. Based on recent utility findings and pilot projects, installation of covered
conductors and spacer cable systems typically ranges from approximately $200K per mile to
$1,350K per mile, with more comprehensive proposals reaching up to $2,140K per mile.1 Pacific
Power’s pilot projects indicate costs from roughly $200K per mile 1 to $700K per mile, while
Liberty Utilities reports approximately $1,220K per mile for covered conductor installations. 2
From SCE’s experience, inspection and vegetation management activities are substantially
lower, ranging from $0.12 per transmission line mile to $52.7 per distribution line mile,
depending on line type and inspection method. 3 Actual costs are highly site-specific and depend
on factors such as pole condition, span length, terrain, access constraints, labor markets,
wildfire risk classification, and whether projects are bundled with broader system upgrades.1

6.2 Group 2: Visibility and Monitoring
Building on the foundation of physical hardening and basic digital mapping, the next group
focuses on enhancing situational awareness and operational visibility across the distribution
system. These technologies allow utilities to see, measure, and track grid conditions in real
time, enabling a faster and more informed response to outages and performance issues.

6.2.1 Technology Benefits and Challenges
Key upgrades in this group include line sensors and smart fault indicators, which automatically
detect and locate faults along feeders, and Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI), which
provides two-way communication with customer meters for near-real-time usage and outage
data. When integrated with an Outage Management System (OMS), AMI data helps visualize
where and when outages occur, allowing operators to confirm customer impacts and prioritize
restoration. At this stage, some utilities also begin deploying remote-controlled switches and
reclosers, not yet for full automation but to extend visibility and enable manual remote operation
through SCADA or radio control. These devices serve as a bridge between monitoring and
automation, allowing operators to isolate faults and restore service faster even before advanced
automation systems are in place.

Table 51 summarizes the key technologies introduced in this group, highlighting their primary
benefits and implementation challenges.

6.2.2 Regional Experience with Technologies
Emerald PUD’s reliability program offers a clear example of how Group 2 technologies can
significantly strengthen distribution system performance. By deploying reclosers equipped with
integrated voltage sensors on all six bushings, EPUD gains detailed, real-time visibility into
feeder conditions without relying on external Potential Transformers (PTs) or complex field
wiring. These devices provide continuous monitoring of voltage and current, enabling precise
fault characterization, improved protection settings, and more informed decision-making during
switching and outage events. The reclosers’ communication capabilities allow integration with
EPUD’s SCADA system, giving engineers centralized oversight of both field and substation
devices while supporting rapid remote diagnostics and event-log analysis after faults. 1 These
enhancements have reduced the need for manual inspections, shortened restoration times, and
improved overall situational awareness, which are important benefits for a small utility with
limited staff and a heavily forested service area.

6.3 Group 3: Remote Control and Automation
With technologies and strategies from this group, utilities progress from simply observing
system conditions to actively controlling and automating distribution operations. Building on the
visibility and data infrastructure developed in earlier stages, these equipment and software allow
utilities to respond to faults and changing grid conditions in near real time.

1 NOJA Power. “How Emerald PUD Improved their Network Reliability”, Aug. 2021.

https://www.nojapower.com.au/expertise/2021/how-EPUD-improved-their-network-reliability
