Automated sectionalizers and programmable reclosers enable feeders to detect and isolate
faults autonomously, clearing temporary disturbances and restoring power without human
intervention. These devices form the foundation of automated fault management and help
minimize outage duration and service interruptions.

Enhanced connectivity allows for the integration of systems such as OMS, SCADA, and GIS
into a single operational view, giving operators a unified platform to monitor and manage
network performance. At the same time, Asset Management Systems (AMS) collect and
analyze condition data from equipment sensors, allowing utilities to plan maintenance
proactively rather than reactively. Intelligent Electronic Devices (IEDs) extend local decision-
making by embedding protection logic directly into field equipment, enabling faster and more
precise control actions. Together, these technologies represent a transition from manual and
centralized operations to automated, coordinated control.

Deploying remote control and automation increases cybersecurity risks by expanding the utility’s
digital exposure. Networked reclosers, IEDs, and sensors can become entry points for
unauthorized access if communications are not properly secured, potentially allowing attackers
to disrupt operations, manipulate settings, disable protections, or obtain confidential data.
Mitigating these risks requires a risk-based cybersecurity approach that includes strong access
controls, secure network architecture, continuous monitoring, incident response planning, and
periodic reassessment as technologies and threats evolve. 1

1 U.S. Department of Energy. “Energy Sector Cybersecurity Framework Implementation Guidance,” Jan.

2015. https://www.energy.gov/ceser/articles/energy-sector-cybersecurity-framework-implementation-
guidance

 Group 3: Remote Control and Automation

 201

6.3.2 Regional Experience with Technologies
Avista Utilities services parts of eastern Washington, northern Idaho, and western Montana, the
utility has implemented advanced grid automation systems that integrate both remote-controlled
switching and automated outage detection. 1 Through its Spokane Smart Circuits Project, the
utility installed 36 midline reclosers and 124 smart switches connected via a Tropos wireless
mesh network, enabling remote fault detection, isolation, and power restoration without manual
field intervention. This system allows automatic power rerouting to minimize outage duration
and improve reliability. In the Pullman Smart Grid Demonstration Project, Avista expanded
these capabilities by linking 34 reclosers and 30 capacitor banks to a private broadband
communications network integrated with AMI. The AMI system enables real-time outage
detection and confirmation of service restoration.

6.4 Group 4: Integrated Management
By adopting technologies and strategies from this group, utilities evolve from operating
independent systems to managing them in a coordinated, data-driven, and integrated
environment. The focus shifts from responding to events to proactively optimizing system
performance and maintaining stability under a wider range of conditions. This group of
technologies enables utilities to merge information from multiple sources (e.g., operations,
assets, distributed resources, and customer endpoints) into a unified operational view, allowing
more efficient, flexible, and resilient grid management.

6.4.1 Technology Benefits and Challenges

A key component of this phase is the Advanced Distribution Management System (ADMS),
which integrates SCADA, OMS, and GIS data into a single platform for centralized monitoring
and control. The ADMS provides the operational environment needed to support a range of
advanced applications, such as FLISR and VVO. FLISR automatically detects and isolates
faults while restoring service to unaffected areas, while VVO continuously manages voltage and
reactive power to improve power quality and reduce energy losses. The addition of a
Distributed Energy Resource Management System (DERMS) further extends visibility and
control to distributed generation and storage, allowing coordinated operation of solar PV,
batteries, and electric vehicles as grid-supporting assets.

Utilities can also deploy front-of-the-meter battery systems at substations or feeder ends to
support grid balancing, provide emergency power, and enable black-start capabilities during
outages. Combined with microgrid controllers and islanding capability, these systems ensure
that critical facilities can remain powered even when disconnected from the main grid.

Reported costs for monitoring and control technologies such as ADMS, FLISR, and DERMS are
commonly expressed on a per-customer-account basis and vary depending on system scope,
included functionalities, and whether O&M costs are incorporated. 1 Based on recent utility
filings, standalone ADMS investments can range from approximately $14.3 2 to $19.9 3 per

Guide,” Sep. 2024. https://www.energy.gov/sites/default/files/2024-
11/111524_Monitoring_and_Control_Technologies.pdf

Minnesota Public Utilities Commission, Minneapolis, MN, Docket No. E002/M-21-694, 2021.
3 Long Island Power Authority (LIPA). “LIPA-PSEG Long Island 5-Year Strategic Roadmap (2023 to

 Group 4: Integrated Management

customer account, while DERMS investments range from approximately $10.7 1 to $14.8 per
customer account (excluding O&M costs). FLISR implementations have been reported at
approximately $32.4 per customer account. More comprehensive grid modernization programs
that bundle multiple technologies and supporting infrastructure show significantly higher costs,
ranging from approximately $206.3 2 to $280.0 3 per customer account. Actual costs depend on
factors such as system size, legacy IT integration requirements, communications upgrades,
cybersecurity enhancements, regulatory treatment, and the breadth of technologies deployed
under a given program.

6.4.2 Regional Experience with Technologies
An example of these advanced capabilities in practice is PG&E’s EPIC 3.11 multi-customer
microgrid demonstration at the Arcata-Eureka Airport. Developed in partnership with the
Redwood Coast Energy Authority (RCEA), the project integrates a 2.2-MW solar array and a 2.3
MW, 8.9-MWh battery system with a dedicated microgrid controller and SCADA interface
capable of supporting seamless transitions between grid-connected and islanded operation. The
microgrid supplies several critical facilities, including the regional airport and the U.S. Coast
Guard Air Station, and is designed to maintain nearly continuous renewable power during
extended outages.

Through real-time coordination between PG&E’s distribution control center and RCEA’s
generation assets, the project tests the interoperability of utility-owned infrastructure and third-
party-controlled DERs under both normal and emergency conditions. Beyond demonstrating
technical feasibility, the initiative also develops engineering standards, protection schemes,
testing protocols, and experimental tariffs needed to operate front-of-the-meter, multi-customer
microgrids. These foundational elements provide a replicable model for other utilities and
communities seeking to enhance local resilience through integrated microgrid solutions.

Part III: Community Energy Baseline Preface
The third and final part of this Handbook deals with community resources, vulnerabilities, goals,
and plans related to electricity and resilience. While utilities have a significant role in maintaining
the affordability, reliability, and resilience of the electric system, they are by no means the only
group responsible for and participating in such efforts. Developers, landowners, or residents
might choose to invest in local generation projects (Chapter 7), communities might prioritize
different buildings or neighborhoods for upgrades (Chapter 8), and local governments might
make plans, set goals, or enact policies that align with their vision for a good energy future
(Chapter 9). Even for changes that utilities can make, like integrating DERs or upgrading
infrastructure, there are local factors that may guide or constrain those changes. The following
three sections present some of those local factors that impact investor, community, local
government, and utility decisions about energy.

 Figure 67. Lane County with city logos.

 Part III: Community Energy Baseline Preface

 211

 Chapter 7
Local Electricity Generation Resource
 Availability
 Candace Briggs and Ian Hermanson

In this section, we estimate the technical and economic potential to generate electricity
from local Lane County energy resources. Local energy resources are naturally
occurring within Lane County, and include solar, wind, biomass, hydropower,
geothermal, and marine energy. Existing local Lane County energy generation facilities
are documented in Section 1.6.2.

Lane County might consider developing new utility- and small-scale generation facilities to
harness local energy resources. This analysis maps energy resource availability across Lane
County and outlines hypothetical local solar and wind project requirements and performance.
The information provided can be used as a quick reference when considering new projects. We
do not recommend specific sites or technologies and instead aim to present information that can
guide future study priorities.

Our analysis is limited to solar, wind, and biomass. Geothermal energy resources are not
considered technically feasible in Lane County, and new hydroelectric development is limited by
environmental and regulatory constraints. Marine energy technologies, including tidal and wave
energy, have not reached commercial maturity. While Lane County has existing natural gas
power plants, natural gas is an imported fuel that is not available locally, and therefore the
resource potential is not subject to geographical analysis.

 What metric do we use to compare generation types?
 Levelized cost of energy (LCOE), expressed as cost per unit electricity ($/kWh), is a
 summary metric used to compare the economic performance of different energy
 generation technologies. LCOE represents the discounted average cost per unit of
 electricity over a project’s life and is calculated by dividing the present value of all capital,
 fuel, and operation and maintenance costs by total lifetime generation. 1 While LCOE
 enables comparisons across technologies and is a useful tool for long-term planning and
 investment decisions, LCOE has well-documented limitations and does not represent
 costs for grid integration, metrics of system reliability or dispatchability, or environmental
 or social impacts of large-scale deployments.

1 U.S. Department of Energy, “Solar Radiation Basics”, https://www.energy.gov/eere/solar/solar-
radiation-basics

 212

NLR provides a variety of publicly available web applications to assess renewable energy
technology potential for a given location. We use these applications in our analyses.
 NLR’s PVWatts Calculator estimates electricity generation and cost for a potential PV
  installation. We used PVWatts to compare solar installations in central Eugene.
 NLR’s Electricity Annual Technology Baseline (ATB) provides energy technology cost and
  performance parameters projected through 2050 for a variety of scenarios. We used the
  ATB to estimate solar and wind technology LCOE based on Lane County’s resource.
 NLR’s System Advisor Model (SAM) is a techno-economic modeling tool that estimates the
  performance, financial viability, and levelized cost of energy of renewable energy projects.
  We used SAM to model a wind turbine in Lane County’s valley region.
 NLR’s State and Local Planning for Energy (SLOPE) tool is a geospatial analysis
  application that provides high-resolution data on energy technical potential, generation, and
  costs at the state and county levels. We do not use SLOPE in this analysis.

7.1 Solar Resource Potential

7.1.1 Resource Overview
PV developments are frequently categorized as residential-, commercial-, and utility-scale
(illustrated in Figure 67). The scale of a solar installation is defined by its peak (or rated)
capacity, typically measured in kW or MW, which represents the maximum power the system
can produce under ideal sunlight conditions. The actual energy output depends on orientation,
shading, efficiency, temperature, and other factors. Solar panels operate more efficiently when
orientated directly toward the sun. Solar-tracking technology can rotate a panel along one or two
axes to follow the sun and increase efficiency, but that technology comes at a higher capital
cost.

 Figure 68: PV Scale Comparison 1,2

7.1.2 Local Potential
The potential for PV technology depends on how much solar energy a location receives from
the sun. Solar resource is commonly measured by irradiance (instantaneous solar power on an

1 Data from Abundant Solar. https://www.abundantsolar.com/services/residential-solar/
2 Data from U.S. Department of Energy. https://www.energy.gov/eere/solar/solar-radiation-basics.

 Solar Resource Potential

 213

area in W/m2) or insolation (the cumulative solar energy on an area over a given period in
kWh/m2/day, month, or year). Insolation better represents potential PV electricity generation and
is typically used in resource potential maps. Figure 68 maps long-term average solar insolation
across Lane County using data from SolarGIS (2021), 1 a private company that offers the free
Global Solar Atlas for preliminary planning. Solar insolation in Lane County ranges from 2.85-
4.32 kWh/m2/day with lower values near the coast and higher values towards the east edge of
the county. Solar in the Eugene and Springfield areas ranges between 3.75–4.00 kWh/m2/day.

Table 54 shows the production of several hypothetical solar PV installations in central Eugene.
A 4kW, residential-scale, roof-mounted, PV system typically occupies around 28 m² and
represents a common installation size. In central Eugene, NLR’s PVWatts Calculator 2 estimates
such a system would generate 4,400 to 5,000 kWh annually, meeting about half of the average
annual electricity consumption of a Lane County household. 3 In the same central Eugene
location, a hypothetical 1 MW single-axis tracking, ground-mounted, utility-scale system would
generate 1,260,000 to 1,460,000 kWh annually, enough to power 111 to 129 homes. This utility-
scale installation would require 2.8 to 4.2 acres of open land. 4, 5 By comparison, 1 MW of
distributed residential rooftop solar, about 250 individual 4 kW systems, would require about 1.8
acres of combined roof area and produce 1,100,000 to 1,240,000 kWh annually, enough to
power 97 to 109 average Lane County households.

The 1 MW utility-scale system generates 10–20% more electricity because single-axis tracking
increases daily solar exposure and ground-mounted systems can be optimally oriented and
spaced to minimize shading and maximize output. But utility-scale ground-mounted systems
require substantially more land to accommodate row spacing, access roads, and maintenance.
Rooftop solar is more space efficient but produces less electricity per installed PV capacity.

NLR’s 2024 ATB defines ten PV resource classes to represent solar resource quality at a given
location. PV Resource Class 1 corresponds to the highest-quality solar resource, while Class 10
represents the lowest. The Eugene area falls into Class 9. For each resource class, the ATB
provides technology-specific performance and cost projections for utility-, commercial- and
residential-scale PV systems. 2022 is the baseline year for 2024 ATB projections. Base year
estimates are derived from modeled cost data benchmarked against industry and historical
market data. The ATB projects future costs for new energy technology installations through
2035 and 2050 under three scenarios:
 Conservative: Lower research and development investment slows technology improvement.
  Costs largely stay the same as the baseline year.
 Moderate: Research and development investment continues at current levels. Technology
  incrementally improves without major breakthroughs.
 Advanced: Increased research and development investment accelerates innovation. Cost and
  performance improvements are consistent with historical technological advancement.

7.1.3 Economic Feasibility
Figure 69 compares the Resource Class 9 utility- and commercial-scale PV installation 2022
baseline and 2050 projection for conservative, moderate, and advanced technology scenarios.
2026 BPA Tier 1 Rates and the U.S. Energy Information Administration’s (EIA) NW Average
Electricity LCOE are provided for reference. These will likely change by 2050. In 2022,
commercial PV installations are significantly more expensive than utility-scale installations (note
that in 2022, all utility-scale estimates exactly overlap). Utility-scale PV benefits from economies
of scale, including bulk infrastructure purchases and the distribution of fixed costs across a
larger capacity. 1 While 2022 utility-scale PV is more expensive than BPA Tier 1 Rates, utility-
scale PV is comparable to 2022 NW average electricity LCOE. In 2050, commercial-scale PV is
projected to narrow the cost gap with utility-scale PV.

1 Utility Dive. “Utility-scale solar is significantly cheaper than rooftop. Does that make it better?” Jul. 2015.

https://www.utilitydive.com/news/utility-scale-solar-is-significantly-cheaper-than-rooftop-does-that-make-
i/402517/

 Figure 70: Commercial and Utility-Scale Solar LCOE vs BPA Tier 1 1 and NW Average (2025)
 LCOE 2

Technological advancements, improved efficiency, and continued cost reductions are expected
to make both utility- and commercial-scale PV installations economically competitive with BPA
Tier 1 rates and Northwest wholesale electricity. Even when solar is not cost-competitive, it can
still provide grid resilience benefits that are difficult to quanity, as noted in Section 4.4.

7.2 Wind Resource Potential

7.2.1 Resource Overview
Wind turbine size is generally defined by two dimensions: hub height (the distance from the
ground to the center of the rotor) and rotor diameter (the span of the blades). Increasing hub
height or rotor diameter increases turbine generating capacity. Greater hub heights access
stronger winds at higher elevations, and larger rotor diameters can collect energy from a larger
area. Over the past two decades, the hub height and rotor diameter of newly installed utility-
scale wind turbines have steadily increased. 3 Turbines are often grouped together into grid-
connected wind farms.

Wind turbines can be installed onshore or offshore, on floating, moored platforms in the ocean.
There are also fixed-bottom offshore wind turbines that stand on platforms that are fixed to the
bottom of the ocean, but the steep continential shelf on the west coast of the United States
prohibits the use of fixed-bottom technology. Large offshore turbines typically have higher hub
heights and larger rotor diameters than onshore turbines. Offshore wind speeds are stronger,
more consistent, and less turbulent than onshore winds. As a result, offshore turbines generate
substantially more energy than onshore turbunes (see Figure 71), albeit with a greater capital
and operational expense. 4

1 Data from Bonneville Power Administration, “Provider of Choice Policy Record of Decision,” Mar. 2024.

https://www.bpa.gov/
2 Data from Energy Information Administration, “Forecast Wholesale Power Prices and Retail Electricity

Prices Rise Modestly in 2025,” Jan. 2025. https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=64384
3 https://www.energy.gov/cmei/articles/wind-turbines-bigger-better
4 P. Joshi and C. Gokhale-Welch, “Fundamentals of Wind Energy,” Nov. 2022.

https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy23osti/84501.pdf

The generating capacity of a single wind turbine ranges widely. A small 10 kW wind turbine can
power a single building. Figure 70 is a photo of a 10 kW wind turbine installed by the University
of Oregon in 2013 to power the Charleston Marine Life Center at the Oregon Institute of Marine
Biology. The turbine shut down in 2022. The largest operating turbine is 15 MW, and even
larger designs are in development. Figure 71 illustrates onshore and offshore wind turbine
sizes.

7.3 Biomass Resource Potential
Biomass energy is produced by burning organic materials, converting organic materials into
fuels, or burning biogas. Biomass feedstocks include crops grown specifically for energy use,
agricultural byproducts like stalks and leaves, timber and forestry byproducts, algae, wood
processing byproducts like sawdust and pulp, and municipal waste like garbage, food waste,
sewage, and manure. Organic materials can be combusted on site or distributed to a facility.
Biogas is methane or carbon dioxide produced during the decomposition sewage, garbage, or
manure and can be burned to produce energy or generate electricity. 4

In Oregon, the industrial sector is the largest producer and consumer of biomass energy. 5 Wood
and wood waste is Oregon’s primary source of biomass, and biomass energy consumption has
grown steadily since 2002 due to increased demand for biofuels. In 2014, NLR mapped the total
quantity in tons of solid biomass resources per Square Kilometer in the United States by county

1 International Renewable Energy Agency. “Offshore Wind: From 83 GW Today to 2,000 GW by 2050”,

Sept. 2025. https://www.irena.org/News/articles/2025/Sep/Offshore-Wind-From-83-GW-Today-to-2000-
GW-by-2050
2 Data from Bonneville Power Administration, “Provider of Choice Policy Record of Decision,” Mar. 2024.

https://www.bpa.gov/
3 Data from Energy Information Administration, “Forecast Wholesale Power Prices and Retail Electricity

Prices Rise Modestly in 2025,” Jan. 2025. https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=64384
4 U.S. Energy Information Administration, “Biomass explained,”

 Biomass Resource Potential

 222

(Figure 74). Lane County contains a relatively high concentration of solid biomass resources.
Cascades wildfires since 2014 may have reduced Oregon’s concentration of solid biomass.

 Figure 75: U.S. Biomass Resource per Square Kilometer 1

Burning biomass releases greenhouse gases. Wood smoke contains harmful pollutants such as
carbon monoxide and particulate matter which pose risks to local cardiovascular health. Burning
municipal solid waste reduces landfill volume, but releases chemicals and other substances
contained in the waste into the air. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) requires
waste-to-energy plants to include air pollution control devices. Lane County has several
biomass facilities discussed in Section 1.6. Recently, members of the public in the Eugene area
have expressed deep concerns about biomass facilities. 2

As wildfires become increasingly prevalent, interest has grown in collecting byproducts from
wildfire mitigation and cleanup for use as biomass feedstocks. Collecting woody waste from
utility vegetation management could support wildfire resilience while supplying biomass
resource. Integrating biomass feedstock recovery with wildfire management requires more
research and planning to determine feasibility. Region-specific strategies and coordinated multi-
use approaches are important. 3

 Chapter 8
 Geospatial Data to Inform Project
 Prioritization
 Candace Briggs, Amanda Krelling, Kaiyu Sun, and Ali
 Trueworthy

As Lane County government, utilities, and partners ideate, prioritize, and select energy
projects and strategies, they will consider where within the county projects are most
needed. There are numerous factors that can be used to indicate priority areas for
different types of projects. In this section, we use datasets of socio-economic metrics
and locations of critical facilities to explore potential priority areas within Lane County for
work aimed at 1) maintaining affordable energy service, 2) boosting electricity system
resilience, and 3) enhancing backup power availability. These project aims are not
mutually exclusive, and the metrics alone cannot determine where a project or strategy
should be implemented.

8.1 Priority Areas for Maintaining Affordable Energy Service
It is especially important to maintain affordable energy services in areas where people already
experience economic difficulties and may be struggling to afford energy. Metrics used to show
these economic difficulties include housing energy burden and social vulnerability index
(Section 8.2).
The housing energy burden metric is derived from the U.S. Department of Energy’s Low-
Income Energy Affordability Tool developed by NLR. This tool combines data from the U.S.
Census Bureau and the U.S. Energy Information Administration to estimate the percentage of
household income spent on home energy costs. Data comes from the 2016-2020 5-year
American Community Survey. NLR classifies energy burdens into three categories: low (<3.8%
of household income), medium (3.8–6.0%), and high (>6.0%). A low burden reflects relatively
affordable energy costs, a medium burden indicates moderate financial strain from energy
expenses, and a high burden indicates that households spend a large share of their income on
energy.
Figure 75 shows Lane County’s housing energy burden by census tract. In Lane County,
approximately 31,500 residents—about 8% of the county’s 2020 population—live within census
tracts categorized as having a medium housing energy burden. These areas are distributed
across both urban and rural parts of the county, with roughly a third of affected households
located within EWEB’s service territory in Eugene. The remaining medium-burden areas are
dispersed throughout rural regions served by a mix of Blachly-Lane Electric, EPUD, Lane

 224

Electric, Pacific Power, and Central Lincoln PUD. Several of these tracts span multiple utility
territories, reflecting how energy affordability challenges extend across jurisdictional and service
boundaries rather than being concentrated in one provider’s area. No Census Tracts in Lane
County fall into the high energy burden category. Values for housing energy burden in
Figure 75 range from 0% (light green) to 5% (light gray), and utility boundaries are shown in
black.

 Figure 76: Lane County Household Energy Burden by Census Tract 1

8.2 Priority Areas for Reducing Hazard Impacts
Projects aimed at reducing the impacts of, and vulnerability to, natural hazards might be
prioritized in places where vulnerability is already high. To characterize high vulnerability to
natural hazards, we map two multi-variable metrics: Social Vulnerability Index and Expected
Annual Loss.

1 Data from National Lab of the Rockies “Energy Affordability - Household Energy and Transportation

Burden”. State and Local Planning for Energy. https://maps.nlr.gov/slope/data-
viewer?filters=%5B%5D&layer=eej.household-energy-burden&year=2020&res=county

 Priority Areas for Reducing Hazard Impacts

 225

The Social Vulnerability Index (SVI) is a metric developed by the Centers for Disease Control
and Prevention (CDC) designed to identify communities that are at higher risk to hazards and
community-level stressors such as natural disasters, disease outbreaks, or chemical
exposures. 1 Overall vulnerability is calculated based on 16 U.S. Census variables grouped into
4 categories: Socioeconomic Status, Household Characteristics, Racial & Ethnic Minority, and
Housing Type & Transportation. Vulnerability is ranked as a percentile from 0 to 1, with values
closer to 1 representing higher vulnerability. Data to create the current SVI comes from 5-year
averages of the American Community Survey between 2018-2022. 2
Lane County’s overall social vulnerability score of 0.74 indicates medium-to-high vulnerability.
Figure 76 displays Lane County’s social vulnerability by census tract. Urban areas exhibit a mix
of high, medium, and low vulnerability, while rural areas generally fall in the mid-range. Eleven
census tracts are in the highest vulnerability category (100%-80% indicating higher vulnerability
than 100%-80% of other census tracts), including nine urban and two rural tracts. Across the
four SVI themes, the highest-risk tracts show mid-to-high vulnerability for social economic
status, household characteristics, and housing type/transportation, and low-to-mid vulnerability
for ethnic minority status. Drivers of high risk in Lane County tracts that score in the top 90th
percentile are social economic status, housing type, and transportation.

1 Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry, “Social Vulnerability Index,” July 2024.

https://www.atsdr.cdc.gov/place-health/php/svi/index.html
2 U.S. Census Bureau, “2018-2022 ACS 5-year Estimates,” Nov. 2023.

https://www.census.gov/programs-surveys/acs/technical-documentation/table-and-geography-
changes/2022/5-year.html

 Priority Areas for Reducing Hazard Impacts

 226

 Figure 77: Lane County Social Vulnerability Index by Census Tract
 where utility boundaries are overlaid in black. 1

Expected annual loss represents the “average economic loss in dollars resulting from natural
hazards each year.” 2 These losses reflect the potential impact on buildings, agricultural assets,
and populations exposed to hazard events. They do not account for indirect economic losses,
such as the loss of trees or wildlife during fires. A wide range of hazards are considered in this
calculation including avalanche, coastal flooding, cold wave, drought, earthquake, hail, heat
wave, hurricane, ice storm, landslide, lightning, river flooding, strong winds, tornado, tsunami,
volcanic activity, wildfire, and/or winter weather. Expected annual loss is calculated
independently for buildings, agricultural assets, and populations. Buildings and agriculture
losses are measured in dollars, and population losses are measured in injuries and fatalities.
Data to create the current expected annual loss scores comes from 5-year averages of the
American Community Survey between 2018-2022. 3

FEMA ranked counties across the United States as a percentile from 0-1 with 0 being low
expected annual loss and 1 being high expected annual loss. Lane County has an expected

1 Data from Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry, ibid.

annual loss score of 0.96 at the county level. Figure 77 shows the distribution of expected
annual loss by census tract. There are three tracts classified in the “very high” category—two
within EWEB, and one within SUB service territory. 20 tracts are classified in the “relatively high”
category across rural and urban areas. Most of the estimated losses are associated with
buildings, while expected losses to population and agricultural assets are low. The hazards
contributing to this risk profile include coastal flooding in a few coastal tracts, high expected
losses from earthquakes across all tracts, and moderate losses from ice storms countywide.
Additional contributors include mixed levels of riverine flooding risk across tracts and elevated
landslide risk in several rural western areas. Lightning-related risk is shown as historically
moderate but with low expected annual losses, and wildfire risk likewise displays historically
moderate values with low expected annual loss.2 The low expected annual loss due to lightning
and wildfire is mostly because these hazards have historically happened in places with less
human-made infrastructure.

SVI and expected annual loss might be considered in a variety of ways when prioritizing
projects. While SVI might indicate places where it is especially important to provide continuous
electric service, expected annual loss might add context to where to place (or avoid placing)
significant new infrastructure.

8.3 Priority Areas for Enhancing Backup Power Capabilities
Backup power—electricity that is available even when the larger electric grid is down—is an
essential part of disaster response and recovery. For some, maintaining access to electricity
can be essential for human health and safety. When considering where to build out backup
power capabilities and how to most effectively use available local generation, understanding the
location of critical facilities and the status of their available backup power is essential.
Critical facilities are buildings and infrastructure assets that provide essential services (i.e.,
lifelines) necessary to protect public health, safety, and welfare during normal operations and
emergency conditions. 1 A facility may be considered critical based on the consequences of
service disruption, the population served, the duration of acceptable downtime, and/or its role in
emergency response recovery. Criteria typically include whether the facility supports life-
sustaining functions (e.g., hospitals), emergency coordination (e.g., emergency operation
centers), public safety (e.g., fire and police stations), or essential utilities (e.g., water and
wastewater treatment plants). Additional factors may include dependency relationships with
other infrastructure and the facility’s importance to vulnerable populations. 2 Figure 78 illustrates
the preparation, response, and recovery cycle for critical facilities.

 Figure 79: Lifeline Response and Stabilization Process Supporting Critical Facilities 3

1 Inter-agency Network for Education in Emergencies, “Critical facilities,” https://inee.org/eie-

glossary/critical-facilities
2 Institute for Policy Research & Engagement., “Solar + storage microgrid feasibility: Final report prepared

for Josephine County Emergency Management and Energy Trust of Oregon.,” University of Oregon,
School of Planning, Public Policy, and Management, Mar. 2022.
https://scholarsbank.uoregon.edu/server/api/core/bitstreams/7c730e49-8915-42ff-aa40-
4c998ff1bf08/content
3 Information from Federal Emergency Management Agency, “Community Lifelines,” Feb. 2025.

https://www.fema.gov/emergency-managers/practitioners/lifelines-toolkit/community-lifelines

 229

Mapping critical facilities helps visualize their geographic distribution relative to hazards,
infrastructure networks, and energy systems. This spatial understanding supports identification
of service gaps, clustering of high-priority assets, and areas where resilience investments may
have greater impact. Mapping also enables assessment of proximity to substations, feeders,
floodplains, wildfire risk zones, and transportation corridors, which supports coordinated
planning and resource allocation. At the time of writing this document, Lane County and the
national laboratory researchers providing support through the E2C program are working
together to collect and curate data on critical facilities in Lane County. Here, we include some
preliminary data and discussion of important energy related data points.

Figure 79 shows the fire stations and fire district boundaries. Figure 80 illustrates travel
distances along the road network from locations across Lane County to the nearest hospital and
ambulance service areas. The maps highlight strong coverage in and around the Eugene-
Springfield metropolitan area, where emergency service facilities are concentrated and travel
distances are relatively short. In contrast, large portions of eastern Lane County, characterized
by mountainous terrain and lower population density, are outside of fire districts and show
significantly longer travel distances to hospitals, indicating greater isolation from emergency
response resources.

Lane County residents receive water from public water systems or rely on wells. About 329,000
of the approximately 382,500 individuals in Lane County are served by public water systems. 1
The remaining 14% of Lane County residents rely on well water. In most power outage
scenarios, residents served by water utilities will still have access to water for hours to days
without power because elevated storage tanks maintain water pressure in public water systems.
However, residents who access wells through electric submersible pumps (the most common
method) would almost immediately lose access to their water in a power outage. Without
backup power, these residents have to rely on previously stored water until power is restored.
Only resients with suitably sized portable or standby backup power woud be able to continune
pumping water from their well during an outage. Figure 81 maps public water systems across
Lane County.

 Figure 82: Lane County Public Water Systems1
Collecting consistent data on critical facilities is essential to evaluate resilience and energy
security. Lane County, with support from national laboratory researchers, is undertaking an
effort to better understand the vulnerability of key critical facilities and to support emergency
preparedness and response planning across the county. Table 56 summarizes key data metrics
that support this assessment, describing why each metric matters and how it can be used by
utilities, emergency managers, facility operators, and planners.

1 Oregon Health Authority, “Oregon Public Water Systems,”

https://experience.arcgis.com/experience/02c5d88395404ad081884c98067a3565#data_s=where%3Adat
aSource_1-pws_active_5534%3APWS%3D'00967'&zoom_to_selection=true

 233

The metrics described in Table 56 represent examples of information that can help evaluate
facility resilience and operational needs during disruptions. The County is refining its data
collection approach to focus on the types of information that are most useful and feasible to
obtain, based on local priorities and stakeholder input. Lane County’s efforts to identify and
characterize critical facilities are also informed by the requirements of Oregon House Bill 3630
(HB 3630), 1 which directs electric utilities and local partners to improve planning for energy
resilience at facilities that provide essential community services.
Identifying and characterizing critical facilities in Lane County presents several challenges.
Definitions of “critical” may vary across agencies, and facility roles can change depending on
the type and scale of disruption. Data availability is often inconsistent, outdated, or incomplete,
particularly for backup power capacity, fuel storage duration, and actual critical load
requirements. In addition, some operational details may be sensitive or restricted due to security
considerations, limiting data sharing across jurisdictions. The county’s large geographic area
and mix of urban, rural, and remote communities further complicate consistent assessment.
Addressing these challenges requires cross-agency coordination, standardized data collection
practices, and periodic updates to ensure that resilience planning reflects current conditions and
emerging risks.

 Chapter 9
 Community Energy and Resilience
 Planning
 Brooke Marten and Ali Trueworthy

Local governments are increasingly undertaking energy or resilience planning initiatives.
These initiatives can give strategic direction for projects, policies, and coordinated
action within a community. Often, they include a community’s goals or vision along with
strategy areas or policy recommendations for reaching those goals. Community
plans related to economic growth, transportation, and electrification can impact how
much electricity and/or fuel a community uses.

In this section, we provide best practices for community energy planning and community
resilience planning. The best practices are concerned with the planning process rather than the
plans themselves. Then we review existing resilience and energy planning by drawing from a
variety of plan types from cities and communities in Lane County. By reviewing these plans, we
aim to: (1) Build a wider understanding of community energy and resilience goals across the
county, (2) Identify common energy related policies and strategies being pursued across the
county, and (3) Assess how the electric system is considered in resilience and energy planning.
All plans that we reference are listed in Appendix B.

9.1 Best Practices
Best Practices for community energy planning and for community resilience planning are
outlined in this section.

9.1.1 Community Energy Planning
Community energy plans, which are sometimes also called “strategic energy plans”, are
roadmaps for achieving community energy goals in both the near and long term. Often,
community energy planning is initiated or led by local authorities but completed by a broader
group of stakeholders. A major part of strategic energy planning is coordinating other plans as
they relate to the energy system. Sometimes strategic energy plans are part of larger plans
such as master plans or climate action plans. Benefits from strategic energy planning can
include cost savings for consumers, avoided infrastructure development need, and reduced
pollution. 1

1 Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory, “Best Practices in Integrated Resource Planning: A guide for

planners developing the electricity resource mix of the future,” Nov. 2024.
https://emp.lbl.gov/publications/best-practices-integrated-resource

 235

The U.S. Department of Energy’s strategic energy planning process is shown in Figure 82.

 Figure 83: Nine-Step Community Energy Planning Cycle 1

The nine-step, cyclical process is similar to processes published by others. 2, 3, 4 DOE’s nine-step
process includes:
 Step 1 – Identify/Convene Stakeholders: This step involves identifying and bringing together
 stakeholders such as local government, utilities, businesses, nongovernmental
 organizations (NGOs), residents, and “energy champions” (i.e., those interested in planning
 and energy savings). Ongoing, inclusive engagement with these stakeholders using
 strategies such as meetings, surveys, and digital tools (e.g., websites, email lists, social
 media, texting) helps to build trust and ensures community input is incorporated throughout
 the process.

1 Figure adapted from U.S. Department of Energy, “COMMUNITY GREENING: HOW TO DEVELOP A

STRATEGIC ENERGY PLAN,” Feb. 2010. https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy10osti/45652.pdf
2 Public Service Commission of Wisconsin, “Energy Planning Basics,”

https://psc.wi.gov/Pages/ServiceType/OEI/EnergyPlanning.aspx
3 K. Arkema, K. Grantham, D. Rose, and A. Trueworthy, “Deployment Readiness Framework Subtask 1.1

(Literature Review),” Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, PNNL-33575, 1896960, Aug. 2022.
https://www.doi.10.2172/1896960.
4 U.S. Department of Energy, “Guide to Community Energy Strategic Planning,” Mar. 2013.

https://www.energy.gov/sites/prod/files/2014/05/f15/cesp_guide.pdf

 236

Step 2 – Form Leadership Team: This step involves establishing a cross-sector leadership
  team with decision-making authority and the ability to gather stakeholders, acquire funding,
  and promote the project. The team often includes relevant municipal departments and
  dedicated external advocates, each with clear roles to ensure transparency and
  accountability.
Step 3 – Develop Energy Vision: This step involves creating an energy vision that reflects
  community priorities such as efficiency, renewable energy adoptions, resilience, and/or
  affordability. The vision clarifies the plan’s scope and direction. A vision answers the
  question: “What do we want our energy system to look like in 20 years?” Or it might be a
  simple list of priorities. Most importantly, the vision should be something that will keep
  participants inspired throughout the planning process and beyond.
Step 4 – Develop Energy Baseline: This step involves collecting and organizing data on
  energy use, costs, and emissions across all sectors deemed relevant to the established
  energy vision. The baseline data characterizes where the community is relative to their
  vision. The data collection process may include working with utilities and energy auditors,
  reviewing utility bills and equipment data, documenting assumptions and data quality, and
  preparing a business-as-usual forecast to compare against proposed actions.
Step 5 – Develop Specific Goals: This step involves translating the vision and baseline into
  specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART) goals with defined
  metrics and timelines. The goals span short, intermediate, and long-term horizons, identify
  responsible parties and preliminary budgets, and are validated through community
  engagement and an understanding of zoning and other constraints.
Step 6 – Evaluate and Rank Programs: This step involves identifying potential projects and
  policies and assessing them using evaluation criteria such as effectiveness, timeframe,
  feasibility, and co-benefits such as social equity and economic development. A transparent
  scoring and ranking framework used to prioritize near-term actions and to phase more
  complex projects over time as resources and capacity allow.
Step 7 – Funding Source: This step involves developing a funding and financing strategy that
  could involve a range of funding sources (e.g., grants, utility incentives, committed municipal
  budget support), and financing tools matched to project types and timelines. If an immediate
  funding source is unavailable, free programs and/or partnerships that provide their own
  funding should be prioritized.
Step 8 – Compile the Plan: This step involves consolidating the baseline, goals, prioritized
  actions, and performance metrics into a formal plan for adoption by the governing body.
  Prior to adoption, the leadership team should re-engage the broader group of stakeholders
  and interested community members. After adoption, the document can guide the
  community’s policy- and decision-making.
Step 9 – Measurement & Verification (M&V) and Plan Alterations: This step involves
  establishing measurement and verification protocols to quantify achieved energy savings
  and assess ongoing plan performance. A regular reporting cadence allows for, if necessary,
  adjustments to targets, re-ranking of actions, and reallocation of resources. The team might
  choose a timeline for when to reconvene to assess progress and eventually restart the
  process as their situation changes.

 237

Local Government Role in Strategic Energy Planning

There has been some academic research about the capacity of local authorities to steward
strategic energy planning. Multiple studies note that local authorities acting in the energy space
tend to apply ad hoc and opportunistic approaches to energy projects while struggling to
achieve strategic direction, 1, 2 but a more recent study looking at efforts in Denmark shows
success in aligning strategic plans and energy projects. 3 This research exemplifies that, like
many of the other best practices presented in this document, best practices for community
energy planning are currently being researched and improved.

In Figure 83, we show some of the services that can be expected of a strategic energy planning
body (i.e., the stakeholders leading a strategic energy planning process) alongside some of the
functions municipal or county governments often play. The purpose of Figure 83 is to exemplify
some of the ways that the existing roles and services of local governments can make them well
suited to lead energy planning. That said, other community leaders may initiate, lead, or steward
community energy planning. The important thing is that they can facilitate progress toward the
energy vision.

1 L. Allman, P. Fleming, and A. Wallace, “The progress of English and Welsh local authorities in

addressing climate change,” Local Environ., vol. 9, no. 3, pp. 271–283, Jun. 2004.
https://www.doi.10.1080/1354983042000219379.
2 C. S. E. Bale, T. J. Foxon, M. J. Hannon, and W. F. Gale, “Strategic energy planning within local

authorities in the UK: A study of the city of Leeds,” Energy Policy, vol. 48, pp. 242–251, Sep. 2012.
https://www.doi.10.1016/j.enpol.2012.05.019.
3 L. Krog, “How municipalities act under the new paradigm for energy planning,” Sustain. Cities Soc., vol.

47, p. 101511, May 2019, doi: https://www.doi.10.1016/j.scs.2019.101511.

9.1.2 Community Resilience Planning
Community resilience plans are strategic frameworks designed to prepare for, mitigate,
respond to, and recover from disruptive events. A resilience plan sets performance goals for the
built environment based on the community’s social needs such as human health, safety, and
general welfare, addresses vulnerabilities, and integrates both structural (e.g., temporary repair
or retrofits) and non-structural solutions (e.g., change to land use policy).

The National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) has developed useful products for
community resilience planning including a Community Resilience Planning Guide for Buildings

 239

and Infrastructure Systems. Volume one of this document 1 outlines a six-step approach to
community resilience planning. Volume two of this document 2 outlines how to characterize a
community’s social and economic dimensions, dependencies and consequences potentially
associated with building and infrastructure system decisions, the performance of buildings and
infrastructure (i.e., transportation systems, energy systems, communications systems, water
and wastewater systems), and community resilience metrics. Additionally, NIST provides Guide
Briefs 3 that summarize the best practices that align with each of the steps in the recommended
six-step approach and a companion playbook 4 that provides more detail on what specific
actions communities can take when establishing their own community resilience plan (note that
templates are also available). The six-step community resilience planning process is shown in
Figure 84.

 Figure 85: Six-Step Community Resilience Planning Process 5

More information on what each step entails can be found in volume I of NIST's Community
Resilience Planning guide for Buildings and infrastructure System.1 The publication also
highlights other community resilience guidance that is available including: SPUR Framework, 6
Baseline Resilience Indicators for Communities (BRIC), 7 the Community and Regional

Resilience Institute (CARRI) framework, 1 the Oregon Resilience Plan, 2 NOAA’s Coastal

Resilience, 7 and FEMA’s Resilience Analysis and Planning Tool (RAPT). 8

Local Government Role in Community Resilience Planning

Local governments are well-positioned to lead community resilience planning due to their
unique authority and established roles within the community. 9,10 Local governments are already
responsible for efforts such as land-use planning, hazard mitigation, and economic
development. These responsibilities allow them to coordinate and collaborate effectively with
relevant entities, ensuring that resilience goals integrate seamlessly into existing plans. For
example, Natural Hazard Mitigation Plans (NHMPs), which are a distinct planning product from
community resilience plans, identify natural hazards, assess vulnerabilities through risk
assessment, and establish a mitigation strategy to reduce risk. NHMPs are generally a
prerequisite for receiving FEMA hazard mitigation funding and can complement and inform
community resilience planning.

In addition, municipalities often own or regulate key buildings and infrastructure systems,
making it easier to access data, identify risks, and engage the appropriate stakeholders. Local

governments are tasked with implementing community building codes, statutes, and broader
community planning efforts, providing them with the tools for effective resilience plan
implementation.

While having a dedicated community resilience office is ideal for streamlining the planning
process, this may not always be feasible due to resource constraints. However, this limitation
should not hinder the development of robust resilience plans. Other capable stakeholders, such
as community organizations or regional partnerships, can lead the planning team if they can
provide the necessary leadership and oversight.

The foundational services essential to successful community resilience planning include:
 • Engaging diverse stakeholders at all community levels
 • Assessing risks and identifying critical buildings and infrastructure
 • Establishing recovery goals for essential community functions
 • Integrating resilience goals with existing community plans
 • Identifying and prioritize implementable resilience actions
 • Documenting and formalize the comprehensive resilience plan
 • Updating resilience plans regularly to reflect changing needs.

9.2 Approaches Applied in Lane County
While few communities in Lane County have formal community energy or resilience plans, many
have adopted climate plans, emergency operations plans, and natural hazard mitigation plans
(Table 57). Additionally, seven of the communities in Lane County have identified buildings that
could serve as Resilience Hubs to support their communities through an EPA-funded effort. 1
Note that other facilities may be considered community resilience hubs outside of the scope of
that effort.

At the county level, Lane County has implemented several key frameworks: an Internal
Operations Climate Action Plan, 2 a Greenhouse Gas Reduction Plan, 3 and a Climate Adaption
and Resilience Plan, 4 all of which incorporate elements of strategic energy planning. In terms of
county-level resilience planning, the Lane Regional Resiliency Collaborative works to “link,
leverage, and align community resources (e.g., county, city, special district, nonprofit, private

 242

sector, etc.) across Lane County using a whole community framework.” 1,2 Additionally, Lane
County continues to update its Multi-Jurisdictional Natural Hazard Mitigation Plan, 3 which aims
is to identify, assess, and reduce Lane County’s natural hazards risks. The Lane County
Emergency Operations Plan 4 focuses on disaster preparedness through coordinated
activities, including protection, prevention, mitigation, response, and recovery activities.

On a broader scale, the State of Oregon offers a suite of resources related to resilience
planning 5 and maintains a statewide energy strategy document. 6 Furthermore, Energy Trust of
Oregon provides a Strategic Plan focused on accelerating the adoption of clean energy
solutions, reducing energy costs, and delivering community-centered benefits. 7 While county-
and state-level plans are valuable, they may not always align with the unique needs and
capabilities of individual cities.

9.2.1 Community Energy Planning, Goals, And Policies
Across Lane County, energy objectives are distributed among climate plans, comprehensive
plans, and sector plans rather than consolidated in stand‑alone strategic energy plans. Some of
the most common energy related goals and strategies set by municipal governments are listed
in Table 58, along with which cities within Lane County have adopted those goals and
strategies.

At the county level, the Greenhouse Gas Reduction Plan establishes a portfolio of strategies
targeting reductions in the County’s energy consumption ranging in scope from electric vehicle
adoption, electric grid decarbonization, electrification of residential appliances, and energy
efficiency and conservation. 1 Additionally, Lane County’s Rural Comprehensive Plan includes
energy conservation as an explicit goal, directing Lane County to promote efficient land use,
buildings, and transportation; provide public education and interjurisdictional collaboration
related to energy conservation; and develop financial incentives to support energy-efficient
projects. 2

Individual municipalities reflect similar energy goals in their comprehensive plans as articulated
in the county and state energy planning documents, however they vary in their level of
specificity and corresponding policy actions.

As the largest city in Lane County, Eugene’s planning documents are the most prescriptive with
respect to energy planning. To progress towards achieving the fossil fuel and emissions
reduction targets set in Eugene’s Climate Recovery Ordinance, the Community Climate Action
Plan 2.0 3 outlines both current and future actions related to reducing emissions associated with
energy used in buildings, transportation fuels, and consumption. Specifically, to reduce the
emissions associated with energy used in buildings, current actions are focused on energy
efficiency programs, smart metering, community outreach on energy audits, and building
upgrades, and future actions are focused on advancing state building codes to energy-efficient
standards, incentivizing electrification, and supporting electric vehicle charging infrastructure.

Other cities advance energy goals through Comprehensive Plan policies and utility
partnerships. It’s important to note, however, that many Oregon communities’ Comprehensive
Plans originated in the late 1970s and early 1980s and have been updated irregularly through
post-acknowledgement plan amendments, making it difficult to determine how current specific
energy policies are without reviewing adoption and amendment records (Table 58).

 sources, which includes pursuing actions related to implementing forward-looking energy policy, pursuing
Cottage grants for project execution, developing pilot projects, establishing solar panel incentives, and
 Grove encouraging resource conservation. Another strategy of the city’s is to track, support and encourage
 sustainable development. One way this is being pursued is using a local “Nobel Prize” for citizens that
 design or implement successful energy-conservation ideas and actions. 1
 The city has goals “to provide for the housing needs of the citizens of the State”, which includes a policy to
 encourage the incorporation of energy efficiency standards in the existing housing stock, and “to conserve
Coburg

 decisions, and encourage residents to utilize Pacific Power’s free home energy audit. 2
 The city has eight energy conservation policies that include: developer utilization of energy-efficient design

Creswell

 energy (i.e., wind and solar energy) and land use and transportation planning policies that will conserve
 energy, while recognizing constraints for their adoption (i.e., height limits, setbacks, etc.). 6 A unique
Florence recommendation from Florence’s Comprehensive Plan is to consider the ways that trees and foliage
 provide a natural windbreak that acts to conserve energy when planning residential developments. Trees
 provide energy efficiency benefits for heating in cool, windy places like the Oregon Coast, but can also
 provide energy efficiency benefits for cooling in warm, sunny areas like the Valley and Cascades. 6
 The city’s energy planning efforts seek to conserve energy while also lowering the cost of energy
Junction consumption in city facilities. To do so, the city’s zoning ordinance encourages the reduced use of
 petroleum-based fuel and has a program to replace streetlights with higher efficiency models. Additionally,

 Like many other cities in Lane County, Lowell has a community goal of energy conservation in alignment
 with the broader statewide Land Conservation and Development Commission goals and guidelines. As
 such, the community promotes the use of techniques such as efficient land use patterns, effective housing
  Lowell rehabilitation and construction methods, the use of alternative energy sources (e.g., solar), and energy
 efficient transportation policies. Other energy-related policies in the comprehensive plan require new utility
 lines be placed underground, encourage the conversion of existing overhead utilities to underground
 service, and encourage the adoption of energy conservation practices in public facilities. 1
 The city also has the goal of energy conservation, which the comprehensive plan states will be supported
 by the following: individual energy conservation promoted through governmental programs, taxation
 incentives, educational programs, land use regulations (e.g., higher density dwelling construction),
Oakridge building codes (e.g., require highly insulative building materials), transportation planning (e.g., well-thought
 out city design to decrease the transportation distance between residences and common destination
 points), and exploration of renewable energy options available in the region (e.g., geothermal and solar). 2

 The city’s comprehensive plan has implementation strategies outlined for its policies and recommends
 considering the use of incentives for promoting the adoption of renewable energy. 3 Additionally,
Springfield Springfield’s 2035 Transportation System Plan has a policy requiring the consideration of environmental
 impacts of the overall transportation system, which has associated actions related to corridor planning and
 consideration of alternative energy infrastructure needs (i.e., electric vehicle charging stations). 4
 The city’s comprehensive plan claims that through avoiding urban sprawl, energy conservation will be
 achieved, however other than policies requiring that electric lines be placed underground for service
 Veneta extensions to new land development and, when feasible, in existing commercial areas, there are not
 explicit policies related to energy planning and energy conservation. 5
 Like several of the other communities in Lane County, Westfir has a goal encouraging energy
 conservation through education programs and structural weatherization. Strategies for achieving this goal
 mentioned in the comprehensive plan include effective land use and land development, public educational
 efforts to encourage energy conservation and alternative energy source adoption, reducing city building
 Westfir energy use (i.e., proper insulation and weatherization), and making information about relevant tax
 incentives available to residents). Other goals in the comprehensive plan relate to housing, where policy
 requires coordination of housing rehabilitation efforts with energy conservation action (i.e., insulation and
 weatherization programs). When infrastructure expansions are being planned, projects that would require
 a distribution system upgrade must be directly negotiated with Lane Electrical Cooperative, Inc. 6

Overall, across Lane County, energy planning is embedded in climate, comprehensive, and
sector-specific plans. County-level planning prioritizes demand reduction, electrification, grid
decarbonization, electric vehicle adoption, and efficient land use and transportation. Cities
mirror these levers through utility programs and their comprehensive plan policies. This is likely
because many of the local government-level energy planning strategies are modeled after state-
wide goals and requirements put forth by the Land Conservation and Development Commission
(LCDC), which includes goals “to conserve energy” and “to provide and encourage a safe,
convenient and economic transportation system” which stipulates that a transportation plan shall
conserve energy. 1 Common policy approaches to achieve the goals of energy conservation
include focusing on energy efficiency, improving building performance, targeted renewables,
and land-use measures that curb urban sprawl and lower demand (summarized in Table 59).

9.2.2 Community Resilience Planning, Priorities, and Strategies
The following section summarizes the community resilience-related plans, priorities, and
strategies currently in place across the county. Like energy planning, resilience planning is more
mature at the county level in Lane County and serves as the connective tissue for cities with
limited capacity to develop their own resilience plans.
One driver of this county-level emphasis is recent state support for energy resilience planning
through legislation (HB 3630), which provides up to $50,000 per county to develop an energy
resilience plan. 1 Although not all Oregon counties are participating in the energy resilience grant
program, potentially due to remaining funding gaps even with the grant and/or capacity
constraints, Lane County is participating. As part of Lane County’s fulfillment of the energy
resilience grant from ODOE, national laboratory partners are assisting with data collection and
estimates related to critical facility energy used (see Chapter 8).
Lane County’s Climate Adaption and Resilience Plan outlines 21 strategies the County plans to
use to become more climate resilient. The strategies address critical areas such as wildfire risk
reduction, green infrastructure, renewable energy storage, urban heat mitigation, personal
preparedness programs, and climate-resilient housing. Broadly, these strategies emphasize
building resilience through infrastructure upgrades, community engagement, policy support, and
environmental restoration while prioritizing vulnerable populations across Lane County. 2 Lane
County also has an Emergency Operations Plan that establishes a framework and procedures
for coordinating response and short-term recovery during emergencies. 3
Alongside formal government strategies, the Lane Community Organizations Active in Disaster
(COAD) convenes nonprofits, businesses, community organizations, and volunteers to
coordinate assistance before, during, and after disasters. Guided by its own Emergency
Operations Plan and partnering with city and county emergency managers, Lane COAD
complements official response when state or regional resources are not yet mobilized or have
demobilized. 4
At the municipal scale, adoption of resilience plans is mixed; among the twelve cities, five have
a Community Resilience Plan, Emergency Operations Plan (EOP), NHMP, and/or a dedicated
resilience section in their Comprehensive Plan. Additionally, 8 cities participate in Lane County’s
NHMP (Table 60): 5

  Grove from nine assessed hazards, and the EOP, which provides guidelines for immediate hazard response.

 which reviewed natural hazards relevant to the area and assessed the regions vulnerability, finding “very
Springfield high” risks for geomagnetic disturbance, and earthquake natural hazards, and “high” risks for winter
 storms, droughts, wildfires, and windstorms. Recommended mitigation actions for these high-risk
 hazards include upgrading local transportation infrastructure for seismic conditions, retrofitting critical
 facilities, conducting an emergency fuels assessment, increasing fuel capacity, seismically retrofitting

 developing of a city evacuation plan.3

 manmade disasters.” Additionally, each broader goal in the development strategy has resilience
 implications which include creation of: close-knit social networks, an emergency response protocol, local
 Veneta recovery initiatives, diverse industries, infrastructure redundancy, increased resident mobility, and robust
 infrastructure. 4 The City’s EOP is another resource available pertaining to the community’s resilience

 from hazards. While some strategies are aimed at limiting exposure in high-risk zones (i.e.,
 land-use and code measures) other strategies involve place-based investments (i.e., resilience
 hubs, emergency water supply, cooling and smoke refuge centers, often paired with renewable
 energy and storage for backup power). Reviewed resilience plans also emphasize implementing
 green infrastructure, stormwater and floodplain management, and upgrading critical facilities,
 and coupling these actions with public education, cross-jurisdiction collaboration, and a
 deliberate focus on vulnerable populations.

 Overall, local resilience plans have limited engagement with the electricity system. They do not
 characterize grid resilience, nor do they characterize the community’s vulnerability to grid
 outages. In Lane County’s NHMP, outages are considered a consequence of other hazards,
 such as winter storms.

 9.3 Gaps and Opportunities in Community Energy and
 Resilience Planning Approaches Applied in Lane County
 Lane County has strong county-level frameworks for energy and resilience planning, and
 several cities have their own plans. However, many cities still lack stand-alone energy and
 resilience plans and instead rely on energy and resilience goals and strategies discussed in
 comprehensive plans, EOP, and NHMP documents.

 The four largest cities by population (Eugene, Springfield, Cottage Grove, and Florence) have
 invested in local resilience planning. This pattern is common in literature; larger cities, which
 often have more resources and exposure to a wider array of hazards, are more engaged in
 resilience planning. 1

 Dedicated community energy plans provide a coordinated roadmap that aligns land use,
 buildings, and transportation with energy planning strategies (e.g., efficiency improvements,
 electrification, renewables adoption, grid needs). Similarly, stand-alone resilience plans
 complement EOPs and NHMPs by coordinating whole-community actions across prevention,
 protection, mitigation, response, and recovery steps, particularly for cross-sector priorities that
 are not typically addressed in depth in hazard-focused planning.

 There is an opportunity for more individual communities in Lane County to develop stand-alone
 energy and resilience plans. They might considering implementing specific renewable energy
 guidelines beyond the general encouragement for renewables that they now offer.
 Opportunities for grants related to energy and resilience planning may arise, such as the
 resilience hub development effort. 2 Looking to the Oregon Energy Strategies discussed in
 Section 2.5, cities might find states support for actions like energy workforce development,
 collaborative electric vehicle infrastructure planning, and utility-government
 collaboration on system hardening, and sectionalizing and islanding opportunities. As they
 develop their own plans, communities can also adopt their own effective energy and resilience
 strategies already implemented by peer jurisdictions across Lane County.

 1 J. M. Keenan, “Types and forms of resilience in local planning in the U.S.: Who does what?,” Environ.

   Sci. Policy, vol. 88, pp. 116–123, Oct. 2018, doi: 10.1016/j.envsci.2018.06.015.
 2 Oregon Department of Human Services, “Resilience Hubs and Networks Grant,”

 https://www.oregon.gov/odhs/emergency-management/pages/resilience-grants.aspx

 Opportunities for Improving Lane
 County Electric System Affordability,
 Reliability, and Resilience
 Ali Trueworthy
 The purpose of this document is two-fold. First, it serves to help leaders, community decision
 makers, and the interested residents of Lane County establish a baseline understanding of their
 electric system. Second, it identifies some existing gaps and opportunities for action to improve
 the affordability, reliability, and resilience of the county.

 Throughout this document, we present best practices for utilities and communities aiming to
 improve their electricity systems. We identify opportunities for improvement by comparing
 existing known practices to best practices. In this section, we summarize the opportunities that
 are presented throughout this Handbook. In total, we outline 24 opportunities across four
 categories:
 Utility Studies and Programs includes opportunities that can be undertaken by the utilities
 (with or without support) to create results that are useful for the utilities and their customers.
 Collaborative Evaluation, Planning, and Framework Development includes opportunities
 that necessitate and take advantage of the benefits of collaborative approaches. Each
 opportunity involves establishing plans or processes for sustained collaboration.
 Public Accessibility, Education, and Outreach includes opportunities focused on public
 participation and/or data collection to benefit the public.
 Countywide Modeling includes opportunities that can be implemented relatively uniformly
 across the county and do not require specific utility data or action. These opportunities
 generally involve the creation of information that can be used or implemented by multiple
 parties across the county.

 The categories are used to organize themes, not to rank opportunities. Opportunities are not
 prioritized within or across categories. The opportunities that we present can be used to pursue
 future actions, grants, or technical support. As part of the Power Lane County project, staff from
 Lane County, the Center for Rural Livelihoods, and three U.S. Department of Energy national
 laboratories will engage utilities and other stakeholders to further develop a topic for the
 remaining two years of the project, including selecting which of these opportunities will be
 advanced with the project scope, drawing from this initial set of opportunities. Between one and
 three of the outlined opportunities will be able to be part of Power Lane County. None-the-less,
 we provide the broader list to inform future project pursuits.

 Funding for future projects might come from within the community, state grants, federal grants,
 or technical assistance programs. Table 61 lists some common funding sources.

 1) Rate Design Studies

 Rate design studies can explore ways for utilities to pursue some or all their goals through
 thoughtful rate-setting. Goals might include energy conservation, reduced peak energy use,
 fairness to customers, or increased revenue for capital projects. In this document, we introduce
 rate structures in Chapter 1 and discuss a variety of rate options throughout Chapter 4. Rate
 design studies allow for utilities to estimate future costs and revenues associated with changes
 to their rate schedules

 2) Sectionalizing, Islanding, and Blackstart Studies

 Sectionalizing, Islanding, and Blackstart studies would include the identification of areas of the
 grid that would benefit from the capability to isolate from the rest of the grid. This sort of isolation
 can be desirable in several circumstances, such as if one section of the grid poses a high
 wildfire risk and would be beneficial to de-energize without de-energizing other parts of the grid
 (sectionalizing). Alternatively, the ability to keep a portion of the grid energized if the wider grid
 faces outages or shortfalls (islanding) can be important for maintaining electricity service to
 critical infrastructure. If the broader grid goes down, blackstart capabilities would enable utilities
 to use the existing local generation to restart an isolated portion of the grid, such as an area
 connecting an existing generation source to nearby critical facilities. We discuss technologies
 and practices related to this opportunity in Sections 4.4, 5.2, and 8.3, and Chapter 6.

 3) Collaborative Peak Management Programs

 Collaborative peak management programs could enable multiple utilities to establish programs
 while sharing the costs of staff training, customer outreach, and other expenses associated with
 initiating and running the program. These programs are discussed in Section 4.3.

 4) Demand Response Operational Training

 Demand response operational training for utilities can help minimize the costs of implementing
 demand response or other energy efficiency or conservation programs. For customers, training
 can help them participate in programs and adopt the associated technologies. Sharing the costs
 and effort of such trainings between utilities can save time and money. We discuss demand
 response further in Section 4.3.

 5) Enhance Situational Awareness

 Situational awareness includes real-time monitoring of weather and system conditions.
 Improving situational awareness can help utilities make more informed decisions, and, in some
 cases, automate decisions. Numerous modern technologies can provide these capabilities (see
   Section 6.2 and 6.3).
