 6) Utility Evaluation and Improvement Plans

 Evaluating utilities based on their adoption of best practices for distribution planning and/ or risk
 management can facilitate improvements to plans and practices. For distribution planning,
 evaluation might include how well utility planning aligns with policy timeframes, how utilities
 perform planning and scenario development, how well utility data and technology are integrated,
 and how utilities adopt metrics (see Section 4.2). For risk management, evaluation might further
 explore how the best practices outlined throughout Chapter 5 are implemented. Utilities might
 undertake the evaluation and improvement planning themselves or reach out to the third party
 of experts.

 7) Critical Customer Tracking and Load Shedding

 Updating critical customer tracking and load shedding plans annually and sharing them between
 utilities and the county would help keep both parties informed about vulnerable populations in
 the county and how those populations will be served. Utilities and/or external assessors could
 explore options for integration of load shedding software tools. The county could integrate
 utilities’ load shedding plans in emergency response drills and emergency plans. We discuss
 load shedding in Section 3.2 and Critical Facilities in Section 8.3.

 8) Consequence Analysis Framework

 Consequence analysis is the analysis of the potential harms of a utility-caused wildfire ignition
 (or other hazards). While there is no standard workflow for performing consequence analysis,
 and quantifying potential impacts can be challenging, consequence analysis could help utilities
 justify investments like grid hardening. We discuss consequence analysis in Section 5.2.

 9) Collaborative Wildfire Mitigation Plan Innovation

 Collaborative Wildfire Mitigation Plan (WMP) innovation between utilities in the county can
 improve the wildfire mitigation planning process. Because Lane County’s COUs have similar
 gaps in their WMPs, a collaborative process is likely to have wide benefit and potential cost
 savings. Steps to such a process could include: WMP evaluation, WMP template development,
 WMP integration plans, supply chain management database development and benchmarking,
 weather monitoring, after-action reporting standardization, training and tabletop exercises, and
 customer engagement. We discuss wildfire mitigation planning in Section 5.2.

 10) Cost Sharing Arrangements for Risk Mitigation

 Cost sharing arrangements could be explored by interested utilities and local governments for
 wildfire mitigation activities. We discuss wildfire mitigation activities in Section 5.2. This scope
 option would focus on evaluating practical and equitable approaches for utilities and local
 governments to share the costs of risk mitigation activities, including identifying eligible
 activities, potential funding mechanisms, and basic implementation considerations.

 11) Collaborative Grid Planning Process Development

 Collaborative grid planning process development includes establishing a process by which
 utilities could work with cities, the county, developers, and other stakeholders for distribution
 grid planning. This could foster collaborations between utilities and city planners to keep well-

 260

 informed of potential developments or other step changes to load within a city. A process could
 also support utilities and electric boards in adopting planning metrics that are important to their
 customers and stakeholders, such as hosting capacity, cost of service and avoided costs,
 resiliency, equity, energy efficiency, and/or environmental impacts. We discuss this type of
 distribution planning in Section 4.2.

 12) Local Energy Resilience Planning

 Most cities in Lane County do not have plans related to their energy systems. A more directed
 and sustained planning effort in these cities and communities could clarify local goals, priorities,
 and feasible actions related to energy and electricity. We discuss energy and resilience planning
 in Chapter 9.

 13) Electric Vehicle Infrastructure Planning

 City or county level electric vehicle infrastructure planning could help prepare Lane County for
 increases in electric vehicle adoption. Furthermore, infrastructure planning in collaboration with
 utilities could help to minimize the number of grid upgrades needed to serve electric vehicles
 and to allow the utilities to harness opportunities to integrate electric vehicle infrastructure as a
 distributed energy resource for demand management. Electric vehicle planning aligns closely
 with Oregon’s State Energy Strategy, discussed in Chapter 2.

 14) Energy Workforce Development

 New energy technologies and Oregon’s Energy Strategy require a skilled energy workforce.
 Strengthening that workforce in Lane County could expand access to well-paying local jobs.
 Oregon’s Clean Energy Workforce Coalition, launched in 2022, aims to help build the local
 energy workforce and could be a key partner. Initial efforts in Lane County should engage the
 Coalition and other stakeholders to identify local needs and opportunities. See Chapter 2 for
 further discussion.

 15) Increased Access to Utility Planning Documents

 While researching this Handbook, we found that some utility documents were much easier to
 access than others. Cities, the county, utility boards, and customers can encourage utilities to
 make their planning documents more accessible to the public so customers and other
 stakeholders can stay informed and have meaningful opportunities to participate. Accessibility is
 foundational to transparency and discussed in Section 4.1.

 16) Public Demand Response Interest Survey

 For the most part, Lane County utilities do not implement demand response programs. This is
 because pf the limited potential financial benefits, uncertain levels of customer interest, and
 necessary technological investment. As electricity costs change, demand response programs
 might become more appealing for utilities. Surveying interest in demand response among
 residential, commercial, and industrial customers could help utilities estimate likely participation
 and plan accordingly. Surveys can also educate the public of the potential benefits and
 limitations of such programs. Further discussion of demand response is in Section 4.3.

 17) Customer Willingness to Pay Assessment

 Investments in grid resilience can be costly. The costs are paid up front, and the benefits might
 be far off in the future. Fundamentally, many resilience efforts are about preparing for disaster
 events that could happen tomorrow—or 50 years from now. Utilities must make decisions about
 what events they prepare for, and how much money they spend on those preparations. For
 COUs especially, surveying their customers’ willingness to pay for resilience investments can
 help utilities understand how their customers weigh risks relative to future benefits. Pursuing this
 opportunity could help utilities predict what kind of rate increases might be acceptable to
 customers in exchange for a more resilient system. Willingness to pay is further discussed in
 Section 4.4.

 18) Specific Renewable Energy Guidelines/ Goals

 While many cities in Lane County have general goals to boost renewable energy, few have
 tangible guidelines or actionable goals. If renewable energy remains a priority for cities, they
 might consider developing more specific guidelines and goals. City implementation of energy
 planning is further discussed in Chapter 9.

 19) Countywide Load Forecasting

 Electricity use in Lane County has remained relatively stable over the past few decades.
 However, with trends in electrification and city and county development goals, changes may be
 on the horizon. Forecasting future electric loads can help utilities prepare. See Section 4.2 for
 more information on load forecasting.

 20) Peak Load Scenarios Investigations

 Investigating future peak load scenarios can give utilities similar insights to those gleaned from
 load forecasting, but with simpler models. Estimating future peak loads involves building an
 understanding of where, and under what circumstances (e.g. weather, customer behavior)
 electricity demand will be highest. And in those circumstances, how high utilities should expect
 loads to be. Having a better idea of this information can help utilities prepare to serve changing
 loads. See Section 4.2 for more on peak loads.

 21) Countywide Energy Efficiency and Conservation Scenarios

 Energy efficiency and conservation measures can help to offset increasing loads from
 electrification or development. A study investigating different energy efficiency and conservation
 scenarios can help cities, the county, and utilities understand the capacity for such an offset and
 further inform the need for infrastructure investments. See Section 4.3 for more information.

 22) Valuing Resilience

 Resilience measures come at clear costs, but their benefits are often harder to quantify because
 they can be indirect, in the future, and somewhat subjective. Developing methods to value the
 benefits of resilience measures in Lane County would help local governments and utilities
 evaluate options systematically and communicate the tradeoffs of resilience measures clearly.
 These methods should reflect the unique needs and preferences of Lane County utility
 customers. See Section 4.4 for more details.

 23) Wildfire Risk Assessment

 Lane County’s wildfire risk models are out of date and not specific to utility assets. Improving
 estimates of wildfire probability and intensity across the landscape and translating those results
 into a risk metric that explicitly accounts for utility infrastructure would enable utilities to base
 wildfire mitigation plans on more accurate, asset-specific risk assessments. See Section 5.2 for
 more details.

 24) Wildfire Spread Models

 Wildfire spread models are models that utilities can use in real-time during a fire to predict how
 the fire will spread. It can inform their decisions to send out crews, de-energize lines, or take
 other mitigative measures. See Section 5.2 for more details.

 25) Geospatial Data Tracking

 Chapter 8 includes a small subset of geospatial data that could inform future decisions about
 where to dedicate electricity system improvement efforts. Continuing to collect such data,
 expanding the type of data collected, and developing prioritization methods can further support
 decision making.
